CHAPTER 2
Setting Up Your Device
Introduction
In this chapter, we will discuss the essential steps of installing and
configuring Kali NetHunter, a powerful platform designed for mobile
penetration testing. Various device types, such as rooted and unrooted
Android devices, will be discussed, ensuring a broad reach of practical
knowledge. For iOS enthusiasts, jailbreaking devices will also be explored,
equipping readers with the skills to effectively prepare machines for security
testing.
As we navigate this chapter, remember that each device is like an instrument
in a symphony, ready to be tuned to play its part. We will help you attune
your device to the harmonious symphony of mobile penetration testing,
whether an Android or an iOS device, rooted or unrooted, physical or
emulated.
Structure
The following topics will be discussed in the chapter:
Determining which device you need
Rooting android devices
Configuring Kali NetHunter for Android devices

--- Page 72 ---
Kali NetHunter for unrooted Android devices
Jailbreaking iOS devices
Virtual and emulated devices
Objectives
In this chapter, our primary goal is to guide you through the process of
installing and configuring Kali NetHunter across various device types. The
chapter is designed to equip you with the necessary skills and knowledge to
effectively prepare and utilize these devices for mobile penetration testing.
We will explore different device requirements, rooting techniques for
Android, configuring Kali NetHunter for optimal performance, and the
nuances of dealing with virtual and emulated devices. By the end of this
chapter, you will have a deep understanding of how to transform your device
into a potent tool in the orchestra of mobile penetration testing.
Determining which device you need
At the core of mobile penetration testing is clearly understanding the
landscape you are traversing. By selecting a device well-suited for your
testing goals, you empower yourself to explore this landscape in its full
complexity and richness. For example, specifbootloader. might offer more
freedom in regard to system modifications, allowing you to delve deeper into
its security aspects. Conversely, others may provide more robust defenses,
challenging you to find and exploit potential vulnerabilities.
Deciding which device you need when installing and configuring Kali
NetHunter is essential, so let us discuss it in more depth.
Importance of device selection
Ensuring that you use a suitable device can expand your testing capabilities
and dramatically enhance your learning process. By grappling with various
security aspects, identifying potential vulnerabilities, and developing
strategies to counteract them, you learn by doing a vital process that
encourages a robust understanding of mobile security.

--- Page 73 ---
The device selection process for mobile penetration testing mirrors a journey
of discovery and mastery. Like a blacksmith chooses their metal carefully,
you too, must select your device with an understanding of its capabilities and
limitations. This choice will significantly influence the scope of your testing
capabilities, the depth of your exploration, and your proficiency in mobile
penetration testing. Just as the suitable metal brings the blacksmith's
masterpiece to life, the right device will also pave the way for your success
as a mobile penetration tester.
Criteria for choosing the right device
Choosing the right device should consider three primary factors:
compatibility, processing power, and flexibility. A compatible device ensures
that your tools and systems will function properly, while adequate
processing power is crucial for running penetration testing tools efficiently.
Flexibility refers to how much control and freedom you have over the
device's systems and settings; the more, the better.
Firstly, let us delve into compatibility. Compatibility, in this context, refers to
the device's ability to run the necessary software and tools for penetration
testing. Imagine trying to plant a desert cactus in the heart of a rainforest. No
matter how much care you give it, the cactus will not thrive because it is
incompatible with the rainforest environment. Similarly, a device
incompatible with your chosen software tools will only hinder your progress.
For instance, if you plan to use Kali NetHunter for your penetration testing
needs, selecting a device that supports it is crucial.
Additionally, it is essential to consider the hardware compatibility with the
type of testing you wish to conduct. If the built-in wireless card does not
support monitor mode or packet injection, it could be challenging to carry
out some tests. Consulting tool documentation and community forums can
be valuable resources to help determine device compatibility.
Next, we come to processing power, the device's strength, and stamina.
Adequate processing power is essential for efficiently running demanding
penetration testing tools and tasks. Some tools can be resource-intensive,
requiring substantial processing power to run smoothly. In this regard, a
device with a powerful CPU, sufficient RAM, and ample storage space will
ensure your tools run smoothly and data can be stored and processed without

--- Page 74 ---
a hitch. If you plan to do on-device password cracking, you must have the
computing power to process the password hashes and the storage capacity to
hold word lists.
Lastly, we have flexibility, referring to your control and freedom over the
device's systems and settings. Imagine if a botanist could control every
aspect of their garden's environment, the sunlight, the rainfall, and the soil's
nutrient content. That is the kind of control you would want over your
device. A flexible device allows you to modify the system, alter settings, and
tweak parameters to suit your testing needs. This is particularly crucial in
scenarios that require system-level access or the ability to modify system
files, such as rooting Android devices or jailbreaking iOS devices. Although
hacking capability can be severely limiting, devices that have not been
rooted or jailbroken can also be used for testing.
Choosing the right device for mobile penetration testing involves careful
consideration of compatibility, processing power, and flexibility. By bearing
these factors in mind and carefully assessing potential devices against these
criteria, you can ensure that you select a device that not only meets your
current needs but also supports your growth and development as a
penetration tester.
Specifics of device compatibility
Device compatibility hinges on the software and hardware capabilities of the
device in question. For Android, this would entail checking if the device can
be rooted, its compatibility with the desired version of Android, and support
for Kali NetHunter. In the case of iOS, compatibility might depend on
whether the device's iOS version can be jailbroken and its support for tools
such as unc0ver or Chimera.
In the world of Android, compatibility assessment involves several key
factors. First and foremost is the device's rootability, the ability to root the
device. Rooting is a process that allows users of Android smartphones,
tablets, and other devices to attain privileged control over the Android
system. It opens many options for deeper system access and manipulation,
which is crucial for penetration testing activities.

--- Page 75 ---
Additionally, you need to consider the device's compatibility with the
desired version of Android. Some penetration testing tools might only
support specific Android versions, or a particular version may have a
vulnerability you wish to study. A given device usually has a suggested
Android version that should be used as a base when installing NetHunter.
Support for Kali NetHunter is another critical factor for Android devices.
Kali NetHunter is an Android ROM overlay that turns an ordinary phone
into the ultimate mobile penetration testing platform. Not all devices are
compatible with Kali NetHunter, so checking the official Kali NetHunter
documentation for supported devices is an important step.

Rooting android devices
Rooting, at its core, is the process of gaining privileged control, known as
root access, over the Android subsystems. In an unrooted device, the user
operates like a city resident, able to interact with these departments to a
degree but unable to make sweeping changes. Rooting your device elevates
you to the position of mayor, giving you the authority to delve into these
departments and make alterations as you see fit.
Rooting allows you to overcome the limitations carriers and manufacturers
place on some devices. For example, some apps you want to use might
require root access; you cannot use them without rooting. Certain tweaks and
modifications that can optimize your device's performance or personalize
your experience also need root access. Rooting lets you control your device,
customize it to your liking, and improve it.
Before proceeding, it is essential to note that rooting your device can void its
warranty, potentially brick the device if not done correctly and make it
vulnerable to security risks. Please proceed cautiously and ensure you are
comfortable with the potential consequences.
The steps for rooting an Android device are as follows:
Step 1: Backup your device
Before making significant changes to your device, it is always a good idea to
back up your data. If anything goes wrong, you will be able to restore your
data. If you have obtained a dedicated testing device and have not used it for
anything significant, feel free to skip this step.
You can use the built-in Google Backup service, available on most Android
devices, to back up your apps and settings. If you want a more
comprehensive backup (including SMS messages), use a third-party app like
Helium.
Step 2: Enable developer mode
To access USB debugging functionality on your device, you must first
enable developer mode.

--- Page 81 ---
Go to the settings app, then click on about phone. From there, locate
software info, and find the build number. Once you find the build number,
tap it seven times. You will see a notification that you have enabled
developer mode.
Step 3: Enable USB debugging
Enabling USB debugging allows us to use the ADB tool, an essential part of
rooting.
Go to the settings app, scroll down, and tap developer options. From there,
scroll down and tap the button to enable USB debugging. You will receive a
prompt that asks if you want to allow USB debugging. Tap OK.
Step 4: Plug into the computer
At this point, you should connect your device to a computer.
Once you connect your device to a computer with a USB cable, you will
receive a prompt notifying you that your computer is trying to connect to the
device. Tap the box beside always allow from this computer, then tap OK.
Step 5: Unlock the bootloader
The bootloader is a program that the device uses to boot up. By default, it
only allows booting with an OS it approves of. To root the device, we need
to change this.
The process varies depending on your device's manufacturer. The official
Android documentation regarding locking and unlocking the bootloader can
be found here:
https://source.android.com/docs/core/architecture/bootloader/locking_u
nlocking
You must use a tool like Fastboot from the Android SDK Platform for many
devices. The link to the tools package is as follows:
https://developer.android.com/tools/releases/platform-tools
As an example, it is possible to use the ADB tool in your command-line
interface to accomplish this. If the Android device is connected to your

--- Page 82 ---
computer, running the following command will reboot the device into the
bootloader menu:
adb reboot bootloader
Once the device has rebooted into the bootloader menu, the following
command will unlock the bootloader, allowing us to continue:
fastboot oem unlock
Refer to Figure 2.1:
Figure 2.1: An example of a successful bootloader unlocking procedure
At this point, you should follow the prompts shown on your device to
complete the unlocking procedure. It varies for every device, so it is
impossible to provide exact guidance here.
Step 6: Install a custom recovery
A custom recovery program can install updates and backups on your device.
It gives you access to boot sectors of your device that would otherwise be
inaccessible. We will need this to root your device.
The most popular custom recovery is TWRP. You can install it using
Fastboot, just like with the bootloader. Download the latest version from
here:
https://twrp.me/

--- Page 83 ---
Be sure to download the image for your device.
In your command-line interface, browse to the location of the twrp.img file
that you downloaded. Be sure that your device is on the bootloader screen.
From there, use the following two commands to install twrp as a custom
recovery:
fastboot flash recovery twrp.img
fastboot reboot
Refer to Figure 2.2:
Figure 2.2: Successfully flashing twrp into the recovery partition and rebooting the device with
fastboot
Step 7: Obtain the appropriate rooting software
Many tools are available for rooting, so you should choose which tool is best
for you depending on your Android device and version. Some popular
options include Magisk, KingoRoot, and One Click Root.
For most rooting tools, you will download a ZIP file that can be installed
through your custom recovery.
Download the latest version of Magisk here:
https://github.com/topjohnwu/Magisk/releases/tag/v26.1
Step 8: Root your device
With your bootloader unlocked, custom recovery installed, and rooting
software obtained, you are ready to root your device.

--- Page 84 ---
The process varies depending on the rooting software you are using. After
you have rebooted your phone into TWRP custom recovery, copy the
Magisk file to your device using the command-line interface, as per the
following:
Adb push Magisk-v20.4.zip /
Now that the Magisk file is copied to the device tap on the install menu in
TWRP. Then locate and tap the newly copied Magisk file. Tap install and
follow the prompts on-screen.
Step 9: Verify your root
After rooting your device, you will want to verify that the process worked
and your device is rooted.
You can use a root-checking app like Root Checker. Simply download the
app from the Google Play store and run it. It will tell you whether your
device has root access.
The process above may seem complicated, but each step is crucial to
ensuring a safe and successful root. If you follow the instructions carefully
and understand what you do at each step, you can root your device
successfully.
Note that each device might require slightly different procedures for
unlocking the bootloader, installing a custom recovery, etc. It is always best
to follow a guide specifically designed for your device. Sites like the XDA
Developers Forum (https://xdaforums.com/) can be an excellent resource
for this.
Tools for rooting
Navigating the path to rooting an Android device can be likened to an
intrepid explorer journeying through unfamiliar lands, and Team Win
Recovery Project (TWRP) and Magisk are two of the most reliable tools in
your adventure kit. They are significant and pivotal to the rooting process,
serving as your compass and map, guiding you through the complex terrain
of the Android operating system.

--- Page 85 ---
Think of TWRP as a comprehensive, high-end toolbox replacing the basic
set of tools that came with your car. The stock recovery on Android devices
is a limited toolset, enabling only basic user functionalities such as applying
updates or performing factory resets. TWRP, on the other hand, is a custom
recovery that offers far more advanced capabilities. The transition from
stock recovery to TWRP broadens your range of actions, opening doors to
functionalities that were previously out of reach.
One of the key features of TWRP is the ability to install custom firmware,
also known as Read-Only Memory images (ROMs), which are essentially
modified versions of Android. Imagine having the option to replace the
entire layout of your city, customizing it to meet the specific needs of its
citizens. That is the kind of flexibility TWRP provides. Furthermore, it
allows the execution of advanced recovery features, like making full system
backups, that serve as a safety net in case things go south during the rooting
process.
Now, let us introduce Magisk. If TWRP is the toolbox, Magisk is the master
key that unlocks the city gates. It is the tool that performs the actual rooting.
Magisk is a systemless rooting method, meaning it does not alter the system
partition of your device. In the ever-evolving cat-and-mouse game between
device rooting and security updates, Magisk provides a significant advantage
by allowing seamless installation of system updates even on rooted devices.
At the heart of Magisk is its capability to provide root access to the device.
However, this is not just any root access; Magisk operates systemless. The
term systemless means that Magisk does not directly modify the system
partition of your device. Instead, it modifies the boot partition and leaves the
system files untouched. This systemless method has the remarkable benefit
of allowing the installation of system updates without losing root access, a
hurdle that often challenges traditional rooting methods.
An equally compelling facet of Magisk is its ability to perform systemless
modifications that bypass SafetyNet. Google's security API performs device
integrity checks, verifying if the device has been tampered with or is running
on an emulator. Many apps, including banking and streaming services,
refuse to run on devices that fail this check. Here, Magisk shines with its
Magisk Hide feature. It can make rooted devices appear unrooted to certain

--- Page 86 ---
apps, thereby fooling SafetyNet and maintaining the functionality of these
sensitive apps on rooted devices.
Bootloader unlocking and custom recovery installation
Unlocking the bootloader is like opening a door and completely controlling
your device. It is the first step in rooting, allowing you to install custom
recoveries like TWRP and root access tools like Magisk. Custom recovery,
meanwhile, enables you to install, backup, restore, and wipe the device's
software, as well as provide a host of other features that the stock recovery
lacks.
Delving into device rooting, one encounters terms like Bootloader
Unlocking and Custom Recovery Installation that often elicit queries. Why
are they crucial? What roles do they play in the rooting process? To find
answers, we return to our city metaphor, where unlocking the bootloader
resembles opening a massive gate guarding the city and installing a custom
recovery is akin to installing a powerful city administration system.
Unlocking the bootloader is the first key step toward gaining complete
control over your Android device. In layperson's terms, a bootloader is a
program that decides what applications will run in your device's startup
process. Manufacturers often lock the bootloader to ensure the device only
runs its authorized operating system. It acts like a massive gate that prevents
users from making significant changes to the device. However, by unlocking
this gate, you can make broader modifications to your device, including
installing a custom recovery and rooting your device.
Upon unlocking the bootloader, the next progression in the rooting journey
is the installation of a custom recovery. The stock recovery that comes with
your device is quite basic, providing limited functionality such as
performing system updates or factory resets. Installing a custom recovery
like TWRP, on the other hand, is like replacing a rudimentary city
administration with a state-of-the-art system.
This sophisticated system (the custom recovery) brings various features to
your fingertips. It enables the installation of custom ROMs, modified
versions of Android fine-tuned for performance or added features. Imagine
being able to redesign your city's infrastructure for optimal functionality;

--- Page 87 ---
that is the freedom a custom recovery provides. It also offers advanced
backup and restore options, allowing you to create a complete image of your
device's current state that you can revert to if necessary. This is akin to
having a comprehensive contingency plan for your city. A custom recovery
lets you wipe various aspects of your device's software, giving you granular
control over its functioning.
In essence, unlocking the bootloader and installing a custom recovery are
pivotal steps toward achieving total control of your Android device. Like
opening the city gate and setting up a high-tech city administration, these
actions enable broader system modifications, comprehensive backup and
restoration options, and more nuanced control over your device. They form
the foundation of the rooting process, paving the way for tools like TWRP
and Magisk to perform their magic.
These features are significant to our context, as most severe hacking and
penetration testing requires accessing non-standard functionality. The
guardrails put in place by the manufacturers to protect the average user
hinder security testing. Rather than using the devices for common purposes,
we intend to emulate malicious attackers and squeeze every power drop out
of our devices.

Figure 2.3: Unlocking the bootloader is an essential step to install NetHunter

Configuring Kali NetHunter for Android devices
Kali NetHunter, developed by the team behind Kali Linux, is a free and
open-source mobile penetration testing platform for Android devices.
Consider it your portable laboratory, packed with a suite of tools that allow
you to carry out a wide array of penetration tests. The diversity and depth of
tools in Kali NetHunter equip you with a comprehensive toolset to assess
and exploit vulnerabilities.
Let us discuss the process of configuring Kali NetHunter for Android
devices in more depth.
The importance of Kali NetHunter in mobile penetration testing
The appeal of Kali NetHunter is deeply rooted in its diversity and depth of
tools. This platform has many security and penetration testing tools, each
designed for specific types of assessments and exploits. It is akin to having a
toolbox where each tool is unique, serving a distinct purpose yet contributing
to the collective functionality. From wireless injection and a built-in
hardware address (media access control) changer to many reliable exploits
tucked away in its arsenal, Kali NetHunter comes equipped to handle an
extensive range of penetration testing scenarios.
However, Kali NetHunter's prowess goes beyond its toolset. Its compatibility
with a broad spectrum of Android devices makes it a game-changer, coupled
with its seamless integration with Kali Linux. This means that you can
operate Kali NetHunter on your Android smartphone or tablet while
benefiting from the robustness of the Kali Linux environment. It is like
having a miniature, portable version of your lab, ready to use whenever and
wherever you need it.
Installing and configuring Kali NetHunter
Installing Kali NetHunter involves downloading the right image for your
device from the official site, flashing it via TWRP, and setting it up on your
Android device. Configuring it involves fine-tuning the settings for your
specific needs, including selecting the appropriate wireless card for Wi-Fi
penetration testing, setting up Human Interface Device (HID) attacks, and
configuring services such as SSH and VNC for remote access.

--- Page 89 ---
Before starting with Kali NetHunter, you must determine the appropriate
image for your device.
Start your journey by visiting the official Kali NetHunter website:
https://www.kali.org/get-kali/#kali-mobile
Different NetHunter images are designed for various device models and
Android versions here. Select and download the one that matches your
device's specifications.
Once you have the right NetHunter image downloaded, the next step is to
flash or install it on your device. For this, you will need the help of TWRP,
the custom recovery tool we discussed earlier.
First, copy the install image to your device through the command-line
interface:
adb push nethunter-2023.1-flocm-marshmallow-kalifs-full.zip /sdcard
In TWRP, navigate to the option to install a zip file, select the downloaded
NetHunter image, and confirm the installation. It may take around 20
minutes to complete. Once the installation is complete, you will be ready to
start leveraging the power of Kali NetHunter.
After the installation is complete, open the NetHunter app. Grant the
superuser request, then tap allow on every prompt that appears. The app will
restart. Next, browse the Kali Chroot manager section and tap install Kali
Chroot. This process takes some time, so please be patient. A status bar at
the top of the screen indicates if the Chroot is installed, so please wait until it
updates. Once installed, the status bar will change to Kali Chroot has not yet
started. Tap start Kali Chroot at the bottom of the screen. You will receive a
notification that it has successfully started. Your Kali NetHunter is now in a
base state and ready for configuration and use.
Next, we will begin configuring Kali NetHunter, an essential process that
fine-tunes the platform according to your needs. Configuration settings in
Kali NetHunter cover a wide range of parameters. For instance, select the
appropriate wireless card in the settings to perform Wi-Fi penetration testing.
If your work involves HID attacks, you must set up these parameters within
the NetHunter app. Other configurations might include setting up services

--- Page 90 ---
such as SSH and VNC, which allow you to access your device remotely, like
controlling your lab’s operations from afar. Although we will briefly discuss
some of these topics below, we will cover NetHunter in Chapter 7, Working
with Kali NetHunter.
Understanding the functionality of Kali NetHunter
Kali NetHunter integrates an entire Kali Linux system into your Android
device, with toolsets for network analysis, attack execution, and other
functions. It supports Wireless 802.11 frame injection, one-click MANA Evil
Access Point setups, HID keyboard (Teensy-like attacks), and BadUSB
MITM attacks.
Kali NetHunter's functionality extends further with its Wireless 802.11 frame
injection support. This means your Android device, under Kali NetHunter,
can actively interact with wireless networks beyond the limitations of
standard Android network handling capabilities. Think of it as being able to
manipulate the airwaves, injecting and intercepting wireless frames to assess
network vulnerabilities or perform penetration testing tasks.
Another feature that accentuates Kali NetHunter's functionality is providing
one-click MANA Evil Access Point setups. This lets you quickly create
rogue Wi-Fi access points, a critical function in Wi-Fi penetration testing. It
is akin to setting up a decoy strategically, luring in unsuspecting targets to
reveal potential vulnerabilities.
Lastly, we delve into HID keyboard (Teensy-like attacks) and BadUSB
MITM attacks. These advanced penetration testing capabilities transform
your device into a keyboard or USB capable of executing keystroke injection
attacks or intercepting USB communication.
Kali NetHunter for unrooted Android devices
Working with unrooted Android devices for mobile penetration testing is like
navigating a maze with more walls and fewer exits. You can still explore, but
your range is more limited. Unrooted devices restrict access to many
advanced functions necessary for comprehensive penetration testing. Let us
discuss this in more depth.

--- Page 91 ---
Challenges of unrooted Android devices
Many essential penetration testing tools and techniques require root access,
equivalent to holding a master key to your device. Root access grants
permission to modify system settings, alter applications, and tap into the
device's hardware capabilities in ways that are not ordinarily possible.
Without this privilege, the breadth and depth of your penetration testing are
bound to be restrained.
In addition, there is the issue of restricted hardware access on unrooted
devices. Rooting a device provides direct access to its hardware, which is
essential for tasks like packet sniffing and frame injection in Wi-Fi
penetration testing. In an unrooted state, the device's operating system
enforces a layer of abstraction between the user and the hardware. This
protective layer acts as a stern gatekeeper, limiting your interactions with the
device's hardware components.
Yet another challenge is that powerful penetration testing platforms, like
Kali NetHunter, are designed with rooted devices in mind. While they offer
support for unrooted devices, the functionality can be limited and the setup
process more complex. It is like trying to fit a square peg into a round hole;
it might be possible with modifications, but it is not the perfect fit.
Deploying Kali NetHunter in a Chroot environment
You can deploy Kali NetHunter without root access within a chroot
environment. A chroot on Unix operating systems is an operation that
changes the apparent root directory for the current running process and its
children. This creates a sandbox environment separate from the main system,
within which Kali NetHunter can run.
Chroot, shorthand for change root, is an operation that modifies the apparent
root directory for the current running process and its progeny. This new root
becomes the highest point in the directory hierarchy that the process can
access. The effect of this operation is creating an isolated environment
within the broader operating system. In this safe space, you can run
applications without the risk of them interacting negatively with your
primary system. It is like creating a confined playground within your device,

--- Page 92 ---
one where Kali NetHunter can frolic freely, unhampered by the restrictions
of an unrooted device.
However, it is important to note that a chroot environment does not equate to
gaining full root access. Some limitations and restrictions persist. For
instance, certain device hardware functionalities may remain inaccessible,
and some tools may not operate as efficiently as they would on a rooted
device. Using Kali NetHunter and its accompanying suite of tools on an
unrooted device presents an undeniable advantage.
The general process of running NetHunter in a rootless environment is the
same as running any app from a non-standard application repository.
Typically, Android devices get their apps from the Google Play Store, but
Android offers the ability to use alternate sources. Although the process
varies from device to device, there is usually an option to allow apps from
unknown sources within the phone's security settings. Enable this option.
After enabling apps from unknown sources, simply download the Kali
NetHunter App Store from the following:
https://store.nethunter.com
You can think of this as an alternative to the Google Play Store, which
focuses exclusively on penetration testing tools. Although many functions
and apps will be restricted due to the device not being rooted, it offers many
interesting choices.
An official chart showing the differences in functionality between Rootless
and full NetHunter installations can be found here:
https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/#10-nethunter-editions
The most significant features missing in the rootless version are as follows:
Unable to use the NetHunter app
Able to use Metasploit, but without database support
Unable to conduct WiFi injection attacks
Unable to conduct HID attacks

--- Page 93 ---
Jailbreaking iOS devices
Just as Android has rooting, iOS has jailbreaking. Jailbreaking iOS devices
remove the software restrictions Apple imposes on iOS, iPadOS, tvOS, and
watchOS. It provides root access to the iOS file system and manager,
allowing the download of additional applications, extensions, and themes
unavailable through the official Apple App Store. Let us discuss this in
detail.
The process of jailbreaking iOS devices
Imagine the system restrictions imposed by Apple as a tightly monitored
marketplace, where every item, be it an application, an extension, or a
theme, must pass through rigorous checks before it's allowed on the shelves.
While this control ensures the quality and security of the available items, it
also restricts the variety. A jailbroken iOS device will enable you to venture
beyond this market, opening avenues to other third-party applications,
extensions, and themes unavailable on the official Apple App Store.
One crucial detail is that jailbreaking is not a process for the faint-hearted. It
involves profound system modifications and, if done incorrectly, can result
in a non-functioning device or a voided warranty. However, with careful
execution and an understanding of the risks involved, jailbreaking can
empower users with a new level of control over their devices. Unlike
Android devices, which can be unlocked and rooted by design, iOS
jailbreaking involves actively exploiting vulnerabilities in the device to
make it run alternate code. As such, the process is a little bit less stable.
Nevertheless, we will show you the latest and most stable jailbreaking tools
so that the risk involved is minimal.
As we delve deeper into the world of jailbreaking, we will explore the
variety of tools and techniques at our disposal, discuss how to safely perform
a jailbreak, and understand the expansive landscape that opens once your
device is jailbroken.
Refer to the following figure:

--- Page 94 ---
Figure 2.4: checkra1n is a very popular jailbreaking utility for iPhones
Types of jailbreaks
It is important to note that the availability of jailbreak methods may vary
depending on the iOS version and device model. Newer iOS versions often
have more robust security measures, making it more challenging to develop
jailbreaks. Additionally, different types of security measures equate to
different types of jailbreaks.
There are three different types of jailbreaks for iOS, including tethered,
semi-tethered, and untethered. Let us have a look at them:
Tethered jailbreak: In a tethered jailbreak, the device needs to be
connected to a computer every time it is rebooted. This is because the
jailbreak exploit used to gain root access to the device's file system is
not persistent. If the device restarts without being connected to a
computer, it will boot into a non-jailbroken state. To regain the
jailbroken state, the device must be connected to a computer, and a
tethered boot must be performed using special software or tools.

--- Page 95 ---
Semi-tethered jailbreak: A semi-tethered jailbreak offers more
flexibility than a tethered jailbreak. With a semi-tethered jailbreak, the
device will still boot into a usable state even without a computer, but
some functionality, such as jailbreak tweaks and certain apps, will be
disabled. To re-enable full jailbreak functionality, the device must be
connected to a computer, and a semi-tethered boot must be performed
using a specific tool. This jailbreak allows the device to be used even
if a computer is not readily available. Still, some features and
modifications will only be available when booted with the assistance
of a computer.
Untethered jailbreak: An untethered jailbreak is the most desirable
type because it provides complete and persistent jailbreak
functionality without needing a computer. Once the device is
jailbroken using an untethered exploit, it will remain in a jailbroken
state, even after restarting it. The user can freely reboot the device,
and all jailbreak features and tweaks will continue to work without
requiring additional steps.
Jailbreaking tools
Several tools are available for jailbreaking iOS devices, each catering to
different iOS versions and device models. Both palera1n and checkra1n are
based on the checkm8 bootrom exploit, supporting a wider range of devices,
including (as of May 2023) the latest version of iOS. unc0ver is a semi-
untethered jailbreak that works primarily on iOS 14 or older. Let us take a
more detailed look at each of them:
unc0ver: unc0ver is a popular jailbreak tool that uses different
exploits depending on the iOS version and device model. It is
developed by the unc0ver team and focuses on providing a user-
friendly experience. unc0ver typically employs software-based
exploits to gain root access to the device. It supports a broader range
of devices, including some newer models, but its compatibility may
vary depending on the specific iOS version and device. unc0ver is
currently supported for devices running iOS 14.8 and below. More
info can be found here:

--- Page 96 ---
https://unc0ver.dev/
checkra1n: checkra1n is a well-known and widely used jailbreak tool
that utilizes a hardware-based exploit called checkm8. This exploit
targets a vulnerability in the BootROM (read-only memory) of certain
Apple devices with A5 to A11 chips, including iPhone 5s to iPhone X
and some iPads. The checkm8 exploit is a low-level exploit, making it
highly reliable and persistent. This means that checkra1n provides an
unpatchable jailbreak, as Apple cannot fix this vulnerability through
software updates. However, it is limited to a specific range of devices
running iOS 14.8.1 or below. The official documentation can be found
here:
https://checkra.in/
palera1n: palera1n is a relatively new jailbreak tool developed by the
palera1n team. It is based on the checkm8 exploit, like checkra1n.
palera1n aims to provide an alternative and more streamlined
experience for checkm8-based jailbreaking. While checkra1n is a
command-line tool, palera1n offers a Graphical User Interface
(GUI), making it more accessible for users who prefer a visual
interface. The functionality and capabilities of palera1n are like
checkra1n, as they both leverage the checkm8 exploit. It is notable
that palera1n supports the latest version of iOS (16.6) as of May 2023.
It can be downloaded here:
https://github.com/palera1n/palera1n.
These tools, while different in their functionality, share a common goal:
providing you with the means to break free from Apple's restrictions and
begin to explore customization and exploration. However, remember these
are powerful tools that, if used incorrectly, could potentially harm your
device. Therefore, understanding your device's compatibility and the
functionality of each tool is paramount to a successful and safe jailbreaking
experience.
The process of installing a jailbreak usually involves one of two methods:
Browsing to a specific URL on the device, which will allow you to
exploit the software (usually the browser).

--- Page 97 ---
Downloading a toolkit onto your desktop or laptop, then connecting
your iOS device to the computer and running through the install
process. This usually involves putting the iOS device into DFU mode
and then running the jailbreak software.
Due to the constantly changing nature of jailbreaking and iOS software
updates, it is essential to follow the instructions given by the particular
jailbreak that you wish to use. To that end, we cannot safely provide detailed
instructions here.
Gaining root access and installing alternative app stores
After jailbreaking, you gain root access, opening a new realm of possibilities
for customization and control. Root access provides unparalleled control
over your device, allowing you to customize your iOS device far beyond
what is permitted by the default restrictions. You can modify system files,
change boot logos, apply themes, tweak system functionalities, improve
performance, and much more.
Due to Apple’s wall-garden approach to security and apps, many functions
and apps which would assist in security testing and hacking are not available
in the official App Store and must be installed from an alternative source.
One of the most popular first steps post-jailbreak is to install Cydia, an
alternative app store. Cydia is a repository of many useful tools and utilities
for penetration testing and security analysis, including packet sniffers such
as Pirni and tcpdump, network spoofers like arpspoof, and network analysis
tools like nmap and wireshark. These tools can help you dive deeper into the
realm of mobile security, allowing you to assess and understand the potential
vulnerabilities of your device and other network-connected devices.
You should note, however, that while Cydia opens up a whole new world of
possibilities, it also demands a certain level of responsibility. The freedom to
install and modify software can have its pitfalls if done carelessly. Installing
unreliable apps or making uninformed system modifications could
potentially harm your device or expose you to security risks. Therefore, it is
important to approach this newfound freedom with a degree of caution.
Virtual and emulated devices

--- Page 98 ---
Virtual and emulated devices are an essential tool in the mobile penetration
tester's kit. They allow for a risk-free environment where testers can
experiment, make mistakes, and learn without the risk of damaging a
physical device. For those unable to access certain physical devices, virtual
and emulated devices provide an accessible avenue to continue testing and
learning. While some functions and features like jailbreaking are usually not
possible in emulated environments, they can provide a convenient way to
quickly access a testing environment. As a bonus, it can often be easier to
intercept and modify device traffic through an emulator.
The significance of virtual and emulated devices
These virtual devices have become indispensable assets in the mobile
penetration testing realm. They offer a safe and controlled environment
where you can test, explore, and learn without the fear of damaging a
physical device. Like a scientist in a lab, a penetration tester using virtual
devices can experiment freely, knowing that any negative impacts can be
contained and reversed easily.
Furthermore, these virtual devices offer an excellent solution for
accessibility issues. Not every tester has the resources to acquire a broad
range of physical devices for testing purposes. With emulators and virtual
devices, this is no longer a hurdle. You can emulate various models of
Android and iOS devices, covering different versions of these operating
systems. This allows you to understand how security vulnerabilities and
penetration testing results can vary across different devices and operating
systems, enriching your knowledge and skillset.
Besides, virtual, and emulated devices have practical advantages when it
comes to automation. Automated testing scripts can be easily set up and run
across multiple virtual devices, increasing testing efficiency. They can be
created and destroyed on demand, making them flexible tools for testing.
Android emulators and the Android virtual device manager
Genymotion is a robust and fast Android emulator, known for its seamless
performance and user-friendly interface. Its cross-platform capabilities,
being compatible with Windows, macOS, and Linux, add to its popularity
among penetration testers. One of Genymotion's most notable features is its

--- Page 99 ---
pre-configured Android images. This allows you to emulate a diverse range
of Android devices quickly, with just a few clicks. Whether you want to
simulate a device with a specific screen size, hardware capability, or Android
version, Genymotion provides an easy, streamlined way to do it. This makes
it a versatile tool for penetration testers who need to quickly switch between
different testing environments. One downside is that it is not free, costing
$206/year for the least expensive tier. However, Genymotion does offer a
free 30-day trial. More information can be found here:
https://www.genymotion.com/
On the other hand, the Android Virtual Device (AVD) Manager is an
integral part of Android Studio, the official Integrated Development
Environment (IDE) for Android app development. AVD Manager provides
a more hands-on, customizable approach to creating and managing your
emulated devices. You have control over every aspect of your virtual device,
from the Android version, the screen size and resolution, to the hardware
capabilities such as the amount of RAM and storage. This level of detail
makes AVD Manager a valuable tool for those who need to replicate very
specific device environments. Best of all, it is free. It is included with
Android Studio, which you likely already have installed if you are using the
Android SDK or ADB. It can be downloaded here:
https://developer.android.com/studio
Information about the emulator can be found here:
https://developer.android.com/studio/run/emulator
Both Genymotion and AVD Manager provide an array of options when it
comes to Android versions, screen sizes, and hardware capabilities, giving
you a vast landscape for your testing needs. Whether you are looking for
speed and convenience with Genymotion, or meticulous detail and
customization with AVD Manager, these tools are pivotal in creating a
comprehensive and adaptable testing environment. As with any tool, your
choice will depend on your specific needs and objectives in mobile
penetration testing. The choice primarily comes down to desired options and
budget. Using these emulators, you can explore the nuances of Android
security in an accessible and manageable way.
iOS tools

--- Page 100 ---
When diving into the world of iOS mobile penetration testing, two
prominent tools come to the fore: Corellium and the iOS Simulator with
Xcode. These tools create virtual environments that mimic the functionality
of actual iOS devices, offering a safe and controlled space for exploratory
testing and vulnerability assessment. However, they differ from each other in
scale, budget, and usage.
Corellium is an innovative platform that offers virtual iOS devices in a
cloud-based environment. It is the equivalent of having a digital workshop
filled with every iOS device you could imagine, all housed within your
computer screen. Each virtual device runs the actual iOS firmware, offering
an incredibly accurate representation of the physical device. Corellium's
system goes beyond mere emulation, it virtualizes the entire device, from the
processor down to the memory and I/O system. It offers features like kernel
debugging and customizable device models and versions, which are
tremendously useful for in-depth security analysis. However, Corellium is
generally more tailored toward enterprise users and security researchers due
to its advanced capabilities and cost considerations. Although a basic
Explorer-tier account will cost $3 per device per hour, most of the
functionality is locked away until you reach the professional tier, which
starts at $800/month for two devices. Although a free trial seems possible, it
requires registration and interaction with the Corellium sales team. If you
have the budget, Corellium is an excellent option, but for students and those
interested in learning, there are other choices. More info can be found here:
https://www.corellium.com/
For individuals or hobbyist testers, the iOS Simulator bundled with Xcode
offers a fantastic and more accessible alternative. Xcode is Apple's official
IDE for creating apps for all Apple platforms, and its inbuilt iOS Simulator
is a powerful tool for developers and testers alike. Unlike Corellium, which
runs the real iOS firmware, the iOS Simulator pretends to be an iOS device.
It mimics the user interface and allows you to interact with the device as you
would with a physical one. You can choose from a wide range of iOS
devices and iOS versions, offering a considerable breadth for testing.
Although it may not have the complete range of functionality of a fully
virtualized solution like Corellium, it still provides a controlled and varied
environment that is well-suited for a wide range of testing scenarios. It is
important to note, however, that Xcode is only available on Mac computers.

--- Page 101 ---
In the realm of iOS testing, Corellium and the iOS Simulator each have their
strengths. While Corellium offers a deep, comprehensive testing platform
that closely mirrors actual device behavior, the iOS Simulator provides a
more accessible and user-friendly interface for less intensive testing needs.
Regardless of the tool you choose, both of these platforms offer you the
chance to delve into the intricacies of iOS security within a safe and
controlled environment. Unfortunately for those working within the confines
of a smaller budget, iOS testing requires at least some type of purchase:
Physical iOS device
Corellium subscription
Mac computer to run Xcode and the iOS simulator

In the next chapter, let us take a detailed look at the overall process for
