CHAPTER 3
Mobile Penetration Testing
Methodology
Introduction
This chapter delves into the intricacies of Mobile Penetration Testing
Methodology, a critical aspect of cybersecurity in the age of ubiquitous
mobile devices. We will start by exploring standard penetration testing
processes, including planning, reconnaissance, scanning, gaining and
maintaining access, and analysis and reporting. Then, the focus shifts to the
specialized domain of mobile penetration testing, dissecting its unique
challenges and techniques. This involves a detailed look at both testing
mobile devices and utilizing mobile devices as tools in penetration testing.
From the static and dynamic analysis of mobile apps to the setup and
exploitation techniques using mobile devices, this chapter is designed to
offer a comprehensive understanding of the current landscape and
methodologies in mobile penetration testing.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
Standard penetration testing
Mobile penetration testing

--- Page 105 ---
Conducting penetration testing of mobile devices
Conducting penetration testing with mobile devices
Objectives
The objective of this chapter is to provide a detailed roadmap for
conducting effective mobile penetration testing. We aim to equip readers
with a thorough understanding of the standard methodologies in penetration
testing and how these adapt and expand in the context of mobile devices.
The chapter will guide you through every step of the process, from initial
planning and reconnaissance to the final stages of reporting. It will cover
both the methods for testing mobile devices, including device and
filesystem analysis, and the techniques for using mobile devices as
penetration testing tools. By the end of this chapter, readers should be well-
versed in the strategies and practices essential for proficient mobile
penetration testing.
Standard penetration testing
Penetration testing, also known as pen testing or ethical hacking, evaluates
the security of a computer system, network, or web application to find
vulnerabilities that malicious hackers could exploit. A typical penetration
testing methodology involves several phases involving various techniques,
tools, and practices.
Delving into the world of penetration testing or ethical hacking is akin to
stepping into the shoes of a digital detective. It is about understanding,
evaluating, and fortifying the digital walls that protect our computer
systems, networks, or web applications. These metaphorical walls, the
bedrock of our digital safety, are continuously assailed by hackers with
malicious intent. Hence, it is crucial to regularly probe these walls to find
and rectify vulnerabilities that may serve as potential breach points. This is
precisely where penetration testing comes into play. At its core, penetration
testing is a methodical process comprising several distinct yet
interconnected phases. Let us take a closer look at these stages and decipher
what they entail.

--- Page 106 ---
The first phase, often referred to as planning and reconnaissance, is about
laying the groundwork. This involves understanding the systems to be
tested and outlining the goals of the test. In the reconnaissance part of this
phase, the tester gathers as much information about the system as possible.
This knowledge could span details about IP addresses, domain details, mail
servers, etc., serving as vital clues about potential vulnerabilities. The
OWASP Testing Guide (v4) supplies a robust framework to guide this
process.
Next, we venture into the scanning phase. This stage is all about gaining a
more detailed understanding of the system's architecture and the potential
vulnerabilities therein. The scanning could be static (analyzing the
application's code to understand its potential behavior) or dynamic
(analyzing its code in a running state). Tools such as Nmap, Nuclei, and
Burp Suite Pro can be instrumental here.
We then segue into the gaining access phase. Here, the tester tries to exploit
the vulnerabilities discovered so far to enter the system. This could involve
techniques such as SQL injections, cross-site scripting, or backdoors. It is
important to note that the aim here is not to cause damage but to
demonstrate how a malicious hacker might breach the system. Tools like
Metasploit or Burp Suite Pro often take center stage here.
The next phase is maintaining access, where the ethical hacker tries to
remain within the system undetected for a length of time. This supplies a
realistic picture of the potential damage a real hacker could cause. It also
tests how long it takes for system defenses to detect and respond to the
intrusion. To accomplish this, command and control (C2) frameworks are
often used. Commonly used C2 frameworks include Cobalt Strike, Havok,
and Sliver.
Finally, we reach the analysis and reporting phase. This crucial step
involves compiling a detailed report outlining the vulnerabilities
discovered, accessed data, and the time the tester remained undetected. This
report should also provide recommendations for mitigating the discovered
vulnerabilities. One essential thing to remember is that, as a penetration
tester, you are hired to draft the report, not to create an awesome hack.

--- Page 107 ---
With a brief overview of standard penetration testing under our belt, let us
dive more into depth with each of the phases.
Planning and reconnaissance
Before a penetration test begins, the objectives and scope of the test must be
defined. This could involve defining what systems are to be tested, what
tools will be used, and what methods will be followed. Following this,
reconnaissance or information gathering is conducted to gather as much
data as possible about the target. This could involve network scanning,
domain name research, and social engineering tactics. Tools often used in
this phase include Nmap, Wireshark, Open-Source Intelligence (OSINT)
frameworks like Maltego and Google Dorks.
During the planning phase, we establish clear objectives and define the
scope of our expedition. It is crucial to outline which systems will be tested,
the tools we will use, and the methods to follow. This stage involves an
extensive dialogue between the penetration tester and the client. Agreement
on boundaries (such as testing times, IP ranges, types of attack, etc.) helps
avoid unnecessary disruptions while ensuring that the test focuses on the
areas of greatest concern to the client.
It is of the utmost importance to emphasize that the only difference between
a penetration tester and a black hat (criminal) hacker is the statement of
work with a defined scope. Any testing done outside of the defined scope is
technically a major crime. Additionally, it will also erode the confidence of
clients and lead to less future work, at the very least.
The reconnaissance phase, often referred to as information gathering,
follows the planning stage. Imagine this phase as a thorough exploration of
the landscape before the actual expedition begins. It is during this stage that
we collect as much data as possible about the target. This process might
involve network scanning to detect live systems, open ports, and services
running on hosts. It could also include domain name research to identify
mail servers, IP addresses, and related domains.
Several sophisticated tools aid us during this phase. Nmap, for instance, is a
versatile tool for network scanning and host detection. It helps us peek into
the target's network, offering insights into potential entry points. Nmap also

--- Page 108 ---
has a robust scanning and scripting engine built-in, with many open-source
repositories of scripts available online. Be careful when using them, as
some of the scripts are labeled dangerous because they will identify and
then actively exploit any vulnerabilities they find.
Wireshark is another crucial tool, enabling us to inspect network traffic in
detail, thereby detecting vulnerabilities and anomalies. If a pen tester is able
to identify specific types of services in use by the client via network traffic
analysis, it will greatly assist in later phases of testing.
Moreover, OSINT frameworks like Maltego offer powerful features to
harvest data from various public sources related to domains, IP addresses,
and even individuals. Using these frameworks, one can gather data and
visually map the relationships and links between entities of interest.
Finally, Google Dorks can help discover information that would otherwise
remain hidden from a regular search. By using advanced operators in the
Google search engine, testers can extract specific information about the
target, like exposed file directories, open ports, or even vulnerabilities. For
a deeper dive into the art of Google Dorking, consider exploring the Google
Hacking Database maintained by Offensive Security:
https://www.exploit-db.com/google-hacking-database
The planning and reconnaissance stages of mobile penetration testing, while
time-consuming, set the stage for a structured, efficient, and effective
testing process. The importance of these initial steps cannot be overstated,
as they fundamentally shape the trajectory of the testing procedure.
Therefore, spending adequate time on these stages is a worthwhile
investment. In a well-executed assessment, the planning and reconnaissance
phase may take up to 40-60% of the entire assessment period, as it lays the
foundation for everything that comes after.
Scanning and enumeration
In this phase, the tester begins to understand how the target system responds
to distinct types of traffic. This often involves scanning the system using
tools like Nmap, Nessus, and OpenVAS to ensure open port services are
running and to identify potential vulnerabilities. Enumeration involves

--- Page 109 ---
collecting more detailed information about identified services and networks,
which can help in devising a plan for the attack.
A tool that proves highly instrumental at this stage is Nmap, an open-source
network scanner designed to discover hosts and services on a network, thus
creating a map of the battlefield. Nmap employs raw IP packets to
determine what hosts are available, what services those hosts are offering,
what operating systems they are running, what type of packet
filters/firewalls are in use, and dozens of other characteristics.
Alongside Nmap, we can employ Nessus, a widely used vulnerability
scanner, to scour our target system for potential vulnerabilities. With its
regularly updated plugin library encompassing tens of thousands of
individual vulnerability checks, Nessus provides in-depth analysis that,
when coupled with manual testing techniques, greatly aids in discovering
flaws within a mobile system's security fortifications.
Another trusted comrade in this process is OpenVAS, a software framework
of several services and tools offering vulnerability scanning and
vulnerability management. Like Nessus, OpenVAS helps identify potential
weaknesses by scanning the target's ports and services.
A newer entry to the scanning game is Nuclei, which is an open-source tool
with many active contributors adding new scan profiles daily. It is
suggested that the reader experiments with all of the scanners and sees
which one works for their use case.
Following scanning, we delve into enumeration, which involves gathering
additional, more granular information about the identified services and
networks. At this stage, our objective is to extract as much information as
possible to understand the target system better and tailor our attack plan
effectively. For instance, if we know a particular version of a server
software is running, we can research known vulnerabilities specific to that
version.
Enumeration may include techniques such as DNS lookups and zone
transfers, SNMP sweeps, querying network directories, or even gathering
information via social engineering.

--- Page 110 ---
The scanning and enumeration stage is an essential gear in the machinery of
penetration testing. It is a stepping stone from the initial information-
gathering to the more proactive stages of vulnerability assessment and
exploitation. Patience, perseverance, and thoroughness in these stages could
often mean the difference between a successful and a failed penetration test.
Cultivating these skills, you lay the groundwork for a successful pentest.
Figure 3.1: Scanning for vulnerabilities is often like scanning the skies for signals
Gaining access
In the gaining access (exploitation) phase, the tester attempts to exploit the
vulnerabilities identified in the previous phase to gain access to the system.
It is the stage where the silent observation ends, and the active engagement
begins, where the weaknesses we have identified transform into potential
gateways into the system. This process could involve SQL injection, Cross-
Site Scripting (XSS), exploiting unpatched services, or any number of
other options.
In the exploitation phase, the techniques we employ depend on the
vulnerabilities we have discovered. For instance, we might use SQL
injection if we find a vulnerable input field that communicates with a

--- Page 111 ---
database. SQL injection involves inserting malicious SQL statements into
these fields, effectively tricking the application into performing actions it
should not. In a worst-case scenario, this could lead to full disclosure of the
database or even remote code execution.
Another potential vector of attack we might employ is XSS if we discover
that the application does not properly sanitize user input. An XSS
vulnerability allows us to inject malicious scripts into web pages viewed by
other users, leading to a variety of potential attacks, such as stealing
sensitive information or performing actions on behalf of the user.
If we identify unpatched services or out-of-date software, we can seek
known exploits to leverage these weaknesses. Unpatched software often
contains publicly known vulnerabilities that attackers can exploit using the
proper techniques and tools.
Our trusted ally in this phase is Metasploit, an invaluable framework for
developing, testing, and executing exploit code. It comes pre-loaded with a
myriad of exploits for known vulnerabilities and provides us with an
extensible platform for writing our own when needed. Additionally, we
might utilize more specialized tools tailored to exploit certain types of
vulnerabilities, depending on what we have discovered during the scanning
and enumeration phase.
In the grand scheme of penetration testing, the exploitation phase serves as
a testament to the tester's prowess and ingenuity, a place where theoretical
knowledge meets practical application. Yet, it is essential to remember that
our purpose remains firmly rooted in fortifying security and promoting
safer digital ecosystems, not undermining them.
Maintaining access
Maintaining access (Post-exploitation) phase involves trying to retain the
access gained in the previous phase by installing some sort of backdoor into
the system. The idea here is to mimic advanced persistent threats, which
remain in the system for a long time without being detected. It should be
noted that not every penetration test requires persistence after exploitation,
so be sure to review the scope and understand the client’s wishes. If you
want to focus on stealthy persistence and operating inside a client’s network

--- Page 112 ---
undetected, consider researching Red Team engagements, as they tend to
have a lot fewer restrictions in terms of what can be done and for how long.
To retain access, we typically install some form of backdoor into the
system. This backdoor is essentially a secret passage that allows us to
bypass regular authentication methods. As we are imitating APTs, this
backdoor should be as clandestine as possible, designed to stay under the
radar while still affording us the access we need.
Crafting a backdoor requires a keen understanding of the target system and
its vulnerabilities. We have to be mindful of any security measures in place
that might detect our backdoor, such as Intrusion Detection Systems (IDS)
or anti-malware software. The backdoor should also be resilient and able to
survive system restarts and software updates.
Different tools and frameworks can assist in creating and managing
backdoors. Metasploit, which we have already met in the exploitation
phase, has numerous payloads designed for maintaining access. If custom
tooling appeals to you, there are many resources online about malware
analysis and development, but it is beyond the scope of this book.
Our actions in this phase also extend to understanding what the system
offers. We have gained access, but what can we actually access? This could
involve data exfiltration, where we inspect and possibly extract sensitive
data from the system. Alternatively, we could explore lateral movement
opportunities to see if we can extend our reach into other systems or
networks connected to our initial target.
The post-exploitation phase is where we fully appreciate the implications of
successful penetration. Installing a backdoor and trying to retain access, we
emulate the potential threats an application can face once a breach occurs.
The insights we gain help us understand the depth of possible intrusions,
ultimately aiding us in building more robust and secure mobile applications.
Analysis and reporting
Finally, the tester must compile a report detailing the vulnerabilities found,
data that was extracted, how long they maintained access, and how they
exploited the vulnerabilities. The report should also include

--- Page 113 ---
recommendations for mitigating the vulnerabilities found during the testing
process.
It is also good practice to include a short, to-the-point executive summary at
the beginning of the report. A good executive summary should be one to
two pages long and not be too technical. This serves as an easily digestible
snapshot of the report for those short on time. Although we, as testers,
would prefer that managers and stakeholders read our entire report, it is
often the case that an information security manager or CISO only has a few
minutes to learn the details of a report, so the executive summary is all that
they read.
The primary objective of the report is to document the vulnerabilities
discovered, the data that was potentially exposed, how long access was
maintained, and how the vulnerabilities were exploited. Remember, the
essence of our role as ethical hackers is not merely to exploit but to
enlighten and transform potential weaknesses into learning opportunities.
Our report should provide a clear and understandable overview of our
findings, ideally allowing even a non-technical reader to grasp the
implications.
In detailing the vulnerabilities, we should strive to include as much context
as possible. We could detail the process used to identify the vulnerability,
the potential impact if exploited, and, where applicable, any data that was
put at risk. For instance, if a particular SQL injection vulnerability exposed
user credentials, this should be explicitly stated and explained.
Also important is documenting how we maintained access and the
implications of this. The post-exploitation phase is a sneak peek into what a
malicious attacker could do if they gained access, and by highlighting our
own activities, we can better underline the importance of addressing
vulnerabilities.
Crucially, the report must also include recommendations for mitigating the
vulnerabilities we found during the testing process. Each recommendation
should correspond to a specific vulnerability, providing a clear path for the
developers to address the issues. These recommendations can range from
suggesting code modifications, infrastructure changes, or even staff training
if the vulnerabilities were related to human factors.

--- Page 114 ---
As we compile the report, it is vital to remain cognizant of the importance
of clear communication. We are not merely recounting a tale of cyber
adventure but providing a roadmap to better security practices.
Throughout all of these phases, communication and ethical practices are
essential. Since penetration testing often involves exploiting security
vulnerabilities, it is important to have clear permission and defined
boundaries. Also, any findings should be reported to the organization
immediately to minimize potential risks. It is important to know who to
contact at any given time if something breaks or if a severe vulnerability is
discovered. Clear lines of communication and escalation can help to prevent
misunderstandings that can cause the test to be delayed or derailed. In a
worst-case scenario, an uninformed information security manager can
involve law enforcement if they are not aware of the ongoing test and the
vulnerabilities discovered.
It bears repeating that the only difference between the actions of a skilled
penetration tester and those of a malicious hacker is that the penetration
tester has a document that states that they are legally allowed to perform the
intrusion. In one infamous case, two physical penetration testers in the
United States performing an assessment on behalf of a local government
municipality were arrested and charged for the intrusion despite having
what they believed was legal authorization. More details can be found here:
https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/pen-testers-who-
got-arrested-doing-their-jobs-tell-all
Always ensure that you have proper permission, proper documentation of
your permission, and that the party giving the permission is authorized to do
so.
Communication is the bedrock of ethical hacking. At every stage of our
testing, from planning to reporting, clear communication is essential.
Before the first packet is sent or the first line of code is analyzed, we must
have unequivocal permission to conduct the testing. This permission is not a
mere formality but a mutual agreement between the tester and the
organization. It spells out the objectives, the scope, the methodologies, and,
importantly, the boundaries of the testing process.

--- Page 115 ---
This agreement often formalized as a Rules of Engagement document,
ensures that all parties have a shared understanding of the testing's purpose,
limits, and expectations. It safeguards against potential legal complications
and misunderstandings that could arise. Additionally, SANS provides
various templates for scoping, statement of work, and rules of engagement
documents at:
https://www.sans.org/posters/pen-test-rules-of-engagement-
worksheet/
https://www.sans.org/posters/pen-test-scope-worksheet/
As we identify vulnerabilities and potential security risks, it is our
responsibility to report these findings to the organization promptly. This
minimizes the window during which the identified vulnerabilities can be
exploited, bolstering the application's security posture.
Speaking personally, I have had several instances during my career where I
discovered a severe vulnerability, immediately followed by direct evidence
that malicious attackers had already exploited the vulnerability and were
active on the system. Needless to say, the client was quickly contacted, and
the assessment was put on hold until a proper incident response
investigation could take place.
Furthermore, we must exercise restraint and responsibility in exploiting the
identified vulnerabilities. We aim to uncover weaknesses, not cause harm.
Therefore, we should always avoid actions that could unduly impact the
organization’s operations or its users. For instance, while testing a
messaging app, sending a flood of messages to test for potential denial of
service vulnerabilities would be inappropriate and disruptive.
As we approach the reporting phase, the spirit of clear communication is
reignited. The findings, insights, and recommendations we share must be
comprehensive, understandable, and actionable. Whether it is a developer
implementing the proposed fixes or a decision-maker gauging the system's
security level, our report should provide value to all stakeholders.
In conclusion, ethical practice and clear communication are not merely
peripheral aspects of penetration testing; they are its very heart. We are not
just testers or hackers; we are custodians of trust and security. It is a

--- Page 116 ---
challenging role, no doubt, but one that can make a significant impact in
this age of ubiquitous mobile computing. As ethical hackers, we play an
integral role in ensuring that this digital world is as secure as it can be.
Mobile penetration testing
Mobile penetration testing is a specialized domain of penetration testing
focusing on identifying vulnerabilities in mobile applications and devices.
This discipline often follows a methodology similar to standard penetration
testing but includes unique considerations for the mobile environment.
The world of mobile technology is vast, with different operating systems,
hardware capabilities, network interfaces, and millions of applications.
Android and iOS form the crux of this world, each boasting a unique
architecture and security framework. As we proceed on our quest, we must
familiarize ourselves with the inner workings of these operating systems,
their application development frameworks, file systems, and permission
models.
An assessment begins, as it does with standard penetration testing, with the
planning and reconnaissance phase. However, information gathering for
mobile pentesting often involves additional techniques like app store
research, reverse engineering mobile apps to uncover hidden information,
and extracting configuration data from mobile devices. Tools such as
APKtool and JADX for Android and class-dump-z for iOS prove invaluable
during this phase.
Scanning and enumeration in mobile pentesting involve a detailed analysis
of the mobile application, the associated server-side components, and the
communication between them. Network vulnerability scanners might not
directly apply here. Instead, we employ mobile security frameworks like
MobSF and Drozer, which perform static and dynamic analysis of the
mobile application code, identify insecure storage practices, and detect
potential input validation issues.
Exploitation of vulnerabilities in mobile applications often demands a
different skill set. Manipulating request/response in transit, input fuzzing,
exploiting insecure storage, and insecure communication are commonly
employed techniques. Tools like Burp Suite and Wireshark retain their

--- Page 117 ---
relevance, while Frida and Xposed Framework can allow for runtime
manipulation of mobile application code.
Retaining access and establishing persistence becomes a nuanced affair in
mobile devices, given the sandboxed nature of most modern mobile
operating systems. Techniques often involve exploiting device-level
vulnerabilities or convincing the user to install malicious profiles or
certificates.
The final step, analysis, and reporting is as crucial in mobile pentesting as it
is in standard penetration testing. Here, we compile our findings, describe
the vulnerabilities and the associated risks, and provide a clear remediation
strategy. The OWASP Mobile Security Testing Guide offers an excellent
template for reporting mobile application vulnerabilities. In the end, our
goal is to aid organizations in fortifying their mobile application's security
posture against the myriad threats looming in the cyber realm.
During this process, it is important that we make a distinction between
penetration testing of mobile devices and applications and penetration
testing with mobile devices and applications. While they share the root
concept of penetration testing, their goals, methodology, tools, and scope
could vastly differ. Understanding this difference is a stepping stone in our
journey of mastering mobile penetration testing.
Penetration testing of mobile devices and applications involves the
assessment of the security posture of mobile devices or applications
themselves. The tester attempts to exploit potential vulnerabilities in the
mobile device's hardware, firmware, operating system, or, in the case of
applications, in their code or associated server-side components. Tools that
aid in this process are primarily geared towards static and dynamic analysis,
reverse engineering, and network manipulation. The OWASP Mobile
Security Testing Guide (MSTG) offers a comprehensive manual for
conducting this kind of testing, with detailed instructions and
recommendations for tools.
In contrast, penetration testing with mobile devices and applications aims to
exploit vulnerabilities in other systems using mobile devices as the
attacking platform. Here, the mobile device acts as a conduit, facilitating
the execution of exploits or reconnaissance activities. Tools used in this

--- Page 118 ---
scenario are typically mobile versions or adaptations of conventional
penetration testing tools. For instance, zANTI and cSploit are network
analysis toolkits for Android that come with a host of features like network
scanning, man-in-the-middle attacks, password cracking, and vulnerability
checking. NetHunter is also a fully-fledged Android-based penetration
testing platform, but it will be covered in depth later in this book.
It is also important to note that these two concepts are not mutually
exclusive. In some scenarios, a penetration tester might use a mobile device
to attack another mobile device or application. For instance, you could use
an Android device running an application like dSploit or zANTI to carry out
a penetration test on another mobile device or application. It is a bit of a
mix-and-match situation, tailored to fit the unique requirements of the
testing scenario.
Understanding this distinction also enables us to appreciate the differences
in the skills required. While both involve a solid understanding of
penetration testing principles and techniques, testing of mobile devices and
applications necessitates a strong grasp of mobile architectures,
programming languages, and specific tools. Conversely, testing using
mobile devices demands familiarity with mobile-based penetration testing
tools and, often, the ability to operate within the constraints of a mobile
environment.
Conducting penetration testing of mobile devices
In this section, we turn our focus to the hands-on process of conducting
penetration testing, specifically on mobile devices. This crucial phase in
mobile security analysis involves a unique set of challenges and techniques
distinct from traditional network or application penetration testing. We will
guide you through the systematic approach required to test mobile devices
effectively, starting with comprehensive planning and reconnaissance. This
includes understanding the device's architecture, operating system, and the
apps it runs. We will then dive into the methodologies for performing both
static and dynamic analysis, which are key to uncovering vulnerabilities
that could be exploited by attackers. This process also encompasses an in-
depth examination of the device's file system and network communications,
allowing for a thorough assessment of its security posture. By following the

--- Page 119 ---
steps laid out in this section, you will gain the insights and skills needed to
proficiently identify and mitigate potential security threats in the ever-
evolving landscape of mobile devices.
Planning and reconnaissance
The first stage remains similar: defining objectives and scope, such as
identifying which mobile applications or devices are to be tested.
Reconnaissance involves collecting data about the target application or
device. This might include understanding its functionality, gathering
publicly available information, and identifying associated network services
or backend APIs.
As with other types of penetration testing, the initial step requires us to
establish the objectives and scope of our test. It is crucial to identify which
mobile applications or devices are to be tested and outline the testing's
limitations. Whether it is an Android application, an iOS device, or a mobile
web app, each requires a unique approach and set of skills. Setting clear
boundaries and expectations at this stage will help streamline the
subsequent steps.
Reconnaissance, the process of collecting data about the target application
or device, is the detective work of penetration testing. During this phase, the
pen tester becomes familiar with the target, understanding its functionality,
features, and behavior. This could involve manually using the application or
device, reading documentation, checking user reviews, and even analyzing
the manufacturer's or developer's online presence. Furthermore,
fingerprinting web servers and databases with the help of a utility like
Nmap aids in understanding the technological stack supporting the target,
paving the way for more focused attacks later in the testing process.
Next, pen testers might gather publicly available information relevant to the
target. For applications, this can involve looking for open-source
repositories of the application's code, user forums, or any disclosed issues
about the application. On the other hand, for devices, you might look for
firmware updates, device specifications, or known issues.
Identifying associated network services or backend APIs is another crucial
step in this phase. Network traffic analysis tools, like Wireshark or Burp

--- Page 120 ---
Suite, can be used to monitor and analyze traffic between the mobile
application or device and its backend servers. This can reveal the structure
of network requests, which APIs are used, the data format, and sometimes
even sensitive data transmitted without adequate security measures.
Finally, it is always a good idea to document your findings for future
reference. The information gathered in the reconnaissance phase can
provide valuable insights into potential vulnerabilities, guide the next steps
in the testing process, and serve as a useful resource for post-test reporting.
Remember, this stage of the process is about gaining as much knowledge as
possible to inform your testing approach. It is the groundwork for
everything that follows, so a thorough and thoughtful reconnaissance phase
can significantly enhance the effectiveness of your testing efforts.
Static and dynamic analysis
In mobile penetration testing, this phase involves both static and dynamic
analysis. Static analysis involves examining the application's source code or
compiled binaries to identify potential vulnerabilities, while dynamic
analysis involves observing the application's behavior in real-time. Tools
such as JADX, Ghidra, and MobSF are used for static analysis, while Frida
and Burp Suite are used for dynamic analysis.
Static analysis is the process of examining the application's source code or
compiled binaries to identify potential vulnerabilities without actually
executing the application. Think of this as reading a book, you scrutinize
each line, each word, looking for patterns and anomalies. In the context of
an application, these could be poorly implemented functions, insecure API
calls, or hard-coded sensitive information such as passwords or encryption
keys. For compiled binaries, decompilers like JADX for Android apps and
Hopper for iOS apps can translate machine code back into a more readable
format, allowing us to dissect the application's inner workings.
Static analysis is where tools like Mobile Security Framework (MobSF),
JADX, and Ghidra come into play. Each of these tools offers unique
capabilities that assist in this intricate process. MobSF is an automated, all-
in-one mobile application (Android/iOS/Windows) pen-testing framework

--- Page 121 ---
capable of performing static and dynamic analysis. It can help us
understand what an app does and how it does it.
JADX, on the other hand, is a lightweight and easy-to-use tool that allows
for the decompilation of Android APKs. With it, we can take a compiled
Android app file and transform it back into a more human-readable format,
namely source code. This can be incredibly useful when examining how an
application operates, potentially revealing hidden vulnerabilities in the
process.
Ghidra is another powerful tool in our arsenal, a Software Reverse
Engineering (SRE) suite of tools developed by the National Security
Agency (NSA). While Ghidra can seem a little daunting due to its wide
range of functionalities, it can be an invaluable tool for static analysis. It
helps you to dive deep into the compiled binaries and makes it easier to
understand their structure and functionality.
In stark contrast to the dissection room-like environment of static analysis,
dynamic analysis feels more like observing an exotic animal in its natural
habitat. Here, the application is active, lively, and engaging in its regular
routines. We watch closely, recording its behaviors and identifying anything
unusual or indicative of potential security flaws.
To perform dynamic analysis, we first need to run the application in a
controlled environment. We act as the puppeteer, controlling various facets
of the application's execution while scrutinizing its responses. It is akin to a
scientist experimenting in a lab, manipulating variables, and observing the
outcomes. Throughout this process, we are looking for any abnormal
behaviors or potential vulnerabilities that might manifest during the
application's runtime.
Tools like Frida and objection play pivotal roles in the dynamic analysis
stage. Frida is like a syringe that injects our own code into the application,
allowing us to modify its behavior in real time. It is an extremely powerful
tool that helps us manipulate the running application, offering insight into
how it handles and responds to different conditions. As a scriptable dynamic
instrumentation tool, Frida lets us interact with the application's internal
functions and data, making it a vital asset in our quest to identify potential
weaknesses.

--- Page 122 ---
Objection, a runtime mobile exploration toolkit built on top of Frida, comes
in handy for digging even deeper. With objection, we can peek into the
more obscure corners of both iOS and Android applications. Its explorative
capabilities make it perfect for investigating the application's runtime
environment, revealing details about data storage, network requests, and
user interface components. It is like having a high-powered microscope to
examine the living cells of an organism.
However, as powerful as these tools are, using them effectively requires a
deep understanding of the application's underlying technology stack and a
solid foundation in programming principles. You need to understand the
code you are injecting and anticipate how the application might react. For
further studies, consider delving into topics like Runtime Application Self-
Protection (RASP) and Code Injection Techniques. Understanding these
concepts will help you better wield tools like Frida and objection,
enhancing your effectiveness during dynamic analysis.
Ultimately, dynamic analysis is like the real-time exploration of a living,
breathing application. It provides unique insights, unlike static analysis.
Understanding and utilizing these methodologies, we move one step closer
to becoming skilled mobile application penetration testers capable of
unraveling even the most complex security puzzles.
The combination of static and dynamic analysis enables us to inspect an
application from multiple angles. While static analysis provides a
theoretical understanding of potential vulnerabilities based on the
application's code, dynamic analysis confirms these vulnerabilities in a
practical, real-world context. This dual approach ensures a thorough
examination of the application, increasing the likelihood of identifying
security weaknesses.
It is important to note, however, that these tools and methods are not a
silver bullet. While they can assist in identifying potential issues, it is the
tester's knowledge, experience, and creativity that truly uncovers the hidden
vulnerabilities. As you analyze the code or observe the application, always
think about how an attacker might misuse the functions or features you are
studying.

--- Page 123 ---
The exploration continues further as we delve into the fascinating world of
reverse engineering. In this stage, we work to understand an application's
binaries to discover hidden functionality or bypass security mechanisms.
Tools such as IDA Pro, Hopper, and Radare2 become our allies in this
process, providing the ability to disassemble and debug mobile applications.
We decode, dissect, and learn to comprehend the language of the
applications we are testing, a critical step in our journey as mobile
penetration testers.
As we delve deeper into the rabbit hole of mobile application penetration
testing, we find ourselves in a realm that can be likened to an
archaeological excavation. This world is that of reverse engineering and
decompiling apps. Reverse engineering allows us to analyze compiled
binaries of applications in a bid to uncover hidden functionalities or to
circumvent security measures that have been put in place. It is like cracking
open a safe or deciphering a secret code. The thrill of unveiling the hidden
truths is immeasurable.
To assist us in our expedition, we have a toolbox full of sophisticated
utilities. For instance, we have IDA Pro, a state-of-the-art disassembler and
debugger that is considered the gold standard in the field. Using IDA Pro,
we can pick apart the application, dissecting it byte by byte to understand
its inner workings. Imagine IDA Pro as your high-tech magnifying glass,
allowing you to scrutinize every minute detail.
Then, we have Hopper, a reverse engineering tool that is especially useful
for static analysis. Hopper helps us dissect compiled programs into an
understandable form. Picture this as translating an ancient language into
modern speech, transforming the unintelligible into the comprehensible.
Another useful tool in our kit is Radare2, an open-source toolkit for
disassembly, debugging, and more. With Radare2, we can take a deep dive
into the application's binaries, taking them apart, inspecting their
functionality, and putting them back together. It is akin to dismantling a
complex timepiece, examining each gear and spring, and then reassembling
it.
As we decode and dissect, we become conversant in the language of the
applications we are testing. We learn to interpret the symbols and

--- Page 124 ---
instructions, transforming strings of ones and zeroes into meaningful
information. In this process, we begin to understand the application's logic,
its decision-making process, its vulnerabilities, and its defenses. This
critical understanding empowers us to pinpoint the application's weaknesses
and uncover ways to bypass its security measures.
To enhance your knowledge of reverse engineering, consider diving into
literature about assembly language programming, the inner workings of
compilers, and binary exploitation techniques. This journey of constant
learning is what makes the world of mobile application penetration testing
so endlessly fascinating and rewarding.
Device and filesystem analysis
This phase involves testing the security mechanisms of the mobile device,
the application's data storage and handling practices, and the permissions
model. Our focus broadens from the mobile application itself to its
interaction with the underlying device and operating system. This can
include checking for insecure storage of sensitive data, misuse of system
resources, or vulnerabilities within the mobile operating system.
Mobile applications do not operate in isolation; they interact with the
device's hardware, system resources, other apps, and the operating system
itself. Thus, understanding how the application utilizes these resources is
crucial to evaluating its overall security. We can explore the application's
permission model, what system resources it has access to, and whether it is
following the principle of least privilege. If an application has more
permissions than it needs, this could create opportunities for malicious
activities. For instance, an application that unnecessarily requests
microphone access might be exploited to eavesdrop on the user.
Data storage practices of an application form another critical facet of device
and filesystem analysis. Mobile apps often store data locally on the device,
be it user credentials, personal data, or transaction information. Our task is
to examine where and how this data is stored. We can probe the
application's cache, database files, shared preferences, or any other files it
creates, looking for sensitive data stored insecurely. Tools such as Android

--- Page 125 ---
Debug Bridge (adb) and iExplorer can aid in navigating the device's
filesystem and examining these data storage areas.
Understanding how an application stores data can provide significant
insights into potential security flaws. Whether it is investigating how an
application handles cache data, scrutinizing file permissions, or evaluating
data transmission methods, a thorough analysis of mobile application data
storage is key. If an application stores sensitive information improperly, it
could open up vulnerabilities that may be exploited by malicious attackers.
When it comes to mobile application penetration testing, we often think of
high-tech exploits or meticulously written lines of code. However,
sometimes, the key to cracking an application's defenses lies not in
sophisticated code but in something much simpler: how the application
stores and handles data. The way an application treats its data is akin to how
a person manages their personal belongings. If carelessly left around,
someone could stumble upon something valuable. Similarly, if an
application mishandles sensitive data, it may leave an open door for
malicious attackers.
Like detectives combing through an individual's personal effects for clues,
our task is to scrutinize how the application stores and manages its data. We
probe into how the application handles cache data, which could contain
valuable information in plain sight. If an application fails to properly secure
its cache, sensitive user data might be left lying around, open for anyone
with access to the device to see. This is comparable to leaving your house
keys under your front doormat; anyone who knows where to look could
find them.
Investigating file permissions is another key aspect of our data storage
analysis. Every file in an application has a set of permissions that dictate
who can view, modify, or execute it. If these permissions are set improperly,
it could expose the file to unauthorized access or modification. It is the
equivalent of leaving your car unlocked with the keys inside; anyone who
happens to pass by could take it for a spin.
We also need to delve into how the application transmits data. Are the
communication channels secure? Is sensitive data being sent over an
encrypted connection? Imagine this as sending a letter containing sensitive

--- Page 126 ---
information. Would you choose to send it through registered mail, ensuring
it is secure and only the intended recipient can access it, or would you send
it as a standard letter, risking exposure to anyone along the delivery route?
Lastly, we should pay attention to how the application stores sensitive
information. This could include user credentials, personal data, payment
details, or any other form of sensitive information. If this data is stored in
an insecure manner, it could be akin to leaving a treasure chest unguarded.
One slip-up here could lead to severe data breaches and the potential loss of
user trust.
A thorough analysis of mobile application data storage is a crucial step in
penetration testing. For further understanding of the risks and protections
regarding data storage, consider researching topics such as Secure Data
Storage Practices, Data Encryption Techniques, and Secure Data
Transmission Protocols. These areas of study will provide deeper insights
into the intricacies of data management in mobile applications and enhance
your ability to spot potential vulnerabilities.
Further, the device itself and the mobile operating system come under
scrutiny. This involves inspecting the security mechanisms of the mobile
device, such as its encryption standards, device access controls (like
biometric or password protection), and the security of its communication
channels (like Bluetooth or NFC). The operating system's security
configuration and any potential vulnerabilities are also explored in this
stage.
The essence of device and filesystem analysis lies in considering the
broader picture. It is not only the application that can be a source of
vulnerabilities but also the wider ecosystem in which it operates. Any flaw
or oversight in the interaction between the application, the device, and the
operating system can become a potential entry point for an attacker.
Network communication analysis
This phase involves inspecting the network communications of the mobile
application to identify any data leakage or weaknesses in data transmission.
This can involve intercepting traffic with tools like Wireshark or Burp

--- Page 127 ---
Suite, analyzing SSL/TLS usage, or testing for secure implementation of
APIs.
Our first step into this phase might involve intercepting and inspecting the
network traffic from the mobile application. Tools like Wireshark or Burp
Suite can be instrumental here. They allow us to capture and dissect
network traffic, giving us insights into what data is being transmitted, where
it is going, and how it is being secured. This interception process, akin to
wiretapping a phone line, can reveal sensitive data being transmitted in
plaintext, insecure or inappropriate API calls, or other security
misconfigurations.
Next, we delve into the application's implementation of SSL/TLS protocols.
Secure Sockets Layer (SSL) and its successor, Transport Layer Security
(TLS), are cryptographic protocols designed to provide secure
communication over a network. However, they are not immune to
misconfigurations or outdated implementations. Analyzing the application's
SSL/TLS usage can unearth issues like weak cipher suites, outdated
protocol versions, or vulnerabilities like Heartbleed.
One of the central pillars of network communication for mobile
applications is their use of Application Programming Interfaces (APIs).
These are the conduits that allow the application to interact with backend
services, databases, or other resources. Therefore, testing for secure API
implementation forms a crucial part of this phase. This can encompass
checking for authentication and authorization flaws, data validation issues,
or insecure endpoint configurations. Insecure Direct Object References
(IDOR) or Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) are common
vulnerabilities that might be uncovered during this testing.
Network communication analysis provides a window into the application's
interaction with the wider digital world, potentially revealing vulnerabilities
that might be invisible from a purely local analysis. It underlines the fact
that in our interconnected digital landscape, an application's security is
often as good as the security of its communication channels.
For more on secure network communication and API testing, you might
want to explore the Open Web Application Security Project (OWASP)
resources. The OWASP Testing Guide v4 provides a comprehensive

--- Page 128 ---
methodology for network and API testing, while the OWASP MSTG offers
specific guidance on mobile applications.
Figure 3.2: Network analysis is an essential part of the penetration testing process
Reporting
The last step involves compiling the results into a comprehensive report,
detailing the vulnerabilities found and their potential impact, and providing
recommendations for mitigation.
Creating a robust and detailed report begins with a thorough enumeration of
the vulnerabilities that we have discovered during the testing process. Here,
we list each vulnerability, its location (such as the specific component or
part of the code), its type (for instance, insecure data storage or insufficient
encryption), and the conditions under which it can be exploited. While
doing so, it is essential to provide enough technical detail that allows the
developers to understand and reproduce the issues. Tools like OWASP ZAP
and Burp Suite that we have used during the testing can also assist in
generating detailed vulnerability reports.
Next, we delve into an analysis of the potential impact of each identified
vulnerability. In other words, we answer the question of what a malicious
actor could potentially achieve if they exploited this vulnerability. This
could range from unauthorized access to sensitive user data to potential

--- Page 129 ---
disruptions of service. An honest and comprehensive assessment of these
impacts is vital to understanding the real-world implications of the
vulnerabilities and prioritizing their resolution.
To breathe life into our report, it can be beneficial to include real examples
or case studies of each vulnerability being exploited. If permitted within the
scope of the penetration test, screenshots, logs, or video recordings of the
exploitation can be incorporated. This not only adds a layer of authenticity
to the report but also makes the vulnerabilities more tangible for the
stakeholders.
Now, having laid out the issues and their implications, we move to the most
constructive part of the report: the recommendations for mitigation. Here,
we provide specific and actionable advice to address each vulnerability
based on best practices and industry standards. This could include
recommending code changes, system configuration adjustments, or even
broader policy changes within the organization.
Lastly, an executive summary at the start of the report is a must-have. This
high-level overview is particularly beneficial for non-technical
stakeholders, providing them with a quick understanding of the key findings
and recommendations. When writing an executive summary, a good trick is
to imagine that your tech-illiterate grandparents have five minutes to read it
and need to understand what could happen, why it is bad, and how to fix it.
A well-crafted report is more than a mere list of findings; it is a tool for
communication, education, and improvement. It creates a bridge between
the testers and the developers, fostering a culture of understanding and
shared responsibility for security.
Conducting penetration testing with mobile devices
Our journey through the domain of mobile penetration testing takes an
intriguing turn when we consider the prospect of conducting penetration
testing with mobile devices. The pervasive nature of mobile technology,
coupled with their computational capabilities, has made them viable
platforms for conducting security assessments of networks, servers, and
even other devices. Mobile devices, when equipped with the right tools, can

--- Page 130 ---
be used as effective platforms for penetration testing of networks, servers,
or other devices.
As we equip ourselves with specific tools for testing mobile applications
and devices, we will need to load up our mobile devices with penetration
testing tools. Thankfully, the open-source community and many security
vendors have stepped up to the challenge, developing mobile versions of
popular penetration testing tools. NetHunter, for instance, is a penetration
testing platform for Android devices developed by the creators of Kali
Linux. It brings together an extensive suite of tools for network analysis,
attack detection, and more. We will explore it in-depth later in this book.
When conducting penetration testing with mobile devices, the same
methodology that we have previously outlined applies. During the planning
and reconnaissance phase, you would define your test's objectives and
scope, just as before. However, with a mobile device as your testing
platform, you now have the added advantage of mobility. This can be
particularly useful for testing Wi-Fi networks, for instance, where your
physical location can impact your testing process.
The scanning and enumeration phase involves network scanning and
service identification, and there are mobile versions of tools like Nmap and
Wireshark for this purpose. However, keep in mind that conducting these
activities on a mobile device might present unique challenges due to
hardware limitations and potential restrictions imposed by mobile operating
systems.
Exploiting identified vulnerabilities and maintaining access is also possible
using mobile devices. Tools like Metasploit have mobile versions that you
can use for crafting and launching attacks, and when it comes to post-
exploitation activities, mobile devices, due to their connectivity options and
discreet form factor, can sometimes provide unique advantages.
Network communication analysis, on the other hand, can be a little tricky
on a mobile device due to restrictions on capturing network traffic. Still,
with the right tools and possibly some workarounds, it is feasible. Shark for
Root, for instance, is a traffic sniffer tool for Android that can be used to
capture network packets.

--- Page 131 ---
Lastly, the analysis and reporting phase remains largely unchanged. The
findings from the test conducted using a mobile device will be compiled
into a report similar to what we have previously discussed. However, it is
important to highlight any unique considerations or limitations encountered
while conducting the testing using a mobile device.
Setup
The first step is setting up the mobile device with the required tools. For
Android, tools like NetHunter can transform the device into a penetration
testing tool. On iOS, jailbreaking the device and installing tools through
Cydia are required.
For Android devices, Kali NetHunter stands out as a prevalent choice
among penetration testers. Developed by the team behind Kali Linux,
NetHunter is a penetration testing platform that effectively turns your
Android device into a mobile fortress of network analysis and hacking
tools. Installing NetHunter can be relatively straightforward, but it generally
requires a device with an unlocked bootloader. Some might even require a
custom recovery like Team Win Recovery Project (TWRP) to be
installed. However, once set up, it provides a powerful arsenal of tools at
your fingertips. Refer to Chapter 2, Setting up Your Device, for more
information about setting up NetHunter.
AndroNix is another fantastic tool for Android users. It allows you to run
Linux distributions and applications on your Android device, which opens
up a whole new world of desktop-grade penetration testing tools on your
smartphone. One notable aspect of AndroNix is that it does not require
rooting your device, making it more accessible for those who prefer not to
root their devices (AndroNix Documentation).
For iOS users, the pathway is a bit more winding. Due to the restrictive
nature of the iOS environment, you will need to jailbreak your device to
install and run most of the penetration testing tools. Jailbreaking is a
process that removes the restrictions imposed by Apple on iOS devices,
allowing users to install software from sources outside of the Apple App
Store. Once a device is jailbroken, you can use platforms like Cydia to
install a variety of tools.

--- Page 132 ---
Cydia is a package manager mobile app for iOS that enables a user to find
and install software not authorized by Apple on jailbroken iPhones, iPads,
and iPod touch devices. It is a common platform used by security
professionals to install pentesting and hacking tools on their iOS devices.
However, a word of caution here. Both rooting your Android device and
jailbreaking your iOS device can expose you to additional security risks and
may void the manufacturer's warranty. So, it is essential to understand the
implications and have clear permissions and boundaries, as we have
discussed earlier.
Scanning and reconnaissance
Similar to standard penetration testing, you can use your mobile device to
gather information about the target. Tools like Network Mapper (Android)
and iNet (iOS) can help scan the network and identify targets.
In this phase, our goal is to understand the network environment, uncover
the systems within it, and ascertain the services they offer. We achieve this
through network scanning, a method akin to a sonar system in submarines.
It sends out signals to the network and listens for responses, thereby
revealing information about the devices connected to the network and the
services they offer.
On Android, Network Mapper stands as a popular tool for this task
(Network Mapper; Android Apps on Google Play). As the name suggests, it
is a mobile adaptation of Nmap, a renowned network scanning tool widely
used in standard penetration testing. Network Mapper retains much of the
functionality of its desktop counterpart. It allows you to scan for open ports,
running services, device details, and even vulnerabilities in some cases.
For those on iOS, iNet offers a similar set of capabilities (iNet: Network
Scanner on the App Store). iNet scans the network to detect connected
devices, complete with their IP addresses, vendor names, and network
names. It also provides a simple interface to ping devices, providing a quick
method to check the availability of hosts on the network.
Performing reconnaissance at this stage helps us paint a detailed picture of
the target environment. It reveals potential points of entry and the landscape

--- Page 133 ---
we will navigate in the subsequent phases. One notable aspect to remember
is that different tools can provide different insights. Therefore, it is often
beneficial to employ a variety of tools during this phase.
Exploitation
Mobile devices can also be used to launch attacks on the target.
Applications such as zANTI and Nmap can help in this phase, allowing you
to exploit vulnerabilities, perform MITM attacks, or manipulate network
traffic.
In the realm of mobile exploitation, zANTI reigns as a popular and potent
tool. Designed by Zimperium for Android devices, zANTI is a mobile
penetration testing toolkit that enables IT security administrators to simulate
malicious network attack techniques. From vulnerability scanning and
Man-in-the-middle (MITM) attacks to password cracking and network
exploits, zANTI equips us with an array of options to challenge the security
of our target networks.
Simultaneously, the venerated tool Nmap is also at our disposal, complete
with its scripting engine, which offers a range of pre-defined scripts for
various kinds of exploits. Although traditionally a network scanner, Nmap's
versatile scripting engine allows us to exploit identified vulnerabilities and
gain further insight into their potential impact.
When using these tools, it is essential to understand and respect the gravity
of the actions we undertake. An MITM attack, for example, allows us to
intercept, alter, or even block the communication between two parties
without their knowledge. However, with great power comes great
responsibility. These techniques should be utilized only in an ethical,
approved manner and exclusively for the purpose of improving the security
posture of the targeted systems.
Post-exploitation and analysis
Some tools can help maintain access after an attack or analyze the
vulnerability. These could include packet sniffing tools, traffic recorders, or
vulnerability scanners.

--- Page 134 ---
A fundamental aspect of post-exploitation involves maintaining the access
gained during the exploitation phase. Like an adventurer who has found a
hidden treasure, the goal here is to ensure continued access to the found
vulnerabilities, almost akin to keeping the map to the treasure. There are
various tools that can aid in this process. For instance, Metasploit's
Meterpreter provides functionalities for pivoting, port forwarding, and
session management that are incredibly useful for maintaining access.
Additionally, packet sniffing tools become essential assets for the post-
exploitation phase. These tools, like Wireshark, allow us to monitor and
analyze network traffic at a granular level, helping us uncover any residual
vulnerabilities and gather vital data for analysis. Packet sniffing is
essentially eavesdropping on the network, collecting data packets to analyze
their contents.
Traffic recorders also serve as helpful tools during this phase, logging
network traffic for subsequent in-depth analysis. Popular traffic recording
tools such as Tcpdump and Tshark allow for real-time packet capture and
storage, which can later be inspected to glean useful information about the
target network and the efficacy of the attack.
Complementing these tools are vulnerability scanners like Nessus or
Nexpose. These tools act as vigilant guards, constantly on the lookout for
any new or remaining vulnerabilities within the network or system. By
performing automated scans, they can provide updated insights about the
system's security posture even after the initial attack.
The tools and techniques used during the post-exploitation phase should be
selected based on the specific context of your pen-testing exercise and the
nature of the target system. While Metasploit's Meterpreter might be apt for
maintaining access, you may choose between Wireshark or Tcpdump based
on your specific traffic analysis needs. Similarly, the choice between Nessus
and Nexpose for vulnerability scanning could depend on the complexity of
your target network and the level of detail required in your vulnerability
reports.
Reporting

--- Page 135 ---
Like any penetration testing process, the results need to be compiled,
including the vulnerabilities found, data exploited, and suggested mitigation
steps.
During the reporting stage, our primary task is documenting the
vulnerabilities we have uncovered. These vulnerabilities, the digital chinks
in the armor, should be recorded in great detail. Not only do we list each
vulnerability, but we also describe how we discovered them, the potential
risks they pose, and the data they expose. Providing these details helps the
security team of the organization understand the severity of each
vulnerability, aiding in the prioritization of their response.
Following the vulnerabilities, we record the data exploited during the
process. This includes any sensitive data we managed to extract or
manipulate during our testing. Again, the specifics are important here. We
explain how we were able to access the data, whether it was through an
SQL injection or through insecure data storage practices. We also explain
what kind of access we could gain, whether it was user-level access or
deeper, administrative-level access.
We then proceed to suggest mitigation steps for each of the identified
vulnerabilities. This is where our expertise really comes into play. We
leverage our understanding of the vulnerabilities to provide a detailed set of
recommendations for addressing them. These can range from patching
software and implementing secure coding practices to modifying system
configurations. Remember, our goal is to help the organization bolster its
security, so we need to provide as much guidance as possible on how to do
that.
The reporting stage is not simply about listing our findings. It requires
structuring our insights in a clear, concise, and accessible manner. The
report should enable the reader to easily grasp the vulnerabilities,
understand the potential risks, and start acting on the recommended steps. It
is also crucial to include an executive summary that provides a high-level
overview of the findings for non-technical stakeholders.
Mobile devices are fascinating in that they can be both the victim and the
attacker when it comes to hacking. In both cases, remember that permission

--- Page 136 ---
is essential before carrying out any form of penetration testing, and ethical
practices should be followed at all times.
Conclusion
In conclusion, navigating mobile application penetration testing is both
challenging and rewarding, offering a deep dive into the world of mobile
app security. This journey enhances our understanding and skill set with
each phase, from reconnaissance to vulnerability identification. Armed with
diverse tools like Wireshark and IDA Pro, each unique application we test
strengthens our expertise. The methodologies and strategies outlined here
guide us in securing these applications against emerging threats. Remember,
continual learning and engagement in the cybersecurity community are key
to staying ahead in this ever-evolving field, making the digital world safer
with each vulnerability uncovered and mitigated.
In the next chapter, we will have an in-depth understanding of techniques
for attacking Android applications, focusing on exploiting both client-side
and server-side vulnerabilities.
Points to remember
Understand standard penetration testing steps: Grasp the
foundational stages of penetration testing: planning, reconnaissance,
scanning, gaining access, maintaining access, and reporting.
Mobile device testing specifics: Acknowledge the specificities in
testing mobile devices, including static and dynamic analysis and
network communication analysis.
Device and filesystem analysis: Remember the importance of
analyzing the device and filesystem in mobile penetration testing.
Using mobile devices in penetration testing: Be aware of how
mobile devices can be used as tools in penetration testing, covering
setup, scanning, exploitation, and post-exploitation analysis.
Reporting in mobile penetration testing: Recognize the critical role
of reporting in both testing mobile devices and using mobile devices

--- Page 137 ---
for testing.
Questions
1. What are the key stages in standard penetration testing, and how are
they executed?
2. How does penetration testing for mobile devices differ from standard
penetration testing?
3. What techniques are involved in the static and dynamic analysis of
mobile applications?
4. Why is device and filesystem analysis important in mobile penetration
testing, and how is it conducted?
5. What are the steps involved in using mobile devices as tools for
penetration testing?
6. How is reporting conducted in mobile penetration testing, and why is it
important?

CHAPTER 4
Attacking Android Applications
Introduction
In this chapter, we embark on a comprehensive exploration of Android's
ecosystem, delving into its history, internal workings, and the unique
aspects of Android applications. We will unravel the complexities of
Android's architecture, examining its specific idiosyncrasies, the sandbox
environment, and the permissions model that underpin its security. The
discussion will extend to key features like Secure Interprocess
Communication, Paranoid Networking, and hardware-backed security. We
will also shed light on the roles of Google Play Protect and the Android
Keystore in safeguarding the platform. A significant portion of the chapter
is dedicated to the intricacies of Android security testing, covering
everything from common vulnerabilities and malware to the specifics of
Man-in-the-Middle attacks. This chapter is designed to provide a thorough
understanding of the multiple layers of security within the Android
platform, equipping readers with the knowledge to navigate and secure this
widely used operating system effectively.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
Android history

--- Page 139 ---
Android internals
Android applications
Android-specific idiocyncracies
Sandbox environment
Permissions model
Secure interprocess communication
