Hardware-backed security
Google Play Protect
Keystore
Android security testing
Vulnerabilities
Android malware
Man-in-the-middle attacks
Objectives
By the end of this chapter, readers will have a comprehensive understanding
of the Android OS, from its historical evolution to the intricacies of its
internal architecture. They will be able to grasp the fundamental concepts
and components of Android applications, including their entry points,
activities, and security elements like URL schemes and intent filters. The
chapter will also deepen the reader's knowledge of Android's unique
security features, including its sandbox environment, permission model,
Secure Interprocess Communication, Paranoid Networking, and hardware-
backed security. The readers will also learn about Google Play Protect and
the Android Keystore's roles in securing the platform and user data. The
chapter aims to equip readers with the skills to perform both security testing
and static and dynamic analyses of Android applications, enabling them to
identify common vulnerabilities, understand the nature of Android malware,
and recognize how Man-in-the-Middle attacks are executed on Android

--- Page 140 ---
platforms. This knowledge will provide a solid foundation for effectively
safeguarding Android applications against potential security threats.
Android history
Android's journey began when it was initially developed by Android Inc., a
small startup in Palo Alto, California, in 2003. Google, recognizing the
potential of the project, acquired Android Inc. in 2005, marking the search
engine giant's significant entry into the mobile operating system market.
This move would be a game-changer, opening up a new sphere of influence
for Google and reshaping the landscape of mobile technology.
Google's first Android version, Android 1.0, was unveiled in 2008 alongside
the HTC Dream smartphone. The OS showed promising initial features,
such as an integrated web browser, camera support, and access to the
Google Play Store, then known as the Android Market. Google's emphasis
on an open-source approach fueled the system's rapid growth, enabling
manufacturers and developers to customize and innovate freely.
Google's second version, Android 1.1, was released in 2009, with a host of
performance improvements and API fixes. However, the release of Android
1.5 Cupcake later that year truly marked the start of Android's iconic
naming scheme. Cupcake introduced groundbreaking features such as on-
screen keyboards and video recording capabilities. From the multitasking
capabilities introduced in Android 1.6 Donut, the versatile Google Maps
navigation features in 2.0 Eclair, to the radical interface redesign and
improved security features in 5.0 Lollipop, each version of Android has
played a part in shaping the operating system we know today.
Android's iterative evolution reached a significant milestone with the
release of Android 10. This version marked a departure from the tradition of
dessert-themed names, reflecting Google's more mature approach to the
development of the platform. Android 10 introduced a revamped
permissions system, offering users more control over what data they share
with apps, and introduced a system-wide dark mode.
The first Android device, the HTC Dream (or T-Mobile G1 as it was known
in the US), marked the beginning of a technological revolution. Android's
open-source model and adaptability to a wide array of devices helped it

--- Page 141 ---
rapidly gain popularity among device manufacturers and developers. This
willingness to foster innovation and encourage customization without
imposing strict boundaries set it apart from competitors.
As we delve deeper into the history of Android, we encounter various
versions of the operating system, each building upon its predecessor to
deliver improved functionality, enhanced security, and better user
experience. From its initial version, Android 1.0, we trace the system's
evolution through Cupcake, Donut, Éclair, to the latest version. Each is
named in alphabetical order after desserts, reflecting the sweet journey of
its evolution.
The following is a list of Android version names (according to the official
Android documentation: https://source.android.com/setup/start/build-
numbers), up to the time of writing. While the dessert naming convention
was abandoned after Android 9, the internal codenames still follow the
scheme and are included in parentheses:
Android 1.0
Android 1.1 (Petit Four)
Android 1.5 (Cupcake)
Android 1.6 (Donut)
Android 2.0 (Éclair)
Android 2.2 (Froyo)
Android 2.3 (Gingerbread)
Android 3.0 (Honeycomb)
Android 4.0 (Ice Cream Sandwich)
Android 4.1 (Jelly Bean)
Android 4.4 KitKat (Key Lime Pie)
Android 5.0 Lollipop (Lemon Meringue Pie)
Android 6.0 Marshmallow (Macadamia Nut Cookie)
Android 7.0 Nougat (New York Cheesecake)

--- Page 142 ---
Android 8.0 Oreo (Oatmeal Cookie)
Android 9 Pie (Pistachio Ice)
Android 10 (Quince Tart)
Android 11 (Red Velvet Cake)
Android 12 (Snow Cone)
Android 12L (Snow Cone v2)
Android 13 (Tiramisu)
Android 14 (Upside Down Cake)
Android 15 – Estimate 2024 release (Vanilla Ice Cream)
Simultaneously, we also witness the evolution of Android's security features
and protocols. Over time, Android has had to adapt and fortify its security
measures, responding to increasing threats and vulnerabilities. We delve
into features such as sandboxing, which isolates app data and code
execution from other applications, and permissions, a mechanism that
restricts the access of apps to certain parts of the user's device. This aids us
in understanding the unique challenges posed by Android application
security.
Furthermore, as we explore the vast market share commanded by Android,
it becomes evident why this platform is a significant focus for ethical
hackers and security researchers. As of June 2023, Android holds a
dominating share of the smartphone market, at 70.79%
(https://gs.statcounter.com/os-market-share/mobile/worldwide), making
it a worthwhile target for cyber-attacks while also making Android security
knowledge incredibly valuable.
As of the time of writing, the latest iteration of Android includes more
advanced features, focusing on enhancing user privacy, improving
accessibility, and fine-tuning the interface for seamless user experience.
Each new version introduces additional security measures, showcasing
Google's continued commitment to safeguarding user data and addressing
potential vulnerabilities.

--- Page 143 ---
Android internals
In this section of the chapter, we navigate through the intricate labyrinth of
Android's technical characteristics. A core understanding of the architecture
that drives this globally dominant operating system is crucial to
appreciating the complexity and breadth of its functions.
At its foundation, Android is built upon a modified version of the Linux
kernel. This is the heart of the system, managing core functionality such as
device drivers, power management, and memory management. While Linux
was initially developed for personal computers, Android has adapted the
kernel to suit the mobile environment, with specific optimizations for
unique aspects of mobile usage, such as power conservation and touch
screen input.
Further up the architecture is Android's unique application framework. This
layer provides developers with a robust set of APIs that enable interaction
with the underlying hardware and system services. By understanding this
framework, developers are empowered to create innovative apps that fully
utilize the capabilities of Android devices. For example, APIs allow
applications to use device capabilities like GPS, camera, and accelerometer,
providing a rich user experience.
Central to Android's operation is the Android Runtime (ART), which
replaced the Dalvik virtual machine with the introduction of Android 5.0
Lollipop. ART is responsible for executing application code, bringing apps
to life on the device. It features Ahead-of-time (AOT) compilation,
improved garbage collection, and improved debugging support, all of which
contribute to the performance and smoothness of Android applications.
Complementing ART are the native libraries, written in C and C++, which
provide a range of functionality from rendering graphics and media codecs
to data storage and web browsing. These libraries offer a foundation for
building many of Android's features and allow apps to use native code for
high-performance tasks.
On top of the system architecture are the application services, which offer
high-level access to essential device capabilities. These services include the
window manager, notification manager, and package manager, among

--- Page 144 ---
others, which manage the user interface, system notifications, and app
installation, respectively.
For readers eager to dive deeper into these intricate layers of Android,
further exploration is encouraged. The Android Source Project provides
comprehensive documentation, detailing the ins and outs of Android's
architecture. In-depth guides can be found on Android's official developer
portal. This additional research will equip readers with a deeper technical
understanding of Android, crucial for identifying and exploiting potential
vulnerabilities.
Android applications
Let us take a look at Android applications and their unique architecture.
Entry points
Entry points in an Android application represent the components through
which the system or a user can begin interacting with the app. These entry
points include Activities, Services, Content Providers, and Broadcast
Receivers, each having a unique role in the overall architecture of an
Android application.
An Activity represents a single screen with a user interface and is typically
the main entry point of user interaction with an app. For example, when a
user taps an app's icon on the device home screen, an Activity gets
launched. An Android application may contain multiple activities, each
having a distinct purpose. The Android system manages the lifecycle of an
Activity, providing hooks where developers can insert their code to react to
changes in the Activity's state, like creation, stop, or resume.
Services are another entry point in Android, but unlike Activities, they do
not have a user interface. Instead, Services are designed to perform
background tasks, like network operations or playing music, even when the
user is not interacting with the app. The system can start a Service through
explicit start commands or bind to it as needed, making them a flexible
entry point for tasks that need to run for extended periods or respond to
specific requests.

--- Page 145 ---
Content Providers offer a structured interface to application data. They
encapsulate data and provide mechanisms for defining data security and
access. While they are not typically used as entry points for user interaction,
the system or other apps can query or modify the data through the Content
Provider, effectively making it an entry point for data access.
Finally, Broadcast Receivers are designed to respond to system-wide
broadcast announcements, also known as Intents. Many broadcasts
originate from the system, for example, a broadcast announcing that the
screen has turned off, the battery is low, or a picture was captured. Apps can
also initiate broadcasts to let other apps know that some data has been
downloaded to the device and is available for them to use. Thus, Broadcast
Receivers act as entry points for responding to a variety of broadcast
messages.
Activities
Activities in Android are fundamental building blocks of an application's
user interface. Each Activity provides a screen with which users can interact
to do something, such as dial the phone, take a photo, send an email, or
view a map. Each Activity is implemented as a subclass of the Activity
class.
For example, consider an email application. One Activity might be
responsible for displaying a list of emails. When the user selects an email,
another Activity could be invoked to read the selected email. If the user
decides to write a new email, yet another Activity could be launched where
the user can compose the message. Each of these screens is implemented as
a separate Activity, and the transition between these Activities is managed
by the Android system.
Each Activity in an application follows a lifecycle, managed by the Android
operating system. The lifecycle consists of various states an Activity can be
in, from the time it is first created to when it is destroyed. Key lifecycle
callbacks include onCreate(), onStart(), onResume(), onPause(), onStop(),
and onDestroy(). Android calls these callbacks as an Activity enters a new
state, allowing the Activity to adjust its behavior based on its state. For
example, an Activity might initialize its user interface and prepare to

--- Page 146 ---
display data to the user in the onCreate() method and release these
resources in the onDestroy() method.
Furthermore, Activities can start other Activities by sending Intents. An
Intent is an object that holds the content of a message. For example, an
Activity might create an Intent with an action to send an email. The Android
system then finds the appropriate Activity (in this case, the email composer
Activity) to respond to the Intent.
URL schemes
URL schemes in Android enable an app to declare itself as a potential target
for opening certain URLs. When an app defines a custom URL scheme, it
essentially registers itself with the system as being able to handle requests
to open URLs that use that scheme. This makes it possible to launch an app
from a web browser, or another app, using a URL structured in a specific
way.
To define a custom URL scheme, an Android app must declare an
appropriate intent filter in its manifest file. The intent filter specifies the
type of action and the associated data (in this case, a URL) that the app
component (typically an Activity) can handle. For example, an app might
declare an intent filter to handle URLs with the scheme myapp://, meaning
that any URL starting with myapp:// could potentially open this app.
Once a custom URL scheme has been defined, clicking a URL with that
scheme can launch the associated app. This could happen from a web
browser, an email, a text message, or any other place where hyperlinks can
be activated. When such a URL is clicked, the system checks to see if any
installed app has declared capable of handling that URL scheme. If so, the
system launches that app, and the app can then extract information from the
URL as needed.
URL schemes can greatly improve the user experience by providing
seamless integration between different apps or between an app and a web
page. For example, a social media app might use a custom URL scheme to
enable links on a web page to open a specific user profile or post directly in
the app, rather than in the web browser.

--- Page 147 ---
However, it is important to implement custom URL schemes carefully, as
they can potentially be exploited for malicious purposes. For instance, an
attacker could create a malicious web page with a link that uses the custom
URL scheme of a legitimate app but includes harmful data in the URL.
Therefore, apps that use custom URL schemes should validate and sanitize
any data extracted from URLs before using it.
Content providers
Content providers in Android serve as a crucial component for data
handling. They offer a systematic way to share data among different
applications and are essentially a data-abstraction layer that encapsulates
data and provides a well-structured mechanism to handle it. Content
Providers function as the standard interface that connects data in one
process with code running in another process, facilitating secure
interprocess communication.
A content provider manages a specific set of data typically stored in the
device's persistent storage (like SQLite database), in memory, or even over
a network. The data can be any structured data, such as a collection of
images, personal contact information, or user settings. Each content
provider is identified by a unique authority string used in the content URI
for the provider.
The Android system provides a number of built-in content providers that
manage data such as audio, video, images, personal contact information,
and so on. However, developers can also create custom content providers to
manage application-specific data. The data managed by a content provider
can be queried by any other application with the necessary permissions,
making them a universal access point for specific types of data.
In terms of security, Android Content Providers have built-in features that
allow developers to restrict access to the data they manage. Developers can
declare permissions in the AndroidManifest.xml file, requiring any other
application that wants to access their Content Provider to have the
appropriate permissions. This ensures that only authorized applications can
access sensitive data.

--- Page 148 ---
Services
Services in Android are an essential part of an application that can run in
the background for an indefinite period, perform long-running operations,
or perform work for remote processes. Unlike Activities, Services do not
provide a user interface. They are ideal for operations that need to keep
running without direct interaction with the user or even after the user
switches to another application.
There are two types of Services in Android: started and bound. A started
Service, once initiated, can run in the background indefinitely, even if the
component that started it is destroyed. For instance, a Service might be
started when a user wants to play music. The Service could continue
running, playing the music in the background, even if the user navigates
away from the music playing application.
On the other hand, a bound Service offers a client-server interface that
allows components to interact with the Service, send requests, receive
results, and even do so across processes with Interprocess
Communication (IPC). Components bind to a Service, and once that
happens, the Service is kept running as long as the connection is open.
When all clients unbind, the system destroys the Service.
One of the distinguishing features of a Service in Android is its lifecycle,
which is different from that of an Activity. A Service has its lifecycle
methods (onCreate(), onStartCommand(), onBind(), onUnbind(), onRebind(),
and onDestroy()) that allow it to manage its setup, destruction, and handle
connections and disconnections.
However, Services need to be used judiciously as they are typically long-
lived and can consume resources even when the user is not interacting with
the app. If a Service performs intensive or blocking operations, it can affect
the performance of the app or even the entire system. For this reason, many
long-running Service operations are performed in a separate worker thread.
Broadcast receivers
Broadcast Receivers in Android are components that respond to system-
wide broadcast announcements or messages. These messages could be

--- Page 149 ---
issued by the Android system, for example, to notify apps that the device
battery is low, the device has just booted up, or the locale has changed, or
by applications to signal that some data or event is available for other apps.
An essential aspect of Broadcast Receivers is that they allow apps to
register for events that are outside of their application context, enabling an
app to execute logic even when it is not running in the foreground. For
example, an application could register a Broadcast Receiver that triggers a
notification when the device battery is low.
Implementing a Broadcast Receiver involves extending the
BroadcastReceiver class and overriding the onReceive() method. This
method is where the logic to execute when the Broadcast Receiver gets the
message is written. Broadcast Receivers are declared in the application's
manifest file, where they can be associated with an Intent Filter to specify
what types of broadcasts they should listen for.
Despite their utility, Broadcast Receivers should be used sparingly and
responsibly. They are not designed for long-running operations, and they
run on the main thread where UI operations take place, so heavy operations
can cause a slowdown in the user interface. As of Android 3.1, system
broadcast events are no longer delivered to Broadcast Receivers declared in
the manifest to prevent apps from adversely affecting system performance.
Intents
Intents in Android are messaging objects that play a crucial role in the
communication between various components of an Android application,
including Activities, Services, Content Providers, and Broadcast Receivers.
Intents can initiate actions and transfer data between components within the
same app or across different apps, facilitating robust and versatile
communication within the Android ecosystem.
There are two types of Intents in Android: explicit and implicit. Explicit
Intents are used when the target component is known. For instance, one
Activity in an app might start another Activity in the same app using an
explicit Intent. In this case, the Intent directly specifies the component to
start by its class name.

--- Page 150 ---
On the other hand, implicit Intents do not name a specific component.
Instead, they declare a general action, and the Android system selects a
device component to handle the request. For example, an app might use an
implicit Intent to request that some text be shared, without specifying which
app should handle the sharing.
In addition to their action, Intents often carry data, using a bundle object to
encapsulate key-value pairs. This data can be primitive types, such as
integers or Booleans, or more complex types, such as parcel able or
serializable objects. This allows an Intent to start another component and
transfer the necessary data for that component to perform its work.
The Android system also uses Intents for system-wide broadcasting of
information, such as notifying apps of system state changes or user actions.
For instance, when a text message is received, or the device battery
becomes low, the system sends a broadcast Intent. Any Broadcast Receiver
that is registered and has the appropriate Intent Filter for these actions can
then respond to these events.
Intent Filters
Intent Filters in Android are powerful mechanisms that determine the types
of intents a component, such as an Activity, Service, or Broadcast Receiver,
is capable of responding to. They essentially describe what an Android
component can do and what types of intents it can handle, allowing the
Android system to route intents to the appropriate component based on the
characteristics of the intent.
The primary elements of an Intent Filter are action, category, and data.
The action element describes the general action to be performed, such as
view or edit. It correlates directly with the action specified in the Intent
object. An Intent Filter can declare multiple action elements, meaning it can
handle multiple types of actions.
The category element provides an additional layer of specification. It
characterizes the type of component that should handle the intent, or the
context in which it should be handled. Some common categories include
CATEGORY_LAUNCHER, which indicates that the Activity is the entry point of

--- Page 151 ---
the app, or CATEGORY_BROWSABLE, which implies that the Activity can be
safely invoked by a browser.
The data element is the most complex part of an Intent Filter. It describes
the type of data (by MIME type) that the component can handle and the
URI schemes that it can accept. Data specified in an intent can be a URI
and/or a data type. If both are specified in an intent, the system will only
match intent filters that match both.
Intent Filters are typically declared in the application's manifest file. Once
declared, the Android system can automatically route intents to the right
component. This routing is a crucial part of handling implicit intents, where
the target component is not explicitly defined. When an implicit intent is
dispatched, the system searches all registered intent filters and delivers the
intent to the component with the most appropriate filter.
Android-specific idiosyncrasies
As we traverse the Android landscape, we delve into the distinct
characteristics that lend Android both its strengths and weaknesses.
Android's open-source nature, a trait uncommon in the realm of mobile
operating systems, is one of these defining attributes. This openness fosters
an environment ripe for innovation, where developers and manufacturers
can access and modify the operating system's source code to create unique
user experiences and introduce novel features. Android's source code,
primarily released under the Apache License, has spurred the creation of a
diverse array of custom ROMs, offering a wealth of options for the
adventurous user.
Yet, the very feature that fuels this innovation: Android's open-source
nature also presents a significant challenge, known as fragmentation. The
sheer variety of manufacturers and devices that use Android, each with their
specific hardware configurations and custom user interfaces, leads to a
highly fragmented ecosystem. This fragmentation can result in
inconsistency in user experience across different devices and pose
considerable difficulties for app developers, who must ensure compatibility
across a broad range of devices.

--- Page 152 ---
Furthermore, the decentralization inherent to Android's model means that
manufacturers, not Google, are often responsible for pushing system
updates to their devices. Consequently, the rollout of updates can be slow
and inconsistent, with some devices running older, potentially less secure
versions of Android for extended periods. This inconsistency can exacerbate
known vulnerabilities, leaving devices exposed to potential exploits for
longer than necessary.
Notably, these characteristics create a unique environment for security
practitioners. The fragmentation and inconsistent update schedules can lead
to a broader attack surface, with differing vulnerabilities across devices.
This expansive surface requires security professionals to consider a
multitude of scenarios when identifying and addressing security
vulnerabilities.
Now, the focus shifts to Android's security features. We shall explain
Android's multi-layered security model, designed to protect user data,
maintain system integrity, and provide app isolation. Key aspects, such as
the sandboxing environment, permission model, secure interprocess
communication (binder), Paranoid Networking, hardware-backed security,
and regular security updates, are examined. We will also discuss the role of
Google Play Protect, which scans and verifies applications on the Play
Store.
With the landscape of Android firmly established, the chapter turns its
attention to the cornerstone of our investigation: Android's security features.
The operating system boasts a multi-layered security model, conceived to
safeguard user data, uphold system integrity, and facilitate app isolation. At
the crux of this model is Android's unique sandboxing environment. Each
application operates within its isolated sandbox, a distinct area of storage
and memory space. This isolation restricts applications from accessing each
other's data and system resources, thereby containing any potential damage
an app might cause.
In addition to the sandboxing mechanism, Android employs an explicit
permission model to maintain control over sensitive resources and user
data. Whenever an application seeks to access a resource classified as
dangerous by Android, like the camera, microphone, or user's contacts, it
must request the user's consent. The user can then grant or deny these

--- Page 153 ---
permissions at a granular level, providing a powerful mechanism for
protecting privacy. Further reading on the Android permissions model is
recommended, with the official Android Developers documentation
providing a comprehensive guide (Android Developers, App permissions
overview).
Moreover, Android relies on secure IPC mechanisms, notably the binder, to
ensure that applications can safely interact without breaching their
sandboxes. The binder acts as the middleman for these communications,
handling requests and ensuring they adhere to Android's security and
privacy requirements.
Paranoid networking is another component of Android's multi-layered
security model. This feature restricts how apps interact with the device's
network stack, denying unnecessary connections and limiting potential
attack vectors. Understanding how Android manages network traffic can be
crucial when investigating network-related vulnerabilities, with further
exploration recommended in resources like the AOSP network stack
documentation.
An essential aspect of Android's security model is its hardware-backed
security. Android leverages hardware security modules like ARM's
TrustZone or Google's Titan M chip, providing an additional security layer
that isolates sensitive operations like encryption and secure booting from
the rest of the system. This hardware-backed approach can significantly
mitigate the impact of certain software vulnerabilities.
Lastly, this section covers Google Play Protect, a built-in malware
protection feature that scans and verifies applications on the Google Play
Store. Play Protect uses machine learning to identify harmful behaviors,
providing real-time malware detection and automatic system updates. This
protection extends beyond the Play Store, scanning all apps on the device to
protect users from Potentially Harmful Applications (PHAs).
By comprehending Android's multi-faceted security model, readers can
better understand how Android defends itself against attacks and how
potential vulnerabilities might arise within this system.
Sandbox environment

--- Page 154 ---
The Android sandbox environment is designed to isolate app data and code
execution from other applications, effectively limiting the potential damage
an exploited app can cause. But while the sandboxing environment offers
strong security by design, it is not flawless. As a result, vulnerabilities can
still be found, often linked to inappropriate handling of app permissions or
IPC.
The sandbox environment is a core component of Android's security
architecture, intended to isolate application data and code execution from
other applications. It is fundamentally designed to restrict the operations
that an app can perform, confining it to a private filesystem and specific
IPC interfaces. This compartmentalization minimizes the potential damage
an application can cause if it behaves maliciously or is compromised.
In practical terms, Android achieves this sandboxing by leveraging the
underlying Linux kernel. Each Android app runs in its process, with its
instance of the Dalvik virtual machine or, from Android 5.0 onward, the
ART. The Linux kernel assigns each app a unique User ID (UID) at
installation time. Consequently, file access permissions in the underlying
Linux filesystem enforce the sandbox boundary, making apps' private data
and code execution inaccessible to other apps.
Applications in the sandbox have limited access to system resources and
cannot, by default, interact with other applications or users' data. To access
resources outside their sandbox, applications must explicitly request
permission from the user. Android's permission system provides a
mechanism for extending the capabilities of an application, enabling it to
perform operations that might affect the user's data or the operation of other
applications, but only when explicitly granted by the user.
Moreover, Android's sandboxing mechanism is combined with other
security measures such as code signing, application vetting through the Play
Store, and various runtime security checks to help maintain a secure
environment. Code signing ensures that updates to an app come from the
same source and have not been tampered with, while runtime security
checks are used to detect and prevent potential security threats from
executing.

--- Page 155 ---
Permissions model
The Android permission model, another fundamental security feature,
controls how apps access sensitive resources and data on the device.
However, granting too many permissions or improper management of
permissions can lead to vulnerabilities. Users are guided through various
cases of improper permission use, providing them with a solid
understanding of how to prevent such issues.
Android's permissions model is a fundamental part of its security
architecture, designed to protect user data and system resources. It acts as a
gateway between an application and potentially sensitive data or system
functionality, ensuring that no application can access these without explicit
user consent.
Permissions in Android are divided into four categories: Normal,
dangerous, signature, and special. Normal permissions cover areas that pose
minimal risk to the user's privacy. For example, permissions to set the time
zone or access network state are considered normal. The Android system
automatically grants these permissions upon installation.
Dangerous permissions, on the other hand, could potentially involve user's
private data or affect the device's operation in significant ways. This
includes access to the user's contacts, SMS messages, or location data.
Since Android 6.0 (Marshmallow), apps need to request these permissions
at runtime, rather than at installation time, and users have the ability to
grant or deny these permissions.
Signature permissions are granted based on the signing certificate of the
application. An application can define its permissions that are enforced by
the system, and these are granted only if the requesting app is signed with
the same certificate as the app that declared the permission. This ensures
that only apps from the same developer can share certain capabilities.
Special permissions cannot be requested at runtime and are generally
reserved for system-level apps. These permissions are managed directly in
the system settings rather than through the normal permissions UI. An
example of a special permission is the draw over other apps permission,
which allows an app to create windows displayed on top of other apps.

--- Page 156 ---
Secure interprocess communications
Next, let us discuss secure interprocess communication, typically handled
through Android's binder framework. While the binder helps to prevent
unauthorized data access between apps, vulnerabilities can be exploited if
data is incorrectly handled or validated. Case studies of previous security
issues associated with interprocess communication offer insightful
examples of what to look out for (Further reading on these topics can be
found in resources such as the Android Security Best Practices guide
provided by Google).
Binder is the principal IPC mechanism used in Android, allowing for direct
calls across process boundaries. While common IPC methods like sockets
or pipes exist in Android due to its Linux heritage, the Binder IPC
mechanism is more robust, providing richer semantics such as reference
counting, strong typing, and the ability to transfer more complex data
structures.
Binder operates at the kernel level and provides a high-level interface to
developers. Each process interacting with the binder service has a separate
binder thread for performing IPC. When a binder object is shared between
processes, the receiving process sees a file descriptor that refers to this
binder object. However, the processes do not share the same instance of the
object. Instead, when a process sends a command to a binder object, the
kernel routes this command to the other process where the binder object
lives, enabling a remote procedure call.
One of the key security features of Android's Binder mechanism is its
robustness in identifying the UID and Process ID (PID) of the remote party
in an IPC conversation. The transparency and reliability of these identifiers
are enforced by the Kernel, making it difficult for an application to
impersonate another or escalate privileges. This helps to ensure that inter-
process communication remains secure, with permissions enforced at the
communication end-points.
Binder is also involved in Android's permission checks for IPC calls. For
example, when an application wants to interact with a system service
(which runs in a different process), the system service can use Binder to

--- Page 157 ---
confirm the identity of the calling process and verify whether it holds the
required permissions.
Paranoid networking
Paranoid networking is an essential part of Android's security model,
particularly in how it manages and isolates network access across different
apps and system components. This feature is built upon the Linux kernel's
network namespace functionality and offers an additional layer of
protection against possible network-based attacks or data breaches.
At a high level, paranoid networking ensures that different Android apps
and components cannot directly interact with each other's network traffic.
Each application runs in its unique Linux user namespace and network
namespace, effectively providing sandboxing at the network level. This
isolation prevents a compromised or malicious app from eavesdropping on
or interfering with another app's network communication, enhancing the
overall security.
Furthermore, paranoid networking controls access to certain network
resources such as network sockets. Not all applications need to access all
aspects of the networking stack, and by enforcing restrictions, it minimizes
the potential for misuse. Android also includes a built-in firewall, called
netd, that provides an additional layer of control over network
communications.
Android's VPN support also ties into paranoid networking. Android
provides APIs that apps can use to offer VPN services, routing traffic from
other apps or the entire system through a specific network tunnel. The VPN
sandbox enforces a strict set of permissions to ensure only the VPN app can
access the tunnel, leveraging network namespaces to isolate traffic.
Hardware-backed security
Android's hardware-backed security involves the use of dedicated hardware
to perform critical security functions, separate from the main operating
system. This approach provides additional protection against software-

--- Page 158 ---
based attacks and offers a secure environment for handling sensitive
operations.
One common form of hardware-backed security in Android devices is ARM
TrustZone. TrustZone is a security extension of the ARM architecture that
provides a secure execution environment, isolated from the regular
operating system. The main idea behind TrustZone is the partitioning of
system resources (like CPU, memory, and peripherals) into a secure world
and a normal world. The secure world, the Trusted Execution
Environment (TEE), can store and process sensitive data, such as
cryptographic keys, biometric data, and secure services like Digital Rights
Management (DRM).
Another hardware security module used in Android devices, particularly
Google's Pixel devices, is the Titan M chip. Titan M is a custom-built,
enterprise-grade security chip designed to secure the bootloader, disk
encryption, secure app data, and the integrity of the operating system. It
provides tamper-resistant storage for keys, enforces the integrity of the boot
process, and protects against firmware rollback to older, potentially
vulnerable versions.
Security modules like TrustZone and Titan M often have their secure boot
process, distinct from the device's main boot process. This ensures the
integrity of the secure operating system and apps within the secure
environment. Furthermore, these modules can provide cryptographic
services, like hardware-based key generation and storage, and secure
random number generation, which are critical for many security protocols.
Google Play Protect
Google Play Protect is a security suite for Android devices that helps
protect your device from PHAs. It is integrated into every device with
Google Play and is always updating. It performs several critical functions to
ensure the security and integrity of Android applications and the overall
Android ecosystem.
One of its primary functions is to continuously scan and verify Android
apps installed on a device, whether from Google Play or other sources. This
process uses machine learning and other advanced techniques to identify

--- Page 159 ---
potentially harmful behaviors, check for security issues, and ensure that all
apps adhere to Google's policies for app behavior. If Google Play Protect
detects a harmful app, it will notify the user and often can remove the threat
automatically. This is of particular interest to penetration testers and red
teamers, as a novel malware sample created for use in an assessment could
be slurped up and analyzed by Google before you even have the chance to
deploy it. Be mindful of where your samples reside and where they are sent.
Additionally, Google Play Protect enforces app verification at install time.
When a user attempts to install an app, Google Play Protect checks the app
against Google's constantly updated catalog of PHAs. If an app is identified
as potentially harmful, the user will be prevented from installing it, or they
will be clearly warned of the potential risk.
Another function of Google Play Protect is the Find My Device feature. This
feature allows users to locate, ring, or wipe their device if it is lost or stolen,
thereby protecting their information from unauthorized access.
Finally, Google Play Protect helps ensure the safety of Google Play itself.
Google uses the same technologies that power Google Play Protect to scan
apps before they are published in the Google Play store. Apps that violate
Google's policies are rejected from the store, providing a first line of
defense for users.
Keystore
The Android Keystore system is a crucial component of Android's security
infrastructure, offering a secure repository to store cryptographic keys in a
container, making it more difficult to extract them from the device. It is
intended to protect sensitive keys from being compromised, even if the
device or the software on it is exploited.
The Android Keystore operates at the system level and provides application
developers with APIs to securely generate, import, and use cryptographic
keys. These keys can be used for various purposes, such as establishing
secure connections, encrypting/decrypting data, and verifying the integrity
of data. Importantly, once keys are in the Keystore, they are usable but not
extractable, meaning they can be used for cryptographic operations but
cannot be directly read.

--- Page 160 ---
One important feature of the Android Keystore system is the ability to
enforce restrictions on how and when the keys can be used. For example,
keys can be configured to be usable only after the user has authenticated,
such as by entering a password or providing a fingerprint. This adds an
extra layer of security to key access, helping to protect against unauthorized
use.
Some Android devices also offer hardware-backed Keystore systems. These
systems store keys in a secure hardware module, such as the ARM
TrustZone or a secure element, providing an additional layer of protection
against software-based attacks. The hardware module is isolated from the
rest of the device's components, and it is designed to resist tampering and
extraction attempts.
Android security testing
Now that we have delved into the specifics of Android’s security defenses
and the systems that enable them, let us look at the offensive side of things.
By leveraging what we already know about Android and some Android-
specific security tools, we can craft and utilize some clever exploits in our
security assessments.
Android application vulnerabilities expose a vast landscape of potential
exploits, making it essential to deploy sophisticated tools for identification,
examination, and leverage. Drozer, JADX, Frida, and Ghidra, among
others, have surfaced as pivotal instruments in the cybersecurity toolkit for
this purpose.
Drozer, a comprehensive security and attack framework for Android, allows
users to interact with the Android OS's low-level components or an
application's high-level software stack. This interaction forms the basis for
detecting a variety of security vulnerabilities, including insecure data
storage, unsafe inter-process communications, and insecure WebView
implementations. This chapter includes real-world scenarios where Drozer
successfully exposes these vulnerabilities, complete with a step-by-step
guide on a vulnerable application (For further reading, consider the Drozer
User Guide).

--- Page 161 ---
Meanwhile, JADX is an open-source tool capable of decompiling Android
applications to render their source code visible, thereby permitting static
analysis. Such an analysis can disclose hidden vulnerabilities like hard-
coded sensitive data, insecure coding practices, or improper data encryption
techniques. Readers will come across multiple instances where JADX is
employed to scrutinize application source code and pinpoint vulnerabilities
(Detailed understanding of JADX can be gleaned from the official JADX
GitHub repository).
Frida, a dynamic code instrumentation toolkit, facilitates modification of an
application's code during runtime. This proves instrumental in discovering
vulnerabilities that might not be evident during static analysis. With Frida,
testers can introduce custom scripts into the application's runtime to
monitor its behavior, exposing potential security threats like insecure
network communications and insufficient input validation. The chapter
offers in-depth illustrations on employing Frida effectively to uncover such
vulnerabilities (The official Frida documentation serves as an extensive
guide on Frida).
Complementing the above, Ghidra is a formidable open-source reverse
engineering tool developed by the National Security Agency (NSA).
Offering functionalities such as disassembling, assembling, decompiling,
graphing, and scripting, Ghidra helps analysts understand how an
application works or find vulnerabilities within its code. Using Ghidra's
code decompiler makes it possible to reverse engineer an Android app's
compiled code back to its source code, helping to identify security
weaknesses or malware (For advanced usage of Ghidra, consult the official
Ghidra Documentation).
Additionally, tools like APKtool, for decoding resources to nearly original
form, and dex2jar, for converting .dex files to .jar format, are available.
These tools can convert a fully-deployed Android application into source
code, enabling various code analysis and whitebox testing methodologies.
Vulnerabilities
Using our new toolkit, we can conduct all manner of security testing against
Android targets. For now, let us look at a brief overview of common types

--- Page 162 ---
of vulnerabilities.
Authentication mechanisms often act as the first line of defense for an
application. However, issues such as weak or hardcoded credentials, and
insecure implementation of authentication protocols, can create exploitable
vulnerabilities. These can be bypassed using tools like ADB and Metasploit,
leading to unauthorized access to sensitive data. Authentication
mechanisms, while essential in securing an application's access, can
sometimes pose a weak link due to ineffective implementation or careless
practices. It is important to consider how weaknesses such as hardcoded
credentials, insufficiently strong credentials, and insecure implementation
of authentication protocols can be exploited.
Hardcoded or weak credentials are common vulnerabilities that attackers
can exploit. These vulnerabilities exist when developers, for reasons of
convenience or oversight, incorporate credentials such as passwords, API
keys, or tokens directly into the application's source code. Alternatively,
weak credentials refer to easily guessable or common passwords that do not
offer adequate security. By reverse-engineering the application using tools
discussed earlier, such as JADX and Ghidra, one can identify and exploit
these vulnerabilities.
To gain unauthorized access, the Android Debug Bridge (ADB), a
versatile command-line tool, can be utilized. ADB provides a UNIX shell
that can run various commands on an Android device or emulator. With
ADB, one can pull sensitive files, install or uninstall applications, and run
powerful shell commands, all of which can be used to bypass authentication
mechanisms.
On the other hand, Metasploit is an extremely powerful framework used for
penetration testing. Examples include leveraging Metasploit to conduct
brute-force attacks or exploit known vulnerabilities in the server's
authentication protocol. For instance, if an Android application deploys a
vulnerable version of the Apache web server, Metasploit could use its built-
in exploitation framework to compromise that server and, potentially, the
whole device.
Lastly, the insecure implementation of authentication protocols is another
significant issue. Protocols like OAuth, if not implemented correctly, can

--- Page 163 ---
lead to vulnerabilities that allow attackers to gain access to sensitive data or
even take over user accounts.
Android malware
It is also important to consider how to inject malicious payloads into
Android applications. Previously mentioned tools can modify APK files,
repackage them, and distribute the modified (malicious) versions.
Malware injection into Android applications presents a significant risk to
users and is an advanced method attackers use to compromise mobile
devices. In the Android ecosystem, APK files are employed for the
distribution and installation of mobile apps. Understanding the structure of
APK files is vital in this context. These are essentially archive files, similar
to ZIP format, consisting of the application's compiled code, resources,
assets, and manifest file. Decompiling these APK files using tools such as
APKtool, jadx, or Ghidra helps understand the internal workings of an app
and locate areas where malicious payloads can be introduced.
Once the APK is decompiled, the next step involves injecting malicious
code or payload into the app. This is a critical step and requires knowledge
of the app's source code and Android programming. Smali, an
assembler/disassembler for Android's dex format, is commonly used for this
process. Using Smali, one can insert a malicious payload into the
application's dex files, which contain the compiled code that the Android
runtime executes.
After modifying the application with the malicious payload, the next step is
to repackage the APK. Using APKtool, the modified app is recompiled into
a new APK that includes the injected payload.
Once repackaged and signed, the modified APK is ready for distribution. It
is important to consider the various ways attackers might distribute these
tampered apps, such as through third-party app stores, phishing emails, or
malicious websites. Understanding these methods can assist in developing
more secure applications and being aware of such threats in their
environments. Injecting malicious payloads into Android apps underscores
the need for proper code obfuscation, application integrity checks, and
secure coding practices.

--- Page 164 ---
Man-in-the-middle attacks
Next, let us look at how to intercept and modify network traffic using Man-
in-the-middle (MITM) attacks. This typically involves proxy tools like
Burp Suite and SSL/TLS interception with tools like SSLyze. Intercepting
and modifying network traffic is a formidable technique in the arsenal of a
penetration tester. A MITM attack occurs when a third-party intercepts
communication between two entities without their knowledge and
potentially manipulates the data.
The use of proxy tools such as Burp Suite is fundamental to MITM attacks,
as they allow for the interception, monitoring, and modification of network
traffic. Burp Suite, a popular penetration testing tool, works by rerouting
application traffic through the Burp proxy, allowing the tester to examine
and manipulate data as it transits to and from the target server.
While intercepting HTTP traffic might be straightforward, HTTPS requires
additional steps because it is a secure protocol encrypted with SSL/TLS. To
intercept this traffic, the tester needs to install the Burp Suite's CA
certificate on the Android device, which tricks the device into accepting the
Burp Suite as a trusted entity.
However, SSL/TLS interception might not be feasible if the application
uses certificate pinning, a security measure that associates a specific
cryptographic public key with a specific server. If the server's key does not
match the pinned key, the application will refuse to communicate, thwarting
attempts at SSL/TLS interception.
Despite the security measure of certificate pinning, bypassing it is possible
and often necessary in penetration testing. Bypassing certificate pinning
involves altering the application's code or runtime environment to accept
the tester's certificate.
Although this chapter provides an overview of common vulnerabilities and
attacks, we will take an in-depth look at conducting these assessments in
hands-on chapters later in this book. To wrap up our discussion of Android
security testing, let us look at Android application analysis, both static and
dynamic.

--- Page 165 ---
Static analysis
Static analysis is a method of examining the application's code without
executing it. This process involves investigating potential vulnerabilities,
coding malpractices, and other issues that could compromise the
application's performance or security. Here are some essential steps to
perform static analysis on Android applications:
1. Check for obfuscation, root detection, emulator detection, and
anti-tampering checks: Obfuscation makes the code harder to
understand and reverse-engineer, enhancing the application's resistance
against malicious attacks. Simultaneously, checks for root and
emulator detection ensure the app does not run in potentially insecure
environments. Anti-tampering checks add an extra layer of security by
verifying the application's integrity during runtime.
2. Search for interesting strings: Sensitive information like passwords,
URLs, API keys, encryption keys, backdoors, tokens, and Bluetooth
UUIDs may be found in the code. Their exposure could potentially
lead to significant security risks. The use of such information should
be carefully evaluated and protected.
3. Special attention to Firebase APIs: Firebase is a platform that
provides backend services for building apps. However,
misconfigurations in Firebase can lead to vulnerabilities. Therefore,
scrutinize the use and configuration of Firebase APIs to ensure secure
data storage and retrieval.
4. Read the manifest file: The Android manifest file provides essential
information about the app to the Android system, which the system
must have before it can run any of the app's code. Check if the
application is in debug mode, if the APK allows backups, and look for
any exported Activities, Content Providers, exposed Services, and
Broadcast Receivers. Misconfigurations in these areas can lead to
substantial security threats.
5. Examine data storage: Investigate whether the application is saving
data insecurely either internally or externally. Check for any hard-
coded passwords or if any sensitive data is being saved on the disk.

--- Page 166 ---
Assess the crypto algorithms used to ensure that they are secure and
up-to-date.
6. Check the libraries: Verify if all the libraries compiled using the
Position Independent Executable (PIE) flag. This feature enhances
security by allowing Address Space Layout Randomization
(ASLR), making it more difficult for an attacker to predict target
addresses.
7. Use of static analysis tools: Various static analysis tools can assist
during this phase by automating the scanning process and accurately
identifying potential security threats. These tools should form an
integral part of your static analysis methodology.
Dynamic analysis
Dynamic analysis involves examining the behavior of an application during
runtime. This is crucial for identifying security vulnerabilities and
performance issues that may not be apparent in static analysis. The
following are several key steps for conducting dynamic analysis on Android
applications:
1. Prepare the environment: The first step is setting up the right
environment, which can be online, local VM, or a physical device. The
environment should ideally mimic real-world usage scenarios to
uncover potential vulnerabilities accurately.
2. Check for unintended data leakage: This involves observing if the
application leaks sensitive information unintentionally through
logging, clipboard data, or crash logs. Such information leakage can
provide attackers with exploitable data.
3. Examine SQLite databases: Android applications often use SQLite
databases to store data. Check these databases to see if they contain
confidential information that might be accessible to other applications
or users.
4. Inspect exposed android components: Evaluate the application's
activities, content providers, services, and broadcast receivers for
potential vulnerabilities. If these components are exposed (can be

--- Page 167 ---
accessed by other apps), they might be exploited, leading to a
compromise of the application or user data.
5. Analyze network traffic: Pay close attention to the application's
network communication. Check if the application transmits sensitive
information in clear text or uses weak encryption algorithms, which
could make it susceptible to MITM attacks. Tools like Wireshark or
Charles Proxy can be instrumental in inspecting HTTP/HTTPS traffic
and identifying potential issues.
6. Check for common web vulnerabilities: If the application
communicates with a web server, check for common web
vulnerabilities. This includes issues like SQL injection, Cross-Site
Scripting (XSS), and Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF). Burp
Suite is an invaluable tool for this.
7. Android client-side injections (ACSI): ACSI can allow an attacker to
execute arbitrary code within the application's context. Look out for
these potential vulnerabilities during dynamic analysis.
8. Use dynamic instrumentation tools: Tools like Frida can hook into
the running application and manipulate its behavior or extract data.
This can be invaluable for obtaining dynamic data from the application
that may not be apparent during static analysis.
Conclusion
This chapter has provided a comprehensive view of Android application
attacks, highlighted common vulnerabilities and revealing various
methodologies for identifying and exploiting them. From insecure data
storage to weak encryption and improper handling of user permissions, we
have illuminated key issues facing Android applications.
As we transition to iOS, prepare to adapt to a new ecosystem with its
unique security considerations. The tools and techniques will change,
reflecting the differences between the Android and iOS platforms. However,
the underlying goal remains the same: safeguarding user data and privacy.
Our next chapter will delve into iOS's unique vulnerabilities and distinctive
exploitation techniques, helping us further in our mission to enhance the

--- Page 168 ---
security of mobile applications.
Points to remember
Android's evolution: Recall the history and development milestones
of Android OS.
Internal mechanics: Understand the intricacies of Android internals,
including its architecture and system components.
Application structure: Familiarize with the components of Android
applications, such as activities and URL schemes.
Android idiosyncrasies: Be aware of the unique characteristics and

security in the Android ecosystem.

Protect in maintaining platform security.

importance of the Android Keystore for secure storage.
Security testing: Know the methods and importance of conducting

and their implications.

--- Page 169 ---
Android malware: Recognize the nature of Android malware and
methods of detection.

attacks on Android platforms.
