CHAPTER 5
Attacking iOS Applications
Introduction
In this chapter, we delve into the sophisticated methodologies for penetration
testing on iOS applications, equipping readers with knowledge of both
client-side and server-side vulnerabilities. Emphasizing a hands-on
approach, the chapter provides an in-depth exploration of key tools and
technologies, such as Burp Suite for network traffic analysis, Frida for
dynamic app analysis, Hopper for reverse engineering, MachOView for
binary inspection, and Cydia Impactor for installing modified applications.
These tools are crucial for identifying and exploiting vulnerabilities, ranging
from flawed programming and insecure data storage on the device side to
insecure server configurations and scripting issues. This comprehensive
examination aims to empower readers with the skills to detect, exploit, and
ultimately strengthen the security of iOS applications, contributing to a more
secure digital ecosystem.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
History of iOS
iOS security mechanisms
Sandboxing

--- Page 172 ---
Code signing
Hardware-based security
App transport security
iOS vulnerabilities
Client-side vulnerabilities
Testing toolkit for iOS
Malicious iOS applications
Network interception
Objectives
By the end of this chapter, readers will have gained a thorough
understanding of iOS, starting with its historical context and key milestones.
The chapter explores the security mechanisms integral to iOS, including
sandboxing for application isolation, the significance of code signing for app
authenticity, and the role of hardware-based security in data protection.
Readers will also delve into App Transport Security and its impact on
securing network communications. A significant portion of the chapter is
dedicated to dissecting common vulnerabilities within the iOS ecosystem,
focusing particularly on client-side vulnerabilities, their characteristics, risks,
and exploitation methods. Additionally, the chapter provides an in-depth
look at tools and techniques for testing and exploiting iOS vulnerabilities,
including the construction and operation of malicious iOS applications and
the use of network interception techniques in penetration testing, along with
the associated risks and countermeasures.
History of iOS
iOS is a globally recognized mobile operating system second only to
Android in its extensive usage. Let us delve into the history of iOS and gain
an understanding of iOS's distinct characteristics and inherent security
mechanisms that have evolved to respond to the operating system's unique
challenges. With each upgrade, the platform has continuously improved its

--- Page 173 ---
defenses, leading to an increasingly complex interplay of security features
that potential attackers and, thus, security professionals must comprehend.
The global unveiling of iOS, alongside the iPhone, occurred at the Macworld
Conference and Expo in January 2007. This landmark event marked the
departure from niche devices such as Blackberries and Personal Digital
Assistants (PDAs) that dominated the scene at the time. iOS, with its
innovative multitouch display and user-centric design, set the precedent for
modern smartphones, reshaping our understanding of mobile technology.
In 2007, the consumer electronics industry was just beginning to grasp the
potential of smart devices. Apple's announcement served as a game-changer,
introducing a smartphone that seamlessly combined the features of a phone,
a widescreen iPod with touch controls, and an internet communication
device into one handheld gadget. This convergence of iOS and iPhone
technologies set the stage for a new era of digital interactivity.
From a cybersecurity standpoint, the inception of iOS added a new
dimension to the field of mobile security. With its groundbreaking features,
iOS introduced unique security challenges, necessitating a shift in how
security professionals approached the domain of mobile devices. These
initial iOS versions lacked critical security features we take for granted
today, such as app sandboxing, code signing, and data protection APIs.
The author of this book fondly remembers buying a first-generation iPod
Touch, an iOS device nearly identical to an iPhone but without a sim card
for phone or data connections. Due to the unsophisticated security, it was
trivially possible to jailbreak the device and install new apps that did not
come preloaded. This was significant, as the concept of an App Store was
not introduced until the following year.
In its earlier versions, iOS operated with a few native applications, with
security measures primarily focused on device security. With the
introduction of the App Store in iOS 2, Apple opened the doors to third-party
developers, exponentially increasing the number of applications and,
consequently, the potential attack vectors. It prompted the strengthening of
app-specific security mechanisms, including strict application vetting and
security measures such as sandboxing.

--- Page 174 ---
As we move further into the timeline, from the introduction of multitasking
in iOS 4 to the high encryption standards of iOS 8, each step brought fresh
challenges and spurred the development of new security measures. iOS 9
brought in further improvements with features like app thinning and on-
demand resources, which required new considerations in the application
security field.
As iOS evolved, Apple introduced these necessary security features to
protect user data and maintain system integrity, but these measures also
attracted the attention of malicious entities seeking to exploit any
weaknesses. This led to an ongoing race between exploit discovery and
system patching, setting the stage for the modern field of iOS penetration
testing.
A breakthrough in the security architecture of iOS devices was the
implementation of the Secure Enclave Processor (SEP). This dedicated
hardware module handles data encryption and secures sensitive user
information. It uses a unique ID that is inaccessible to the rest of the system,
reinforcing the security barriers within the device.
A real-world demonstration of the strength of this encryption technology
occurred in the aftermath of the San Bernardino terrorist attack in 2015. The
FBI recovered an iPhone belonging to one of the attackers but found
themselves unable to access the device's encrypted content. As a result, they
appealed to Apple, asking the company to create a backdoor to bypass the
iPhone's security features. However, Apple maintained its stance on user
privacy and security, denying the request.
This high-profile incident spotlighted the robust security measures integrated
into iOS devices and highlighted Apple's lengths to protect user data. While
this incident underscores the significant challenge cybersecurity
professionals face when attempting to access encrypted data on an iOS
device, it also demonstrates the crucial importance of their work. The case
served as a rallying point in the ongoing debate over privacy and security,
prompting discussions about how law enforcement and national security
interests could be balanced with individuals' rights to data privacy.
From a penetration testing perspective, understanding the implementation
and workings of security measures such as the SEP is essential. It represents

--- Page 175 ---
one of the primary hurdles to be overcome when testing iOS devices for
potential security vulnerabilities. Simultaneously, it provides a high bar for
robust security, guiding security researchers to uncover and patch potential
weaknesses in other systems.
This historical exploration of iOS provides perspective on its substantial
market share and exposes the unique security challenges and opportunities it
presents. Recognizing these transformations equips security professionals
with the knowledge to comprehend iOS's ongoing security challenges. It
highlights the importance of persistent vigilance in penetration testing and
exploit discovery, aiming to make the iOS application ecosystem safer and
more reliable.
Refer to the following figure:
Figure 5.1: iOS devices come in various sizes and shapes
iOS security mechanisms
We examine iOS's distinctive security mechanisms and how they are integral
to Apple's commitment to user privacy and data security. The iOS security
model uses layered protection, incorporating sandboxing, code signing,
hardware-based encryption, and App Transport Security.
Sandboxing
Sandboxing in iOS creates an isolated environment for each application,
preventing them from accessing data outside their designated area or

--- Page 176 ---
interfering with other applications' operations. This ensures that a potential
compromise of a single app does not grant access to the entire system or
other apps' data, hence limiting the potential damage.
Delving into iOS security, we find runtime security is of prime importance,
ensured through a sandbox, declared entitlements, Address Space Layout
Randomization (ASLR), and the Execute Never (XN) feature.
The sandbox security feature is a cornerstone of iOS's robust defense
mechanism. It limits third-party apps to their allocated spaces, blocking
access to files stored by other apps or alterations to the device. This safety
measure is like putting each app in a controlled environment where it cannot
affect others or the overall system. Crucially for penetration testers,
exploiting one app does not grant carte blanche access to the device or other
apps' data.
Furthermore, iOS offers protection through declared entitlements. Think of
these as passports for apps, granting them access to specific services like
iCloud or user information. Since these entitlements are digitally signed and
unchangeable, they drastically limit an app's ability to overstep its
boundaries or escalate its privileges. Understanding this layer of defense
allows security professionals to anticipate the challenges they might
encounter during vulnerability testing.
ASLR is another key security feature of iOS. ASLR mitigates the risks of
memory corruption bugs by randomizing the memory addresses of
executable code, system libraries, and related programming constructs. This
means attackers attempting memory manipulation attacks, like the return-to-
libc, face an added layer of complexity as they cannot predict the memory
address layout, minimizing the risk of successful exploitation.
Last but not least, the XN feature provided by ARM technology further
tightens iOS's defense by marking certain memory pages as non-executable.
In the unlikely event that an app needs a writable and executable page, it
faces stringent controls from the kernel, which checks for a specific, Apple-
only dynamic code-signing entitlement. Understanding this feature is vital
for penetration testers planning exploits involving code execution.
In essence, these mechanisms work in unison to fortify iOS against various
potential threats, serving as a testament to Apple's commitment to user data

--- Page 177 ---
security. A firm grasp of these principles is crucial for penetration testers to
design effective strategies for exploit discovery and helps them maintain an
ethical hacking stance – attack to protect. For more information, please refer
to Apple’s official documentation
(https://support.apple.com/guide/security/security-of-runtime-process-
sec15bfe098e/web).
Code signing
Next, we delve into the role of code signing. This mechanism ensures that
only trusted and verified code can be executed on iOS devices. Apple
digitally signs each application on the App Store after a thorough review,
and this signature is checked every time the app is loaded. Ensuring that
every software run on an iOS device has been vetted drastically reduces the
risk of installing malicious applications.
In the complex web of iOS security, app code signing plays a pivotal role. Its
processes are integral to maintaining an environment where all applications
are from known, validated sources and are free from unauthorized
alterations. The key mechanisms involved are mandatory code signing,
developer app signing through certificate validation, code signature
validation, and a specialized process for proprietary in-house apps.
Mandatory code signing is a crucial security protocol within iOS. After the
kernel boots up, it supervises all user processes and apps. To ascertain the
legitimacy and integrity of these apps, all executable code must be signed
with an Apple-issued certificate. Apple signs pre-installed apps like Mail and
Safari, while third-party apps undergo a similar validation and signing
process. This serves as a means of extending trust from the operating system
to the apps, curbing third-party apps from loading unsigned code or
engaging in self-modifying code.
When developers sign their apps, they do so through certificate validation.
To develop and install apps on iOS devices, developers must register with
Apple and join the Apple Developer Program. Apple verifies each
developer's identity before issuing a certificate, which allows them to sign
apps and submit them for distribution on the App Store. All apps on the App
Store are thus submitted by identifiable entities, discouraging the creation of
malicious apps. This also means that every app undergoes a review by Apple

--- Page 178 ---
to ensure it operates as described and is free from obvious bugs or other
issues. This process and the aforementioned security mechanisms build
users' trust in the quality of the apps they purchase.
iOS also supports code signature validation, where developers can
incorporate frameworks within their apps, usable by the app itself or
embedded extensions. To prevent the system and other apps from loading
third-party code within their address space, a code signature validation of all
dynamic libraries linked to a process at launch is performed. This is done
using a Team Identifier (Team ID) extracted from an Apple-issued
certificate. A Team ID, a unique alphanumeric string, ensures a program can
only link against a system library or any library bearing the same Team ID in
its code signature.
Regarding proprietary in-house apps, businesses can create them for internal
use and distribute them among their employees through the Apple
Developer Enterprise Program (ADEP). Users must have the relevant
provisioning profile installed to run these apps, assuring that only the
organization's intended users can load the apps onto their iOS devices. Upon
the first launch of any proprietary in-house app, the device must receive
confirmation from Apple that the app is permitted to run.
By understanding these mechanisms, penetration testers can plan for these
stringent security checks in their exploit discovery and penetration testing
efforts, thus facilitating better protection and defense against potential
attacks.
Hardware-based security
Hardware-based security, another critical pillar of iOS security, offers robust
protection for data stored on devices. Leveraging a dedicated SEP, iOS
ensures all user data is encrypted using a device-unique key inaccessible to
the rest of the system, providing an added layer of security even if the device
is physically compromised.
The architecture integrates a Central Processing Unit (CPU) that drives
system security features alongside dedicated silicon components handling
explicit security functions. These components support strictly defined
functions, thereby reducing the attack surface. The boot ROM, for instance,

--- Page 179 ---
establishes a hardware root of trust for secure boot, while dedicated
Advanced Encryption Standard (AES) engines ensure efficient and secure
encryption and decryption.
A central piece of this security infrastructure is the Secure Enclave, a
System on Chip (SoC) on recent Apple devices. Secure Enclave follows the
same design principles as the overall SoC, housing its distinct boot ROM
and AES engine. It forms the bedrock for the secure generation and storage
of encryption keys necessary for data-at-rest encryption, and it also guards
and assesses the biometric data for Face ID and Touch ID.
Data storage encryption has to be both speedy and efficient without exposing
the data or keying material used to establish cryptographic relationships. The
AES hardware engine addresses this by executing swift in-line encryption
and decryption as files are written or read. A specialized channel from the
Secure Enclave provides the necessary keying material to the AES engine,
safeguarding it from exposure to the Application Processor or the overall
operating system. This ensures technologies like Apple Data Protection and
FileVault protect user files without exposing long-lasting encryption keys.
Apple's secure boot is designed to guard the lowest software layers against
tampering and to ensure only Apple's trusted operating system software is
loaded at startup. The secure boot process initiates in unchangeable code,
known as the Boot ROM, which is embedded during Apple SoC fabrication
and is recognized as the hardware root of trust.
Furthermore, the Secure Enclave processes face and fingerprint data from
Face ID and Touch ID sensors in Apple devices. This enables secure
authentication while maintaining the privacy and security of user biometric
data. The Secure Enclave, an integral part of Apple's SoC, provides a robust,
isolated environment that prioritizes data security. It is critical for
penetration testing and exploit discovery within iOS applications. Its primary
function is to protect sensitive user information, even when the Application
Processor kernel is compromised.
To illustrate this, suppose an attacker is attempting to exploit a shared
resource vulnerability within an iOS application. If the target application
relies on Secure Enclave for critical operations, it will be unaffected as the

--- Page 180 ---
Secure Enclave Processor operates independently, blocking potential side-
channel exploits.
One must also know the Secure Enclave's secure, non-volatile storage
mechanism when conducting penetration testing. This feature provides a
dedicated I2C bus-connected storage device accessible only by the Secure
Enclave. An attacker attempting to extract user data encryption keys would
fail, as these keys are stored in entropy within this non-volatile storage,
inaccessible to other device components.
An important tool within the Secure Enclave's arsenal is the True Random
Number Generator (TRNG). This tool, utilized whenever generating
cryptographic keys, random key seeds, or other entropy, fortifies
cryptographic defenses against predictive or statistical attacks. As a
penetration tester, these defenses mandate discovering more complex or
novel exploits, as traditional cryptographic attacks prove ineffective.
Two vital components of the Secure Enclave, the AES Engine and the
Public Key Accelerator (PKA), handle symmetric and asymmetric
cryptography operations, respectively. These blocks, designed to resist
information leakage through techniques like Static Power Analysis (SPA)
or side-channel attacks, fortify the device's encryption mechanisms. A
successful attack, therefore, requires an understanding of these cryptographic
operations and their potential weaknesses.
Finally, devices with Face ID employ the Secure Neural Engine, which
translates 2D images and depth maps into a mathematical representation of a
user's face. Although very difficult to exploit, penetration testing of
applications using Face ID for authentication should involve probing the
security mechanisms of this Neural Engine. Attempts to manipulate the
authentication process by feeding false data could expose vulnerabilities and
allow for the development of protective patches.
App transport security
Finally, App Transport Security (ATS) enforces best practices for secure
network communication, mandating apps to connect to web services over
HTTPS, thereby encrypting all traffic between the app and servers. This
helps protect sensitive user data from being intercepted dring transit.

--- Page 181 ---
ATS functions as a key security feature in iOS applications. It provides
stringent security prerequisites for network connections, ensuring that apps
adhere to best practices for secure connections when using APIs such as
NSURLConnection, CFURL, or NSURLSession. When conducting
penetration testing and exploit discovery on iOS applications, it is crucial to
understand ATS's role in maintaining the security of network connections.
ATS enforces the use of ciphers that provide forward secrecy by default,
including ECDHE_ECDSA_AES and ECDHE_RSA_AES in Galois/Counter Mode
(GCM) and Cipher Block Chaining (CBC) mode. Forward secrecy ensures
that even if a session key is compromised, past session keys cannot be
determined, hence providing strong resistance against the decryption of
previously captured data.
For instance, a penetration tester may be attempting to exploit potential
weaknesses in the network communication of an iOS application. Without
forward secrecy, the tester could decrypt all previous data if they
compromised the session key. However, ATS's enforcement of forward
secrecy makes this scenario highly unlikely.
Moreover, ATS allows disabling the forward secrecy requirement per
domain, which adds RSA_AES to the available ciphers. While this introduces
flexibility, it can expose the application to risks if not managed
appropriately.
ATS mandates that servers support TLS 1.2 and forward secrecy. It also
enforces that certificates must be valid, signed with SHA256 or stronger, and
use a minimum 2048-bit RSA key or a 256-bit elliptic curve key. Network
connections that do not meet these stringent requirements will fail, except
when the app overrides ATS. Therefore, a penetration tester must check if
the application is bypassing ATS and if such action exposes the app to
potential security risks.
Furthermore, evaluating the trusted status of a TLS certificate is performed
according to industry standards set in RFC 5280 and includes new standards
like RFC 6962 (Certificate Transparency). With iOS 11, Apple devices
periodically update a list of revoked and constrained certificates. As a
penetration tester, you must verify if the application is correctly handling
certificate revocation, especially when a trust evaluation requires an Online

--- Page 182 ---
Certificate Status Response (OCSP), and the response is unavailable. In
such cases, the trust evaluation should fail, or it may expose the application
to security vulnerabilities.
iOS Vulnerabilities
However, despite the robust security architecture, iOS has vulnerabilities.
The unveiling and patching of these security gaps continue to bolster the
system's fortitude. Penetration testing and exploit discovery form the front
line of defense, helping to identify and address weaknesses before malicious
actors can exploit them. Let us discuss a couple of known iOS vulnerabilities
to highlight the importance of this process.
A notorious example is the Broadpwn vulnerability (CVE-2017-9417). This
flaw was located in the Wi-Fi chipset of certain iOS devices, potentially
allowing attackers within range to execute arbitrary code on the Wi-Fi chip.
A key lesson from this was the importance of keeping device firmware up-
to-date and the necessity for rigorous testing of all system components, not
just the core OS and applications.
Another significant vulnerability was the Safari Mail exploit (CVE-2020-
9784 and CVE-2020-9785), which allowed remote code execution after the
user opened a specially crafted email. This vulnerability exposed the risks
associated with input validation and processing untrusted data, highlighting
the need for secure coding practices and thorough application security
testing.
These are prime examples of how iOS, while robust and secure, can have
vulnerabilities. As penetration testers, the goal is to discover and understand
these potential weaknesses. Studying known vulnerabilities can provide
valuable insights into patterns of security issues, aiding in developing tests to
uncover similar flaws. This relentless pursuit of vulnerabilities is essential to
fortifying iOS applications, thereby maintaining the high standards of
security that iOS users expect.
Client-side vulnerabilities
To ensure the security of iOS applications, penetration testers focus on
various client-side vulnerabilities. Understanding these vulnerabilities and

--- Page 183 ---
knowing how to detect them forms the basis of our efforts to protect iOS
applications through proactive attack simulations.
One common vulnerability lies in insecure data storage. Sensitive data, such
as user credentials, Personal Identifiable Information (PII), and
encryption keys, often find their way into unprotected storage locations.
They could be stored in plaintext within SQLite databases, Plists, or even log
files, presenting a treasure trove for attackers if not properly secured.
In the quest for robust iOS application security, penetration testers and
security researchers must be acutely aware of the potential vulnerabilities in
various file types. These can become a repository of sensitive information
and, therefore, a prime target in a penetration testing exercise.
For instance, Property List (Plist) files are often employed to save
application configuration data and can sometimes be overlooked in security
audits, leading to accidental storage of sensitive data. Similarly, Core Data, a
framework offering diverse functionalities for data manipulation, often
utilizes SQLite databases, which can become a repository for sensitive
information if not managed with due caution.
YapDatabase, another SQLite-based storage solution, can also house critical
data. This also extends to Firebase, where misconfigurations can lead to
information leaks. Realm databases, while streamlining local storage and
data syncing, can become vulnerable if sensitive data is mishandled.
Other files to note include Couchbase Lite databases, which, like their
counterparts, can store sensitive information if improperly used. Binary
cookies, a mechanism for managing user session data, can similarly store
sensitive information. Cache data, which improves application performance
by storing data that can be re-used, is also a potential point of vulnerability.
As you assess the security of an iOS application, an important question to
address is whether the application allows the usage of custom keyboards.
Custom keyboards can pose a security risk, as they may capture and store
sensitive information input by the user in their cache files. This should be
checked thoroughly during any penetration test.
iOS applications frequently generate logs to aid in debugging and
troubleshooting. However, these logs can inadvertently expose sensitive

--- Page 184 ---
information if the application logs such data. An integral part of your
security testing should be scrutinizing these logs, checking whether they
contain any information an attacker could leverage.
The creation of backups can provide a potential entry point for attackers.
Stored backups often hold a wealth of sensitive information saved in the file
system, mirroring the file system data mentioned in the initial checklist.
Therefore, checking the security of backups is crucial. Moreover, backups
can be exploited differently: an attacker might alter some application
configurations within the backup. When this modified backup is restored to
the phone, the changed configuration may permit bypassing certain security
features.
An often-overlooked aspect is the iOS feature of automatic snapshots, which
could inadvertently capture and save sensitive visual information. Snapshots
are the images of running apps displayed when changing between apps
during multitasking. Lastly, the iOS Keychain, a secure storage component,
frequently stores sensitive data such as user passwords. Inadequate handling
can make this information retrievable even after the device is sold or
transferred.
Improper input validation is another prevalent issue that can lead to severe
consequences such as SQL Injection, buffer overflows, or XSS. An
application that does not appropriately validate or sanitize input data can
execute malicious commands or leak sensitive information.
This common flaw could result in severe consequences, including SQL
Injection, buffer overflows, or XSS. This is especially relevant when an
application interfaces with external systems, accepts user-generated content,
or utilizes web views.
Consider a situation where an iOS app integrates with a backend database
and blindly trusts user-supplied input. A malicious user could exploit this
trust by injecting SQL commands into user input fields, leading to SQL
Injection. This could allow an attacker to manipulate the database, leading to
data theft, alteration, or even deletion.
Similarly, buffer overflow vulnerabilities can arise when an application
accepts and processes input data without proper length validation, especially
in situations involving lower-level languages or legacy code within the app.

--- Page 185 ---
In such scenarios, an attacker can overflow the buffer's boundary and
overwrite adjacent memory, leading to arbitrary code execution.
XSS attacks, though less common in native iOS applications, can occur in
apps that utilize web views or HTML content rendering. Without adequate
sanitization, user-supplied input could include malicious JavaScript code
that runs within the context of the victim's session, leading to session
hijacking, sensitive data disclosure, or defacement of the app's content.
In each case, an iOS app that fails to appropriately validate or sanitize input
data is leaving a door open for an attacker. Consequently, it is critical during
penetration testing to thoroughly examine these potential weak spots,
ensuring robust validation and sanitization routines are in place to prevent
such vulnerabilities.
Insecure communication, whether Inter-Process Communication (IPC) or
over a network, is a vulnerability often exploited by attackers. iOS
applications often communicate with each other or with backend services.
These interactions can expose sensitive data or allow unauthorized actions if
they are not secured. This could involve insecure use of URL schemes or
unprotected network traffic that fails to use SSL/TLS correctly.
Custom URI Handlers, Deep links, or Custom Schemes are areas prone to
such vulnerabilities. In penetration testing, these aspects should be carefully
examined. Applications registering protocols or schemes require validation
checks to ensure they do not accept or leak sensitive information. For
example, if the app expects to receive certain information via a custom
scheme, another application could intercept this data by registering the same
scheme.
Similarly, Universal Links require cautious scrutiny. As they offer a way for
applications to link directly to specific content, checking the Apple-app-site
association file for potential flaws is crucial. The application must verify and
sanitize user input through these links to mitigate vulnerabilities.
UIActivity Sharing, UIPasteboard, and App Extensions are other areas
where data could be unintentionally exposed or mishandled. For instance,
monitoring the pasteboard may reveal if any sensitive data is being copied
inadvertently.

--- Page 186 ---
WebViews, due to their capability to render online content, can pose unique
challenges. Properties such as javaScriptEnabled,
JavaScriptCanOpenWindowsAutomatically, and hasOnlySecureContent should
be confirmed to be secure. Access to local files or native methods through
JavaScript should also be restricted.
Lastly, network communication demands rigorous testing. A Man-in-the-
Middle (MitM) attack can reveal potential vulnerabilities in web-based
communication. Furthermore, certificate pinning and hostname verification
should be correctly implemented to ensure secure SSL/TLS connections.
Testing toolkit for iOS
A pentester often employs a suite of tools to unearth these vulnerabilities.
Burp Suite, for example, can intercept and manipulate network requests to
test for vulnerabilities in an application's communication. Hopper can
disassemble and decompile an application, aiding in understanding its inner
workings and identifying potential security issues. Frida, meanwhile, is an
invaluable tool for dynamic analysis, enabling live manipulation of the
application's code to explore potential vulnerabilities.
Burp Suite, an established web application security staple, is an invaluable
instrument for penetration testing and exploit discovery within iOS
applications. This utility specializes in intercepting and dissecting network
traffic, offering a granular look into the communication dynamics between
an iOS application and its corresponding server.
Furnished with a suite of tools, Burp provides functionalities that range from
intercepting HTTP/S traffic and mapping the application's attack surface to
even automated vulnerability scanning. For instance, with Burp's Proxy tool,
a penetration tester can intercept and manipulate the HTTP/S requests and
responses passing between the iOS application and the server, thus enabling
real-time examination and modification of data. This could be especially
useful when sensitive data is transmitted without adequate encryption or in
clear text.
Furthermore, Burp Suite's Spider tool can assist in mapping out the
application's architecture by following and cataloging links within the app.
Its Intruder tool allows for automated attacks on identified input vectors,

--- Page 187 ---
which can be vital in brute force attacks or testing for SQL injection points.
Its Scanner functionality further augments the ability to discover common
vulnerabilities automatically.
Considering the above, Burp Suite's utility is not confined to mere
interception; it provides an extensive toolkit for comprehensive testing of
iOS applications. More detailed tutorials and guides about using Burp Suite
for iOS applications can be explored directly on PortSwigger's website, the
developers of Burp Suite.
Hopper emerges as an indispensable asset in the toolkit of iOS application
security, serving as a specialized reverse engineering utility for
disassembling and decompiling iOS apps. This tool is not merely an app
examiner; it is a powerful instrument that lets penetration testers dive deep
into the heart of an application, revealing the very fabric of its code structure
and internal operations.
At its core, Hopper offers functionalities like disassembly, control flow
graphing, and procedural decompilation. Its disassembler lets you break
down the application's compiled code into assembly language, enabling the
penetration tester to examine low-level operations. Hopper shines by
visualizing the control flow graph in complex applications where traditional
code review might be impractical. This aids in comprehending how various
parts of the code interact and execute.
Hopper's decompiler further illuminates the codebase by translating low-
level assembly code into higher-level languages such as Objective-C or
Swift. This aids security analysts in identifying potential weaknesses, such
as buffer overflows or insecure function calls, within the application's
underlying code.
An example scenario where Hopper might be deployed could involve an iOS
app with an encrypted or obfuscated section of code intended to thwart static
analysis. Using Hopper, a tester could disassemble the app, isolate the
encryption routine, and gain insight into the logic behind the encryption
mechanism, subsequently informing possible avenues of attack.
For a comprehensive understanding and mastery of Hopper, several online
tutorials and the official Hopper documentation provide further insights,
deep-diving into more advanced features and usage scenarios.

--- Page 188 ---
Our toolkit triad is Frida, a robust dynamic instrumentation toolkit that truly
flexes its muscles when inspecting and manipulating iOS applications during
execution. It is not a mere observer tool; Frida empowers security
professionals to pry into an application's internal operations in real-time and
dynamically alter its functionality while it runs.
At its core, Frida injects JavaScript snippets into native apps, permitting a
wide array of on-the-fly modifications. This mechanism allows penetration
testers to intercept function calls, modify parameter values, or replace entire
functions. In the landscape of iOS application security, this is particularly
valuable when faced with obfuscated code or anti-tampering mechanisms. It
allows penetration testers to skirt static defenses and probe the application's
behavior in a live environment.
Take, for instance, an iOS application with complex anti-tampering
protections that prevent static analysis. Using Frida, a penetration tester can
hook into critical functions at runtime and alter their return values, nullifying
anti-tampering checks. Alternatively, Frida could be used to investigate how
an app handles encryption by hooking the encryption function and revealing
the plaintext data before it is encrypted.
Frida's extensive capabilities and flexible architecture have made it a
favorite among penetration testers and reverse engineers. The tool has a
vibrant community and a plethora of resources, from the official Frida
documentation to myriad user-generated tutorials and guides, ready to assist
in diving deeper into its functionalities and use cases.
By combining these tools and a comprehensive understanding of common
iOS application vulnerabilities, readers can identify, exploit, and protect
applications from these client-side security issues.
Malicious iOS applications
Transitioning to a more intrusive penetration testing technique, we venture
into the world of payload injection into iOS applications, a craft that, when
mastered, could potentially rewrite an application's rules or offer illicit
access. This approach necessitates the modification of iOS App Store
Package (IPA) files, their subsequent repackaging, and the eventual
distribution of these altered versions. In the arsenal of a seasoned penetration

--- Page 189 ---
tester, tools like Cydia Impactor and MachOView are indispensable for such
endeavors.
Let us start with the task of dealing with IPA files. An IPA file is an archive
for iOS applications containing the app binary and other related resources. A
skilled tester might seek to modify this binary to inject a malicious payload
or alter the app's behavior. Cydia Impactor is useful in this realm, enabling
penetration testers to sideload these modified IPA files onto iOS devices. Its
significance stems from its ability to bypass the usual App Store-only
installation process, offering a direct way to deploy custom, potentially
altered apps onto iOS devices.
This tactic is a particularly potent cyber-attack, wherein an attacker
manipulates the application's behavior or even gains unauthorized access to
its functionalities or data. To perform this attack, one must have a solid
understanding of the iOS application structure, especially the IPA files, the
deployment units for iOS apps. One might consider studying the structure of
IPA files from Apple's developer documentation to better understand their
composition.
Modifying an IPA file to inject malicious code requires careful manipulation.
The attacker essentially unpacks the original IPA file, introduces the
malicious payload into the application's binary or scripting files, and then
repacks it into a new IPA file. Throughout this process, attention must be
paid to maintaining the app's normal functionalities to avoid arousing user
suspicion. You can find further reading on binary exploitation and payload
injection in various cybersecurity resources, such as online blogs, forums, or
academic papers on mobile application security.
The modified IPA file is then ready for distribution. There are several
methods for distributing these maliciously modified apps, ranging from
phishing emails suggesting an app update to uploading the app to unofficial
app stores. Understanding these methods can provide insights into the real-
world application of these attacks and help devise strategies to avoid falling
victim to them.
One of the key tools in this realm is Cydia Impactor, which allows for
installing IPA files onto iOS devices without needing an official App Store.
This application has been widely used for sideloading unofficial apps onto

--- Page 190 ---
iOS devices, thus demonstrating its potency in this type of attack. Cydia
Impactor's official documentation and user community can be a great
resource for learning more about its operation and capabilities.
For example, imagine an app that validates user credentials on the client
side, a design flaw ripe for exploitation. Using Cydia Impactor, one could
inject a payload into the IPA file that bypasses this check, allowing
unauthorized access.
Then there is MachOView, a highly specialized tool that deepens into Mach-
O files: the standard format for executables, object code, shared libraries,
and dynamically loaded code in iOS and macOS. It facilitates the inspection
and modification of binary structures in Mach-O files, enabling a tester to
scrutinize the binary's structure and identify potential vulnerabilities or
points of injection.
For instance, let us consider an iOS app that employs a proprietary
encryption algorithm for sensitive data. Using MachOView, a penetration
tester could dissect the app's binary, reveal the algorithm's implementation,
and potentially find a weak spot to exploit.
While these tools facilitate powerful manipulation, they also underline the
importance of a robust defense strategy for iOS applications. The ability to
modify an app's behavior underscores the need for secure coding practices,
robust encryption, and server-side checks to guard against these attacks.
Remember, we are using these tactics to fortify our defenses, turning the
hacker's tools against them in our quest for the most secure application
environment possible.
Network interception
The chapter expands into an illuminating discussion on interception and
modification of network traffic, often accomplished through MITM attacks.
This form of cyber assault is complex, as it involves the attacker positioning
themselves between the communication of two parties, secretly relaying and
possibly altering the communication. This way, the attacker can snoop on,
manipulate, or interrupt the communication. To gain a deeper understanding
of this kind of attack, readers may want to refer to resources on network
security and encryption protocols.

--- Page 191 ---
When discussing the intricate landscape of MITM attacks in the context of
iOS application security, the conversation would be incomplete without
addressing the significance of proxy tools, particularly the industry standard:
Burp Suite. This versatile tool empowers penetration testers by enabling
detailed interception and scrutiny of network traffic, bridging the divide
between an iOS application and its corresponding server.
With Burp Suite, testers gain a unique vantage point, being able to capture,
monitor, and manipulate data as it transits between the app and server. This
makes it an invaluable tool for dissecting the application's network
communication and unearthing potential areas of vulnerability.
Consider, for example, an iOS app that communicates with its backend
server to authenticate users or process sensitive data. With Burp Suite, a
penetration tester could intercept this traffic and closely inspect the requests
and responses for any weak points, such as weak encryption, poorly
implemented authentication, or even clear-text transmission of sensitive
data.
However, Burp Suite's prowess extends beyond simple interception. Its
ability to map an application's entire data flow provides an even deeper
insight. By examining this flow, testers can identify how data is managed
within the application and detect any potentially hazardous routes that could
be exploited.
For those seeking to wield the power of Burp Suite in their security testing
endeavors, the tool's official documentation serves as a comprehensive
guide, covering everything from the basics to more advanced operations.
Furthermore, online platforms dedicated to cybersecurity education
frequently provide in-depth tutorials on Burp Suite's application in real-
world scenarios, offering tangible, applicable knowledge for penetration
testing and exploit discovery in iOS applications.
However, Burp Suite is just one side of the coin. An attacker must often
overcome encrypted communications using SSL/TLS protocols to perform a
successful MITM attack. Aiding in the execution of successful MITM
attacks, SSLyze comes into play when a penetration tester encounters the
challenge of encrypted SSL/TLS protocols.

--- Page 192 ---
SSLyze performs comprehensive scans of a server's SSL/TLS configuration.
Think of it as a fine-tooth comb for encrypted network communication,
empowering users to scrutinize the server's cryptographic posture
meticulously.
For instance, in an iOS application context, consider a scenario where an app
employs SSL/TLS encryption for secure data transmission between the
client and the server. By leveraging SSLyze, a penetration tester can conduct
a detailed scan of the server's SSL/TLS configuration. This can help reveal
potential vulnerabilities such as weak or misconfigured encryption ciphers,
outdated protocols, or even flaws in certificate validation.
Such vulnerabilities could potentially allow a penetration tester to breach the
encrypted communication, either by downgrading the encryption strength or
exploiting a flaw in the protocol itself. This could lead to a successful MITM
attack, wherein the encrypted data could be intercepted, deciphered, and
manipulated.
SSLyze is not merely a scanning tool; it is a potent weapon that assists in
dissecting and evaluating the encrypted communications within an iOS
application's architecture. The official SSLyze documentation serves as an
in-depth guide for those keen on harnessing its capabilities.
In the intricate realm of iOS application security, sometimes deeper barriers
like certificate pinning must be overcome before achieving meaningful
penetration testing or exploit discovery. Certificate pinning signifies
heightened security, where an application only acknowledges traffic bearing
the precise certificate issued by the original developer. This means
applications with certificate pinning essentially barricade themselves against
communication unless this certification is used to authenticate the
correspondence.
This roadblock poses an intricate challenge for tools like Burp Suite, which
necessitates the installation of a custom certificate to intercept traffic. How
can we navigate this hurdle?
A rigorous journey through the labyrinth of the application's code is often
needed to defeat certificate pinning. We embark on a reverse engineering
process to uncover the mechanism behind the certificate pinning logic and

--- Page 193 ---
devise a strategy to bypass it. Once we have a way around it, we recompile
the modified version of the application and then conduct our security testing.
In the context of penetration testing and bug bounty programs, it is common
for developers to provide a version of the application without certificate
pinning, easing the process for testers. However, that is not a universal
practice. When such a version is not provided, penetration testers must flex
their reverse engineering muscles, relying on tools like Hopper or IDA for
disassembly and then using code patching or hooking techniques with tools
like Frida to subvert the pinning controls.
Conclusion
This chapter has taken you on a thorough exploration of iOS application
security, starting with an understanding of the iOS architecture and inherent
security models, followed by a study of its common vulnerabilities. We have
unveiled various attack techniques against iOS applications, discussed tools
like Burp Suite, Hopper, and Frida, and revealed methods to overcome
encryption using tools like SSLyze. Through our attack to protect
philosophy, we have equipped you with the knowledge to identify and
rectify vulnerabilities, enhancing security for all iOS users. Next, we will
transition our focus from platform-specific vulnerabilities to mobile web
applications in our upcoming chapter.
Points to remember
iOS history: Recall key milestones in the evolution of iOS.
iOS security mechanisms: Understand iOS's security features,
including sandboxing, code signing, hardware-based security, and App
Transport Security.
Client-side vulnerabilities: Be aware of common client-side
vulnerabilities in iOS.
Testing toolkit for iOS: Familiarize with essential iOS testing tools
like Burp Suite for network analysis, Frida for dynamic analysis,
Hopper for reverse engineering, MachOView for binary inspection,
and Cydia Impactor for application installation.

--- Page 194 ---
Vulnerability exploitation: Recognize the methods for identifying
and exploiting vulnerabilities in iOS applications.
Network interception: Understand the techniques and tools for
intercepting network communications in iOS.
Risks and countermeasures: Be cognizant of the risks associated
with iOS penetration testing and the countermeasures to mitigate
them.

contribute to application security?

of iOS devices?
