CHAPTER 6
Mobile Device Penetration Testing
for Web Applications
Introduction
Embarking on the intricate journey of mobile device penetration testing, this
chapter addresses the unique challenges presented by diverse operating
systems, varied device configurations, and fluctuating network conditions.
We delve into the nuances of operating systems like Android and iOS, each
with distinct security features and vulnerabilities, from Android's open-
source transparency to iOS's closed yet potentially exploitable ecosystem.
The exploration extends to device configurations, where simple
misconfigurations can lead to significant security risks and the impact of
varying network conditions, such as the vulnerability shifts between secure
and public networks. Addressing critical vulnerabilities like SQL injections,
the chapter emphasizes rigorous testing methods, including input validation,
to reinforce application security. This journey transcends mere vulnerability
identification, aiming to fortify mobile applications, enhance overall
ecosystem security, and adopt an attacking to protect approach, ensuring a
comprehensive understanding of both static and dynamic analysis and
behavioral testing in mobile device penetration testing.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

--- Page 196 ---
Mobile device considerations
Explanation of vulnerabilities
Vulnerability details
APIs
API testing
Insecure data handling
Verbose error messages
Tools
Objectives
In this chapter, we focus on securing mobile applications by addressing
challenges arising from diverse device types, operating systems, and
network conditions. Our primary aim is to dissect the complexities of
Android and iOS, identifying vulnerabilities and developing strategies to
counter them. We also focus on device configurations, pinpointing security
risks due to improper access controls and data leaks. Additionally, we
examine the effects of changing network conditions on mobile applications,
identifying potential threats. Using examples from minor misconfigurations
to critical SQL injections, we demonstrate the significance of each
vulnerability, adhering to the philosophy of attacking to protect, where
identifying vulnerabilities leads to enhanced mobile security. The chapter
further delves into practical applications of this knowledge, utilizing mobile
device emulators like Android Virtual Device (AVD) Manager and iOS
Simulator to replicate diverse environments and uncover specific
vulnerabilities. Tools like Burp Suite and OWASP ZAP are highlighted for
their ability to intercept and manipulate HTTP/HTTPS traffic, aiding in
identifying and preventing server vulnerabilities like SQL Injection or XSS.
This comprehensive approach ensures a thorough understanding of mobile
application security, equipping readers to effectively test and fortify mobile
applications against a range of threats.
Mobile device considerations

--- Page 197 ---
The world of mobile application penetration testing unfolds a variety of
unique challenges, each requiring a different set of strategies and solutions.
Unlike traditional web or desktop application testing, the diversity in mobile
environments greatly expands the testing parameters we must consider,
revealing a richer but more complex attack surface.
Take, for instance, the extensive range of mobile devices in the market.
From numerous manufacturers and countless models to varying hardware
configurations, this expansive diversity significantly magnifies the number
of variables that come into play during testing. These variables can
drastically impact an application's behavior and the type of vulnerabilities it
may harbor.
Consider screen size as an example. It is not just a matter of aesthetics or
functionality. When a mobile application is developed to fit a myriad of
screen sizes, it can lead to design compromises, which can, in turn, introduce
security loopholes. An improperly handled UI change, such as an orientation
switch from portrait to landscape, could inadvertently expose sensitive
information that would otherwise be hidden.
Hardware capabilities, another key variable in mobile devices, also impact
the potential vulnerabilities in an application. Take Near Field
Communication (NFC) as an example. As a hardware feature that enables
wireless data exchange over short distances, NFC introduces a new set of
potential attack vectors. A mobile app using NFC for sensitive operations
like payment transfers could potentially be exploited if not properly secured.
Network conditions also add another layer to the complexity of mobile
penetration testing. Mobile devices constantly switch between different
network conditions: 3G, 4G, 5G, and Wi-Fi, each with its unique set of
security concerns. For example, a mobile application might function
perfectly fine in a high-speed network environment but could exhibit
information leakage or other vulnerabilities under slower network conditions
due to timeouts or data synchronization issues.
Moreover, understanding the intricacies of mobile OS platforms, primarily
iOS and Android, is critical. Each comes with its unique architectural
principles, security features, and, therefore, potential weaknesses. For

--- Page 198 ---
example, an insecure data storage vulnerability might appear in Android
applications due to improper use of shared preferences or SQLite databases.
Therefore, mobile application testing is not a one-size-fits-all approach. It
requires a deep understanding of the diversity and complexity inherent in the
mobile ecosystem. By understanding these unique aspects, we can
effectively attack to protect, discover, and patch vulnerabilities before they
can be exploited.
Screen size variations among mobile devices serve as more than just display
factors; they can substantially impact mobile application security. The
dynamic nature of screen sizes and orientations can potentially introduce
unique user interface vulnerabilities and information presentation
vulnerabilities. For instance, a banking app might unintentionally expose
confidential account details when the screen rotates or adjusts to fit a smaller
display. Thus, proper UI implementation and testing for diverse screen sizes
become integral to mobile web penetration testing.
Different hardware capabilities, another aspect of mobile device diversity,
can significantly impact the security landscape. These could range from
NFC and Bluetooth capabilities to biometric sensors. For instance, an e-
commerce application might use NFC for contactless payments. If the NFC
transactions are not securely implemented or the data is not properly
encrypted, an attacker might exploit this, leading to unauthorized
transactions or data leakage. Therefore, understanding and evaluating how
an application utilizes hardware features is vital in mobile penetration
testing.
Switching gears to network conditions, we see that variations in network
speeds and reliability can expose a distinct set of vulnerabilities. Suppose a
mobile application does not handle network transitions well, such as shifting
from a 4G to a weaker 3G network. In that case, it may give rise to
vulnerabilities like data leakage, session handling errors, or even denial of
service. Such vulnerabilities could be exploited by an attacker when the
application operates in suboptimal network conditions. Thus, rigorous
network testing becomes a crucial facet of mobile penetration testing.
Given the vast range of mobile devices, physical testing across all device
configurations quickly becomes impractical and unfeasible. This is where

--- Page 199 ---
emulation tools provide immense value. Emulators like the AVD Manager
and the iOS Simulator offer a practical solution by simulating various
devices and environments. Using AVD Manager, we can create and manage
virtual Android devices with different screen sizes, memory capacities, and
Android API levels. Similarly, the iOS Simulator allows testing across
different iPhone and iPad variants.
For instance, suppose we want to test how an application behaves on a low-
memory Android device running an older version of Android. We can
configure a virtual device in AVD Manager to match these specifications and
run our tests accordingly. On the other hand, if we need to check the app's
compatibility with the latest iPhone model's screen size, the iOS Simulator is
the tool.
Hence, in the grand scheme of mobile penetration testing, emulators like
AVD Manager and iOS Simulator are not just optional tools but
indispensable allies that allow us to scale our testing efforts effectively
across the vast mobile landscape. These tools and the technical
understanding of screen sizes, hardware capabilities, and network conditions
provide us with a robust and practical approach to secure mobile
applications.
The terrain of mobile penetration testing becomes even more intricate when
we consider the aspect of browser diversity. Every mobile device could
potentially host multiple browsers, each with its unique implementation,
performance characteristics, and, most importantly, security measures. This
variability in browser functionality could present different interactions and
reactions with the same web application, potentially leading to distinct
vulnerabilities across different browsers.
Take, for instance, the case of XSS attacks, a common threat in web
application security. XSS vulnerabilities occur when an application includes
untrusted data in a new web page without proper validation or escaping,
allowing an attacker to execute scripts in the victim's browser. How a
particular browser handles JavaScript, a common tool in XSS attacks, can
drastically affect an application's susceptibility to such threats.
For example, consider an application that does not adequately sanitize user
input and allows embedding JavaScript code. A user using a more secure

--- Page 200 ---
browser might remain unscathed due to the browser's built-in protections
against XSS, such as Content Security Policy (CSP) or the sandboxing of
iframes. However, another user accessing the same application through a
different browser with less stringent security mechanisms could fall prey to
an XSS attack.
To account for these disparities in browser security measures and to ensure
comprehensive testing, we leverage cross-browser testing tools like
BrowserStack and Sauce Labs. BrowserStack provides an instant access
platform that allows testing across 2000+ real devices and browsers, making
it an invaluable tool in our arsenal. For instance, when we encounter a
potential XSS vulnerability, we could use BrowserStack to assess its impact
across a broad spectrum of browsers, from Safari and Chrome to Firefox and
Internet Explorer.
On the other hand, Sauce Labs offers a similar cross-browser testing
environment but focuses on continuous testing and integration. It is
particularly handy when dealing with complex web applications that require
regular updates and testing. We can automate our test scripts and run them
concurrently across different browser environments, speeding up the testing
process and helping us discover potential exploits quickly.
Both BrowserStack and Sauce Labs are potent allies in mobile web
penetration testing, helping us to account for the sheer diversity of browser
functionalities. By employing these tools effectively, we can ensure that our
web applications are robust against potential exploits, irrespective of the
browser they are accessed from. Exploring browser-dependent
vulnerabilities and utilizing such testing tools exemplify the practical and
expansive approach required in mobile web penetration testing.
We also need to account for the intricacies of various operating systems.
Mobile operating systems like Android and iOS handle certain security
aspects differently. For instance, the same WebView implementation might
have different vulnerabilities on Android versus iOS due to differences in
how each OS handles WebViews. Understanding these differences is crucial
for effective penetration testing, which is why we will dive deeper into these
topics in this chapter.
Explanation of vulnerabilities

--- Page 201 ---
In our journey through the landscape of mobile application security, we stop
to examine common vulnerabilities in web applications, issues like SQL
injection, XSS, and Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) that continue to
plague applications across platforms. While seemingly well-known, these
vulnerabilities are not always fully understood, especially in the context of
mobile web applications. That is why we dedicate a substantial portion of
this chapter to dissecting these vulnerabilities, their root causes, their
potential impacts, and, most importantly, how they can be identified and
mitigated in the mobile environment.
For instance, SQL injection, a vulnerability as old as SQL itself, persists
even in the most modern mobile web applications. An attacker exploiting
this vulnerability could manipulate SQL queries by injecting malicious SQL
code through unprotected input fields, potentially leading to unauthorized
access, data loss, or even entire system compromise. We will delve into
practical demonstrations, using tools like sqlmap, to identify and exploit
SQL injection vulnerabilities in mobile web applications, providing a hands-
on understanding of their risks.
Next, we confront the pervasive threat of XSS. In a mobile context, XSS
vulnerabilities may arise when a web application accepts untrusted input that
is subsequently rendered without proper sanitization in the mobile browser,
resulting in the execution of malicious scripts.
CSRF is another prevalent vulnerability we will scrutinize. CSRF attacks
force the victim's browser to request a site where they are authenticated,
potentially leading to unintended actions. While CSRF is often associated
with traditional web applications, it is also relevant in a mobile context,
especially in hybrid applications that use web views.
Our exploration, however, goes beyond these general vulnerabilities. We
venture into the less-traversed terrain of mobile-centric vulnerabilities, such
as insecure WebView implementation and inappropriate handling of deep
links. These issues, while perhaps less known, have severe implications for
mobile security.
For instance, insecure WebView implementations can lead to an array of
security issues, including JavaScript injection or file manipulation. Similarly,
improper handling of deep links, a common feature in mobile applications

--- Page 202 ---
for integrating with other applications or websites, can result in unauthorized
access to sensitive functionality or data.
By focusing on general and mobile-specific vulnerabilities, this chapter aims
to provide a comprehensive understanding of the security landscape of
mobile web applications. Armed with this knowledge, you can effectively
identify and patch these vulnerabilities, bolstering the defenses of your
mobile web applications.
Vulnerability details
In the case of SQL injection, this vulnerability comes into play when an
application's security mechanisms fail to validate input in SQL queries
effectively. Attackers could exploit this loophole to manipulate the SQL
query, leading to unauthorized access to sensitive data or, in worst-case
scenarios, complete control over the application's database. In the mobile
landscape, especially when dealing with mobile web applications, these
injections can be particularly damaging, given the increasing amount of
sensitive data stored on mobile devices.
SQL Injection is a common web application vulnerability that can have
catastrophic effects when successfully exploited. Armed with knowledge of
the database structure, an attacker can manipulate input fields to run
malicious SQL queries, potentially gaining unauthorized access to sensitive
data. We have a login form in a mobile web application that fails to sanitize
user input properly. An attacker can input SQL commands in the username
or password fields, which the underlying database interprets as legitimate
commands. For instance, an entry such as OR 1=1 might grant the attacker
access to a user's account, bypassing the login mechanism altogether. This is
a simple yet powerful example of how a seemingly innocuous input field can
become a gateway for unauthorized data access.
XSS represents another common yet harmful vulnerability. Here, an attacker
could inject malicious scripts into trusted websites, which are then executed
by the victim's browser. These scripts could steal user data, manipulate web
content, or perform any action that the user could perform. XSS
vulnerabilities could manifest in mobile web applications and potentially
have even more severe implications due to the personal nature of mobile
devices.

--- Page 203 ---
XSS allows an attacker to inject malicious scripts into web pages viewed by
other users. Let us consider a mobile web application with a comment
section where users can post their thoughts. If this application does not
sanitize the user input properly, an attacker can post a comment embedding a
JavaScript code. When other users view this comment, the embedded script
executes within their browsers, possibly leading to session hijacking or data
theft. This paints a vivid picture of how a benign feature like a comment
section can be weaponized in an XSS attack.
CSRF, another prevalent threat in web applications, is also a vital topic of
our exploration. CSRF attacks occur when an attacker tricks a victim into
performing actions on their behalf on a web application where the victim is
authenticated. Like XSS, CSRF can have severe implications in mobile web
applications, especially those that maintain sessions or use cookies for
authenticated users.
Imagine a scenario where a user is logged into a mobile banking app.
Simultaneously, the user opens a malicious email and clicks on an embedded
link. This link could trigger a CSRF attack that initiates a transaction on the
banking app without the user's knowledge. The CSRF attack manipulates the
trust that a site has in a user's browser, forcing the user to perform actions
they did not intend to, which, in this case, could lead to financial loss.
While understanding general web application vulnerabilities is crucial, our
expedition into mobile penetration testing would not be complete without
examining the vulnerabilities unique to mobile environments. We will delve
into mobile-specific weaknesses such as insecure WebView implementation
and improperly handling deep links. Although these vulnerabilities might not
make the headlines as often as their more general counterparts, they carry
substantial security implications, particularly in mobile web applications.
WebView, an integral component of both Android and iOS environments,
allows mobile applications to display web content within the application. It
essentially creates a bridge between the native application and the web
interface. In an insecure WebView implementation, the lines between the app
and the web become blurred, leaving the door open for attackers to execute
arbitrary code within the app. This could lead to a range of security
incidents, from data theft to the complete takeover of user accounts. While

--- Page 204 ---
WebViews can benefit developers due to their flexibility, they can be
vulnerable if not properly secured.
Let us consider an insecure WebView implementation. Picture a mobile app
that uses a WebView to display third-party content. The app, due to an
insecure implementation, allows JavaScript execution within the WebView.
An attacker could lure the user to a malicious site that executes JavaScript
code when loaded in the WebView, leading to sensitive information leakage
or other adverse effects.
It is like embedding a full-fledged browser inside the application, facilitating
a seamless blend of web and native content. However, this blending comes
with security considerations, as the boundary between the application and
the web gets blurred.
Consider Android, where a WebView runs with the same permissions as the
host application. In a vulnerable setup, JavaScript running in a WebView
might have the same access to the Android API as the application, creating a
substantial attack surface. For instance, a malicious website loaded in the
WebView could exploit JavaScript interfaces to access native device
functionalities, leading to potential exploits like unauthorized file access or
code execution.
In contrast, iOS's WebView (WKWebView) maintains a stricter separation
between the application and the web content, limiting the attack surface.
However, it is not without its vulnerabilities. An ill-configured WebView
might allow the execution of arbitrary JavaScript in the context of the host
application, leading to potential attacks like XXS or JavaScript injection.
Understanding these differences and their security implications is not just an
academic exercise. It directly influences how we conduct our penetration
tests. When testing an Android application, we might focus more on the
interfaces exposed to JavaScript in WebViews and any insecure
implementation of JavaScript interfaces. On the other hand, while examining
an iOS application, we would pay more attention to whether any user-
influenced data is being passed to the WebView without proper sanitization.
Equipped with this knowledge and the right tools, such as Drozer for
Android and Frida for iOS, we can conduct an exhaustive investigation of
potential WebView vulnerabilities. Drozer allows us to interact with the

--- Page 205 ---
Android device at the same level as the application, offering a platform to
explore potential exploits. Frida, in contrast, offers dynamic instrumentation,
letting us inject our scripts into the running processes and examine how they
behave.
This deep dive into the intricacies of WebView implementations across
Android and iOS typifies the detailed and nuanced approach required for
successful mobile web penetration testing. The key lies in knowing where to
look and understanding why we look there, empowering us to uncover,
patch, and defend against potential exploits effectively.
Deep links, a common feature in mobile apps, enable a seamless user
experience by allowing navigation to specific content within an app from a
website or another app. However, if not handled correctly, they can turn into
gateways that lead attackers straight to sensitive data or functionalities. For
example, an attacker could trick a user into clicking a malicious deep link
that directs the user's app to perform unintended actions, such as sharing
sensitive data. It is a vulnerability often overlooked yet has significant
consequences for mobile security.
Consider an app with a deep link allowing users to reset their passwords. If
an attacker can trick a user into clicking a manipulated deep link, the user
might end up unknowingly resetting their password and sending the new
password directly to the attacker.
APIs
APIs, the invisible orchestrators of data flow in mobile applications, are an
oft-overlooked bastion of potential security flaws. They act as
intermediaries, linking the front-facing mobile application with the data-rich
server backend. Yet, despite their importance, APIs can sometimes be an
overlooked actor in mobile security assessments, and this omission can
result in far-reaching security implications. Think of APIs as rules defining
how different software components should interact, enabling seamless data
exchange and function invocation. Despite their integral role, APIs are
frequently overlooked in security assessments, a neglect that could
potentially open Pandora's box of cybersecurity threats.

--- Page 206 ---
Understanding the communication facilitated by APIs is pivotal. Mobile
applications often use REST or SOAP APIs to interact with a server for tasks
like fetching data, posting updates, or deleting information. Let us consider a
social media application: it uses APIs for multiple functionalities like
fetching the user's news feed, posting a status update, or even changing
profile settings. The data is sent to the API, which then interacts with the
backend server to execute the requested task and return a response.
However, herein lies a potential security loophole. If an API is not
sufficiently secure, an attacker could manipulate the API requests or
responses, causing undesirable outcomes. For instance, a poorly
implemented API might not correctly validate the inputs it receives, leading
to injection attacks. Similarly, an API that does not adequately check user
authentication could be vulnerable to unauthorized access or data exposure.
Figure 6.1: APIs often expose sensitive information inadvertently

--- Page 207 ---
Consider an example of a SQL injection vulnerability in an API to bring this
to life. Suppose a mobile application uses an API to search for user profiles
based on usernames. The application sends the entered username to the API,
which, in turn, queries the database. What if an attacker enters a SQL
statement instead of a username? If the API blindly appends this input to the
SQL query, the database might execute the attacker's SQL command,
potentially leading to data leakage or even data manipulation.
Testing APIs for such vulnerabilities, therefore, is an essential part of mobile
web application security. Tools like Postman or SoapUI could be used to
send custom API requests and analyze responses. For example, to test for
SQL injection, we could send a request with an SQL command as an input to
the API. If the API returns an error message indicating a SQL syntax
problem or returns data that it should not have, that is a red flag.
The security of APIs also hinges on their ability to manage user
authentication and access control efficiently. JWT tokens are commonly
used for authentication in APIs, assigned to the user upon login, and
included in subsequent requests to verify the user's identity. Testing these
mechanisms involves trying to access API endpoints without a token or with
an incorrect or expired token to see if the API correctly restricts access.
Furthermore, APIs should handle user roles and permissions effectively. An
administrative user might have privileges that a regular user does not have.
Testing whether the API appropriately checks for the user's role before
allowing access to such privileged operations is crucial. This could be done
by attempting to access administrative functions with a non-admin user's
credentials.
Through careful exploration, meticulous testing, and leveraging the right
tools, we can help ensure APIs' robust security, making our mobile
applications resilient to cyber threats. APIs might be the silent conductors of
an application's symphony, but they must take center stage in our quest for
cybersecurity. This journey takes us to the heart of API vulnerabilities, not to
exploit them for harm but to discover and rectify them before an adversary
can. In this sense, we are on the offense for a purely defensive cause:
protecting our applications from cyber threats.

--- Page 208 ---
Consider a mobile application utilizing an API for user registration and
login. Each time a user registers or logs in, the application interacts with the
API, sending and receiving data. A crucial step in securing this process is
carefully testing these API endpoints. This involves assessing whether the
data sent to the API is appropriately sanitized and validated and whether the
API responds as expected when it receives unusual or malicious inputs.
One way to test this is through the manipulation of API requests. For
instance, we can utilize Postman to send custom API requests and study the
responses. Suppose we input an unusually long string into the username field
during registration. If the API is not adequately equipped to handle such
inputs, the server might return an error or crash, indicating a potential buffer
overflow vulnerability. Moreover, we could also attempt to input special
characters or SQL statements, which might indicate susceptibility to an
Injection attack if processed by the API.
Authentication and authorization are another pair of essential factors in API
security. APIs often handle sensitive user information and must ensure that
only authenticated and authorized requests have access. For instance, an API
might implement token-based authentication, where a user is assigned a
unique token upon login, which must be included in subsequent requests to
authenticate the user. If a mobile application reveals this token, or if the API
fails to validate it correctly, an attacker might hijack a user's session.
An API might also handle various user roles with different permissions. An
API request that modifies sensitive user data should only be accessible by a
user with appropriate permissions. Suppose an unauthorized user could
access and modify data by manipulating the API request. In that case, it
clearly indicates a broken access control vulnerability.
Of course, we must be vigilant of insecure data handling in APIs. An API
should only return the data necessary for the application's function and no
more. Overly verbose API responses could leak sensitive data. For instance,
using Rest-Assured or SoapUI, we could test this by sending requests to the
API and scrutinizing the responses. An API response containing unnecessary
data, such as other users' information or server details, suggests a data
leakage issue.

--- Page 209 ---
APIs, the vital communication conduits in a digital landscape, harbor
potential risks that must be diligently navigated and mitigated. Although
generally under-protected, these protocols are integral to the smooth
functioning of mobile applications and, hence, are crucial areas of interest in
web penetration testing.
The secure configuration of API endpoints stands paramount in this
endeavor. API endpoints are specific URI paths where the API can access
resources, acting as gateways for data transfer. In a mobile application, for
example, an API endpoint might be used to fetch a user's profile data. These
endpoints must be shielded from unauthorized access, and various defensive
strategies are employed.
The bedrock of this defensive architecture is strong authentication and robust
authorization. These are the gatekeepers ensuring that only legitimate users
can access the API. Authentication verifies the user's identity, while
authorization checks whether a user has the necessary permissions to
perform a requested operation. One prevalent way of implementing
authentication is through JSON Web Tokens (JWTs). When a user logs in,
the server generates a JWT containing information that verifies the user's
identity. This token is then sent in the header of subsequent API requests,
confirming the user's identity.
However, it is not enough to ensure the user is who they claim to be; we
must also ensure they have the appropriate permissions to perform the
requested operation. This is where Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
comes into play. For instance, consider a banking application. A regular user
can view their balance and make transactions, but they should not be able to
access the functionality reserved for administrators, such as creating new
bank accounts. A secure API must, therefore, verify the user's identity and
role before permitting access to specific operations.
Input validation is another defensive measure to safeguard against malicious
data injection. Suppose an API endpoint accepts a user ID as a parameter and
returns the user's profile information. A secure API must ensure that the user
ID is of the correct type and format before processing it. If an attacker
attempts to send a SQL statement instead of a valid user ID (a potential SQL
Injection attack), a securely configured API should reject this input. Using

--- Page 210 ---
tools like OWASP's ZAP, we can test how an API responds to such
malicious inputs, giving us insights into potential vulnerabilities.
Rate limiting is yet another crucial layer of defense. This practice involves
limiting the requests a user or an IP address can make within a specific
timeframe. Without rate limiting, an API becomes vulnerable to brute force
attacks, where an attacker makes rapid, consecutive attempts to guess a
user's password. By limiting the number of attempts that can be made, the
API can effectively shield itself against such threats.
API testing
Interception and modification of API requests are a cornerstone in the web
penetration testing process, especially in the mobile landscape. It is a
practice that empowers us to observe and manipulate the data transmitted
between the client application and the server, thereby shining a light on
potential soft spots in how the application treats data.
As an initial step, we explore the interception of API requests. Imagine a
mobile app sending a request to fetch a user's profile data from a server. We
position ourselves in the middle of this client-server communication channel
during interception. By using a tool like Burp Suite, we can capture and
scrutinize the API requests made by the mobile application. This allows us to
understand the data being sent, its transmitted format, and security measures
in place.
Examining the requests, we can ascertain if they are being transmitted
securely. One key attribute to look for is encryption. Sensitive data, such as
login credentials or personal information, should be sent over HTTPS, not
HTTP. This ensures the data is encrypted during transit and cannot be easily
eavesdropped. Using Burp Suite's ability to intercept requests, we can
identify whether the requests are sent over an encrypted protocol.
Additionally, the interception process allows us to verify the proper
implementation of security headers. Headers like Content-Security-Policy
(CSP), HTTP Strict Transport Security (HSTS), and X-Content-Type-
Options help prevent attacks such as XXS and clickjacking and ensure that
communication happens over HTTPS. By checking the API response

--- Page 211 ---
headers in the intercepted data, we can verify if these protective measures
are in place.
The second half of our journey delves into the modification of API requests.
With intercepted data at our fingertips, we can alter it before sending it back
to the server. For instance, we could manipulate authorization tokens,
parameters, or endpoint URLs to observe how the server reacts. If, for
example, changing the user ID in a request allows us to access another user's
data, we have found an access control vulnerability.
Modifying requests is not just limited to tweaking parameters. We can also
test how the server handles malicious payloads. We can probe for
vulnerabilities like XXS or SQL injection by injecting scripts or SQL
commands into the request.
Finally, we consider the server's response to our manipulations. If the server
accepts the manipulated request and returns sensitive data, it is clear that the
application has security issues that need patching. On the other hand, if the
server rejects the request or responds with an error, it indicates a level of
robustness against the tested vulnerability.
Insecure data handling
Insecure data handling is a common yet often overlooked pitfall that could
lead to serious breaches in API security. It occurs when sensitive data is not
properly protected during its lifecycle within the application. This could
mean the data is sent over an unencrypted connection, inadequately stored,
or inadvertently leaked through detailed error messages. Through the lens of
mobile web penetration testing, let us unearth these oversights and
understand how to shield applications from such threats.
In mobile applications, sensitive data transmission occurs regularly, be it
login credentials, payment details, or personal user information. Such data
must be sent over secure, encrypted connections. Imagine a scenario where a
mobile banking app sends account details over an unencrypted HTTP
connection. A malevolent actor in the middle could easily eavesdrop on the
communication, capturing sensitive data. A tool like Wireshark can be
utilized to spot these unencrypted packets.

--- Page 212 ---
To help counteract such threats, penetration testers can use tools like Burp
Suite to inspect the transmitted data. Testers can determine whether sensitive
information is transmitted over HTTP or secure HTTPS by intercepting and
reviewing the data packets. This hands-on approach will empower you to
identify and rectify insecure data transmission practices.
Verbose error messages
Next, we focus on an often-neglected threat vector: verbose error messages.
While detailed error messages can be incredibly useful for development and
debugging, they can expose valuable information to attackers when left in
production environments. For example, an error message revealing the
specifics of a failed database query could provide an attacker with insights
into the database structure, paving the way for SQL injection attacks.
A practical way of identifying this flaw is by sending incorrect or malformed
data to the API endpoints and then examining the returned error messages. If
the error messages provide too much information about the application’s
inner workings, such as specific database table names or software versions,
you have stumbled upon a security hole that needs patching.
Preventing insecure data handling is no small task, but with a deliberate and
diligent approach, it is a pitfall that can be avoided. It requires implementing
secure communication channels, careful data management, and being
mindful of the information exposed through error messages. It also involves
regular security testing and code reviews to ensure that data is always
handled securely.
Tools
In exploring mobile application penetration testing, tools serve as our
compass, guiding us through the intricate landscape of vulnerabilities. We
discuss their functionalities and delve into their specific applications in
uncovering and exploiting vulnerabilities. In our quest to secure mobile web
applications, we are empowered by an arsenal of potent tools, each playing a
pivotal role in uncovering vulnerabilities and helping us to patch them
before they are exploited.

--- Page 213 ---
Take, for instance, the powerful penetration testing tool Burp Suite. While
known for its comprehensive web vulnerability scanner, its ability to exploit
XSS vulnerabilities in mobile web applications is particularly interesting.
Burp's Intruder tool, combined with its Payloads library, can automate
injecting malicious scripts into potentially vulnerable input fields, making
identifying XSS vulnerabilities far more efficient. For instance, an e-
commerce mobile app may not correctly sanitize the search input field,
leaving it prone to XSS attacks. In such a scenario, we would configure Burp
Intruder to cycle through various XSS payloads to uncover this vulnerability.
Its ability to intercept and modify HTTP requests and responses provides us
with a microscopic view of data as it travels between the client and the
server. Let us take a hypothetical mobile web application that contains a
form for user feedback. Using Burp Suite, we could capture the HTTP
request made when submitting this form, manipulate the data fields, and
observe the server's response. If the server processes this manipulated
request without proper validation, it will disclose a security flaw in how the
application handles user input.
In the same vein, we have the OWASP ZAP, a tool that equips us to
automate finding vulnerabilities. Imagine a mobile web application that
employs session management but has not implemented securely. Using ZAP,
we could automatically spider through the application, simulate active
sessions, and reveal if the application exposes session IDs, a vulnerability
that could lead to session hijacking.
Another tool is Firebase, Google's mobile application development platform.
While primarily known for its rich feature set, misconfigurations in Firebase
can lead to security vulnerabilities in mobile applications. One such case is
when Firebase databases are left publicly accessible due to improper security
rules configuration. Using a Firebase Exploiting Tool (FET), we can
enumerate the insecure databases and read, write, or delete data depending
on the misconfiguration. For instance, a mobile application may use Firebase
to store user profiles. An attacker can exploit the database to extract sensitive
user data if it is not configured correctly.
When it comes to the powerful adversary known as SQL Injection, we have
a potent ally in the form of sqlmap. Assume a mobile web application that,
due to improper input sanitization, is vulnerable to SQL Injection. We could

--- Page 214 ---
feed sqlmap with the vulnerable URL, and it would attempt a myriad of SQL
Injection techniques. If the application responds differently to certain
payloads, it may indicate the presence of an SQL Injection vulnerability.
Then, we have tools like Postman and Rest-Assured, which are excellent for
testing APIs used by mobile applications. Consider a mobile application that
uses APIs for various functionalities like user log in, data retrieval, and so
on., using Postman. We could send requests to these API endpoints, observe
the responses, and determine if there are any improper data exposures or
authorization issues.
Let us not forget emulators such as AVD Manager and iOS Simulator, which
allow us to mimic different mobile environments. These are invaluable when
testing vulnerabilities unique to certain devices, operating systems, or screen
sizes.
In the realm of mobile web penetration testing, a grand challenge that
confronts us is the vast array of device, browser, and operating system
combinations. Each permutation may potentially introduce new
vulnerabilities; realistically, testing them all is impossible. Therefore, the
necessity for a robust yet pragmatic testing strategy is paramount. This
stratagem must balance thoroughness and efficiency, targeting the most
impactful and probable vulnerability scenarios while avoiding getting
bogged down in an endless testing loop.
Diving into the first facet, device diversity, it is vital to note that it is not just
about different brands and models. Factors such as varying screen sizes and
hardware capabilities can directly influence how an application behaves and
interacts with user input, potentially leading to distinct security
vulnerabilities. The key here is to prioritize. While it is impractical to
physically test every available device, emulators such as the AVD Manager
and iOS Simulator provide a convenient workaround. These tools allow us to
simulate a wide array of devices and their configurations, making it possible
to identify device-specific vulnerabilities without needing the physical
devices on hand.
Equally important is the role of browsers in our testing process. Each
browser, with its unique implementation and security measures, can interact
differently with the same web application. This varying interaction might

--- Page 215 ---
mean that an application is more susceptible to certain attacks on one
browser compared to another. For instance, the browser's handling of
JavaScript might affect the application's vulnerability to XSS attacks. We use
browser testing tools like BrowserStack and Sauce Labs to navigate this
maze. These platforms allow us to test our application across various
browsers, significantly enhancing our testing reach.
When it comes to operating systems, understanding the unique security
measures and nuances of Android and iOS is critical. We have seen how
differing WebView implementations can result in disparate vulnerability
profiles, a characteristic that extends to various other aspects of these
operating systems. Tools like Drozer and Frida enable us to probe these
vulnerabilities, offering a platform for exploring and identifying potential
exploits.
While we explore the myriad combinations, it is essential not to lose sight of
the bigger picture. This is where market share data and vulnerability impact
analysis become our guiding light. By focusing on the most widely used
devices, browsers, and OS versions, we maximize our coverage of the user
base. Meanwhile, vulnerability impact analysis allows us to prioritize issues
that, if exploited, would have the most significant effect.
This methodical, stratified approach is key to maintaining the right balance
between breadth and depth in mobile web application testing. It enables us to
tackle the vast diversity of the mobile ecosystem while ensuring that our
efforts yield the maximum-security benefits.
As a part of our comprehensive examination of mobile web penetration
testing, we venture into the practical applications of various tools renowned
in API testing and vulnerability assessment. Three of these tools, Postman,
Rest-Assured, and SoapUI, play a significant role in enhancing the
effectiveness of our tests, each with its own strengths and unique offerings.
We begin with Postman, a robust yet user-friendly tool that facilitates the
manual exploration of APIs. It allows us to create and send different HTTP
requests to API endpoints and analyze the responses in a clean, structured
format. For instance, we could use Postman to test a mobile application's
login API endpoint by sending a POST request with various inputs and
observing the server's responses to detect any vulnerabilities. An incorrect

--- Page 216 ---
password attempt should not return user data or specific system information,
as that could reveal too much to a potential attacker.
Rest-Assured, a Java DSL designed to simplify testing of REST-based
services, takes our testing to the next level by automating REST API tests.
Imagine a mobile application that uses an API to fetch user profile data. With
Rest-Assured, we can automate a sequence of tests, requesting the profile
data for different users, checking if authentication is required each time, and
validating that the response contains the correct data structure and values.
This level of automation is instrumental in ensuring consistent security,
especially when an application has many API endpoints.
The journey concludes with SoapUI, a widely used tool for testing REST
and SOAP APIs. Its robust features enable us to perform more complex
testing and simulate various API behaviors. For instance, we might use
SoapUI to test a mobile app's API for payment processing. We could
simulate different scenarios, such as high load situations, to observe how the
API handles them and whether any vulnerabilities, such as rate limiting
issues or data leaks, are exposed under stress.
While we have provided a brief overview and practical examples here,
referring to each tool's official documentation is essential for a deeper
understanding.
Conclusion
As we conclude this chapter on mobile penetration testing methodology, we
have navigated the intricate landscape of securing mobile applications
tackling challenges from diverse device ecosystems to complex network
environments. Our journey has equipped us with the tools and insights
necessary to uncover and address Android and iOS systems vulnerabilities,
emphasizing the importance of comprehensive testing across various device
configurations and network conditions. Applying emulators and advanced
tools like Burp Suite and OWASP ZAP has underscored the criticality of
dynamic testing in identifying potential security breaches. Each step in this
process has enhanced our understanding of mobile security and strengthened
our ability to protect the digital landscape from emerging threats.

--- Page 217 ---
As we move forward, the next chapter promises to delve deeper into mobile
penetration testing, offering hands-on guidance on leveraging this powerful
tool to further our capabilities in securing mobile applications.
Points to remember
Diverse operating systems: Understand the security features and
vulnerabilities of different operating systems like Android and iOS.
Device configurations: Recognize the security implications of various
device configurations and the risks of misconfigurations.
Network conditions impact: Be aware of how changing network
conditions can affect mobile application security.
Vulnerability identification: Learn to identify different types of
vulnerabilities, from minor misconfigurations to serious threats like
SQL injections.
API testing: Understand the importance of testing APIs, focusing on
areas like insecure data handling and verbose error messages.
Static and dynamic analysis: Familiarize with both static and
dynamic analysis techniques for a comprehensive security assessment.
Using emulators for testing: Know how to use tools like Android
Virtual Device (AVD) Manager and iOS Simulator for replicating
various environments.
Network traffic analysis tools: Acknowledge the role of tools like
Burp Suite and OWASP ZAP in intercepting and analyzing network
traffic.
Questions

systems like Android and iOS?
2. How can device configuration impact the security of mobile
applications?

--- Page 218 ---
3. In what ways do changing network conditions affect mobile application
vulnerabilities?
4. What methods can be employed to identify and analyze various
vulnerabilities in mobile applications?
5. How does API testing contribute to securing mobile applications,
particularly regarding data handling and error messages?

the context of mobile application security testing?
