CHAPTER 9
Developing a Vulnerability
Remediation Plan
Introduction
In this chapter, readers will acquire the skills needed for detailed
documentation, effective communication, and development of remediation
plans following mobile penetration tests. It explores a range of technologies
and methodologies essential for managing and reporting the results of these
tests, with a special focus on mobile security challenges. The chapter
introduces tools like Dradis, Faraday, and KeepNote for creating structured
reports that catalog vulnerabilities with supportive narratives and evidence,
crucial in complex mobile environments. It emphasizes the need for clear
communication with stakeholders, providing strategies to present
vulnerability data in a compelling manner to different audiences, from
technical teams to upper management. Best practices in both visual and
written presentations are covered to ensure effective conveyance of remedial
urgency. The latter part of the chapter guides readers through developing
actionable remediation plans, utilizing frameworks like the OWASP Mobile
Security Testing Guide and the OWASP Mobile Application Security
Verification Standard. These resources offer structured remediation
approaches, including best practices and checklists specifically for mobile
contexts. Readers will learn to create comprehensive action plans, prioritize
remediation tasks using scoring systems like CVSS and DREAD, and gain
insights into ongoing risk mitigation strategies.

--- Page 277 ---
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
Creating effective penetration testing reports
Communicating vulnerabilities to stakeholders
Developing remediation plans
Prioritizing vulnerabilities
Tracking progress and verifying remediation
Objectives
In this chapter, we focus on the crucial aspects of creating effective
penetration testing reports, communicating vulnerabilities, and developing
comprehensive remediation plans. Our objectives include understanding the
tools and techniques for documenting penetration tests, learning how to
structure reports effectively, and utilizing templates to streamline the
process. We will delve into the art of communicating technical
vulnerabilities to various stakeholders, emphasizing audience segmentation,
the use of visual aids, and the principles of effective communication.
Additionally, the chapter guides readers through the development of detailed
remediation plans, incorporating OWASP Guidelines and strategies for
creating actionable plans. A significant emphasis is placed on prioritizing
vulnerabilities, conducting thorough risk assessments, and calculating risks
effectively. We will also explore the essential practices of tracking progress
and verifying the success of remediation efforts, introducing tools like Jira
and Trello for progress tracking and communication management. By the
end of this chapter, readers will be equipped with the knowledge and skills
necessary to not only identify and report cybersecurity threats but also to
communicate them effectively and manage the remediation process
efficiently.
Creating effective penetration testing reports
The culmination of a successful penetration test is encapsulated in the
creation of an effective report, a critical document that communicates the

--- Page 278 ---
findings, implications, and recommendations to stakeholders. This report
should be structured to provide a clear and concise overview of the testing
process, the vulnerabilities discovered, and the potential impact on the
organization. It is essential to strike a balance between technical detail and
readability, ensuring that the content is accessible to both technical and non-
technical audiences. Key components include an executive summary for
leadership, detailed descriptions of each identified vulnerability, the methods
used for testing, and prioritized recommendations for remediation. Effective
reports also include evidence or proof of concept for vulnerabilities to
substantiate the findings.
Introduction to documentation tools
The need for organized and meticulous documentation in a penetration
testing engagement cannot be overstated. Documentation serves as the
cornerstone of a comprehensive security audit, facilitating the identification
of vulnerabilities and subsequent remediation processes and stakeholder
communications. In the complex arena of mobile security, where one may
deal with a myriad of operating systems, applications, and network
configurations, the necessity for structured documentation is amplified.
Tools like Dradis, Faraday, and KeepNote are invaluable assets for this.
These tools offer features that range from data organization and
collaborative functionalities to reporting capabilities, making them
indispensable in managing large-scale penetration tests. A step-by-step guide
on using these tools will help you understand how to organize and store the
information gleaned during the engagement most efficiently.
The importance of well-organized documentation in a mobile penetration
testing project is paramount. Accurate and thorough documentation not only
aids in systematically identifying vulnerabilities but also underpins the entire
remediation process and serves as the foundation for clear and effective
communication with stakeholders. Given the multifaceted and frequently
complex environment of mobile security comprising various operating
systems, a multitude of apps, and diverse network setups, structured
documentation is not a luxury but a necessity.
In this context, let us delve deeper into specific tools that stand out for their
utility in effective documentation: Dradis, Faraday, and KeepNote. Dradis is

--- Page 279 ---
especially beneficial when working on team-based projects. Its collaborative
functionalities ensure all team members can contribute to the same
repository, thus avoiding data duplication and inconsistency. It also supports
integration with other tools and platforms used in penetration testing,
making importing data for comprehensive analysis and reporting easier. For
instance, in a scenario where your team is evaluating the security of an
enterprise mobile application, Dradis can serve as a central hub where
imported vulnerability data from tools like Burp Suite or OWASP ZAP can
be centrally stored and accessed by all team members.
Faraday, on the other hand, excels in its capability to act as an Integrated
Penetration-Test Environment (IPE). Faraday unifies the workflow by
enabling real-time sharing and data visualization among the team. A specific
use case might involve real-time network mapping in an engagement that
focuses on assessing the security posture of connected mobile devices within
a corporate intranet. As team members identify vulnerable network services
or potential entry points, Faraday allows these findings to be immediately
available to the whole team, thereby speeding up the assessment and
remediation process.
KeepNote serves a different but equally vital role in the documentation
toolkit. While it lacks the real-time collaborative features of Dradis or
Faraday, it offers robust capabilities for individual testers to organize data
hierarchically and include detailed notes, screenshots, and other attachments.
This can be especially useful when building an executive summary for non-
technical stakeholders. For example, during the evaluation of a mobile point-
of-sale system for a retail client, KeepNote can help you compile screenshots
of critical vulnerabilities, such as insecure data storage or weak encryption,
and annotate them for a presentation to company executives who may not
fully grasp the technical nuances.
So, how do these tools come together in a hypothetical scenario? Imagine
you are contracted to conduct a penetration test on a healthcare application
used on both Android and iOS platforms. Your team uses Dradis as the
central repository for findings, allowing seamless sharing of vulnerabilities
discovered during the test. Faraday aids in real-time network analysis,
pinpointing insecure API endpoints that could potentially leak patient data.
The technical team focuses on these data points. Still, for the healthcare
executives, KeepNote compiles a simplified but detailed report featuring the

--- Page 280 ---
most critical vulnerabilities and their potential impact in layman's terms, thus
facilitating decision-making on remediation priorities.
By combining these tools, you are not just conducting a penetration test but
managing an end-to-end security evaluation process that caters to technical
and non-technical stakeholders. Each tool has its unique strengths, and
knowing how to leverage them efficiently is essential for the success of your
mobile penetration testing engagement.
Report structure
An effective penetration testing report for mobile environments is a
comprehensive document that provides a detailed understanding of the
security posture, tailored to meet the needs of both technical and non-
technical stakeholders. It comprises key components: an Executive
Summary for decision-makers, Detailed Findings with in-depth analysis, and
actionable Recommendations for remediation.
The Executive Summary offers a high-level view, highlighting critical
vulnerabilities and their potential impacts, ideal for non-technical audiences
like executives or managers. For instance, issues in a mobile payment
application’s encryption would be summarized here, noting the high-risk
potential for data breaches.
The Detailed Findings section delves into each vulnerability, describing the
issue, providing risk ratings, and including evidence like proof-of-concept,
logs, or screenshots. This section may also have subsections for unique
mobile issues, such as SMS-based vulnerabilities or Mobile Device
Management (MDM) flaws. In a scenario where plaintext credentials are
retrieved from an Android app’s database, this section would detail how the
insecure data storage was identified and the implications of this
vulnerability.
Recommendations provide a direct mapping from findings to remediation
strategies, prioritizing actions based on risk assessments. These suggestions
are specific and practical, with detailed guidance for implementation, such as
using Android’s Encrypted File Storage APIs for securing data,
supplemented by relevant code samples.
Refer to the following figure:

--- Page 281 ---
Figure 9.1: Reporting takes on many forms, including graphs of results over time
For example, in a hypothetical assessment of a bank's mobile app, the
Executive Summary would underline the critical nature of discovered
vulnerabilities like insecure API calls, highlighting the financial and
reputational risks. The Detailed Findings would then offer technical insights
into these vulnerabilities, complete with intercepted logs and authentication
bypass demonstrations. The Recommendations section would then guide the
bank’s technical team on enhancing API security with OAuth 2.0 and
improving login procedures with Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA),
providing detailed implementation steps.
Such a structured approach in reporting ensures that all stakeholders,
regardless of their technical expertise, receive the necessary information to
understand the security issues and participate in the remediation process
effectively.
Use of templates
Utilizing templates for penetration testing reports offers a balance of
standardization and customization, essential for efficiently addressing the
unique aspects of each engagement, particularly in mobile environments.
Templates ensure all crucial report elements, like Executive Summaries and

--- Page 282 ---
Detailed Findings, are consistently included. However, mobile penetration
tests, with distinct challenges like device-specific vulnerabilities or mobile
application issues, require these templates to be adapted accordingly.
There are as many reporting templates as there are penetration testing teams
on the planet, but one excellent repository can be found at
https://pentestreports.com/reports/. This page contains a wide variety of
publicly disclosed penetration reports which can be used to formulate your
own templates.
For example, a standard template for network penetration tests might not
adequately cover mobile-specific issues like insecure data storage or
communication in mobile apps. Customizing these templates allows for the
inclusion of such mobile-relevant sections, ensuring a comprehensive and
accurate report. Modifications might include altering risk assessment criteria
to account for mobile-specific risks or adding code samples relevant to
mobile platforms in the Recommendations section.
Consider a scenario where a mobile Point-of-Sale (POS) system is assessed.
A standard template would not suffice for the unique vulnerabilities
discovered, such as weak authentication mechanisms or insecure Bluetooth
communications. Customizing the template to include these specifics, like
sections on Bluetooth security or mobile device locking mechanisms,
provides a detailed and targeted report. This approach not only maintains the
report’s integrity but also tailors it to address the peculiarities of the mobile
environment effectively.
Communicating vulnerabilities to stakeholders
Effective communication of vulnerabilities to stakeholders is a critical
component of any cybersecurity strategy. This involves clearly articulating
the nature, severity, and potential impact of identified vulnerabilities to all
relevant parties, from technical teams to executive management. The key is
to present this information in a manner that is both comprehensible and
actionable, avoiding overly technical jargon when addressing non-technical
stakeholders. It is essential to convey the urgency and importance of the
issues while providing a balanced view of the risks involved and the steps
required for remediation. This communication should also foster an
environment of transparency and collaboration, encouraging stakeholder

--- Page 283 ---
engagement in the remediation process. By keeping stakeholders well-
informed and involved, organizations can ensure a unified and effective
approach to addressing cybersecurity threats, aligning the efforts of various
departments with the overall security and business objectives of the
organization.
Audience segmentation
Effective communication in mobile penetration testing hinges on
understanding and addressing the diverse needs of your audience, which
typically includes technical experts, management, and legal advisors.
Technical stakeholders require in-depth details about vulnerabilities,
exploitation methods, and remediation techniques, focusing on actionable
information for immediate response. Conversely, management and non-
technical personnel need an overview of the broader impact of
vulnerabilities on organizational goals, finances, and compliance, presented
in simplified language with clear recommendations.
For instance, in a mobile healthcare app with critical vulnerabilities,
technical teams would receive detailed reports including API logs and
specific steps for secure coding practices. Management would get an
Executive Summary emphasizing the risks of data leakage in terms of
patient confidentiality and HIPAA compliance, along with financial
implications. Visual aids like dashboards can effectively convey the severity
and remediation timeline of vulnerabilities to both technical and non-
technical audiences, aiding comprehension across the board.
This tailored approach ensures that technical teams have the necessary
details to address vulnerabilities, while management understands the wider
organizational impact, facilitating informed decision-making and efficient
allocation of resources for remediation.
Visual Aids
The old adage that a picture is worth a thousand words holds a lot of weight
when it comes to effectively communicating complex technical findings.
Utilizing visual aids such as graphs, charts, and dashboards can drastically
improve the comprehension of the vulnerabilities identified during the
mobile penetration test. For example, pie charts or bar graphs can provide a

--- Page 284 ---
quick overview of the distribution of vulnerabilities based on their severity
levels. Dashboards can aggregate multiple data points and present them in an
easily digestible manner. Tools like Microsoft Excel, Tableau, or specialized
security dashboards can aid in the creation of these visual elements. For
mobile-specific data, heat maps may be used to show geographical locations
where vulnerabilities could be exploited, especially if the mobile
environment tested involves a distributed, global user base.
Visual aids are an essential component of effective communication,
especially when discussing intricate technical data derived from mobile
penetration tests. They serve the dual purpose of enabling a nuanced
understanding of vulnerabilities while also facilitating the quick absorption
of key points. Graphs and charts are especially useful for quickly conveying
the distribution of vulnerabilities according to their severity levels, types, or
even attack vectors. In the realm of mobile security, this could include pie
charts that display the proportion of vulnerabilities related to data leakage,
insecure API endpoints, or broken cryptography. Bar graphs could further
highlight how many of these vulnerabilities are categorized as high, medium,
or low severity, providing a quick way for both technical and non-technical
stakeholders to grasp the urgency of remediation efforts.
Dashboards are another powerful tool in the visual communication arsenal,
particularly when handling larger datasets. Dashboards can aggregate
information across multiple axes, such as time, attack vectors, or affected
modules, and present it in a concise format that is easy to interpret.
Specialized security dashboards like those offered by Security Information
and Event Management (SIEM) tools can even allow for real-time
tracking of remediation efforts, serving as both a reporting and monitoring
tool. Given the variety of data involved in a mobile penetration test, a
dashboard might show trends in how particular types of vulnerabilities are
being remediated over time or how frequently new issues of a similar nature
are appearing, providing actionable insights for ongoing security efforts.
For mobile-specific data, heat maps offer unique advantages, especially
when dealing with a globally distributed user base. For instance, if a
vulnerability is related to insecure data transmission and the mobile
application is used worldwide, a heat map can pinpoint geographical
locations where insecure data transmission is most prevalent. This is
invaluable for understanding the scope and potential impact of the

--- Page 285 ---
vulnerability, allowing stakeholders to prioritize remediation in those areas
that are most at risk.
In terms of tools, Microsoft Excel offers basic functionalities for creating
graphs and charts, but for more complex or interactive visualizations,
Tableau or custom dashboards using Grafana or other visualization libraries
can be employed. It is worth mentioning that these tools should ideally be
compatible with the reporting tools you are using, like Dradis or Faraday, to
ensure seamless integration.
Consider a hypothetical scenario where a mobile penetration test reveals that
an e-commerce application has multiple API vulnerabilities that expose
sensitive customer data. A pie chart could be used to show the percentage of
API-related vulnerabilities compared to other types, such as insecure data
storage or broken authentication. A dashboard could then aggregate this data
to show which APIs are most frequently targeted, the types of data exposed,
and the severity of each vulnerability. For a global e-commerce platform, a
heat map could show geographical locations where these vulnerabilities are
being most frequently exploited. This approach ensures that while technical
teams get a comprehensive understanding of the vulnerabilities to begin
remediation, management teams also gain a macro-level understanding of
the risks involved, including potential financial and reputational damages.
Effective communication
Effective communication in mobile security involves a strategic blend of
clarity, completeness, and relatability. It is crucial to adopt a layered
reporting approach to cater to different stakeholder needs. This method starts
with an Executive Summary, offering a high-level overview for non-
technical decision-makers, followed by a Technical Summary with more
detailed risk assessments for technical teams, and concludes with in-depth
analyses in the Detailed Findings section.
Case studies enhance these reports by providing real-world examples of
similar vulnerabilities and their consequences, making technical data more
relatable and emphasizing the urgency of remediation. They serve as
powerful narratives that supplement statistical and technical information,
offering a comprehensive understanding of the vulnerabilities.

--- Page 286 ---
Effective communication also extends beyond just reporting. It includes
monitoring and tracking remediation activities. A Remediation Tracking
section in reports, updated periodically, can outline deadlines and
responsibilities for resolving each vulnerability, offering a clear view of
progress. This information can also be integrated into project management
tools like Jira or Trello for real-time updates accessible to all stakeholders.
For example, in a healthcare app with API vulnerabilities, the report would
succinctly highlight the risks in the Executive Summary, delve into technical
specifics in the Technical Summary, and perhaps include a case study of a
similar app that suffered a breach due to such vulnerabilities. The
Remediation Tracking section would then detail the resolution timeline and
assign tasks, ensuring that both technical and non-technical stakeholders are
well-informed and engaged in the remediation process.
This comprehensive approach ensures that technical details are actionable
for engineers while the broader implications and action plans are clearly
communicated to decision-makers, facilitating an effective and collaborative
remediation process.
Developing remediation plans
Creating a comprehensive remediation plan is an essential step in the process
of strengthening an organization's cybersecurity posture. This plan outlines
specific strategies and actions to address and mitigate identified
vulnerabilities. It involves a collaborative effort, often requiring input from
various departments including IT, security, and operations, to develop a
practical and effective approach tailored to the unique needs and
infrastructure of the organization. Key elements of a remediation plan
include detailed descriptions of the vulnerabilities, proposed corrective
actions, responsible parties for each action, and timelines for
implementation. This plan should also incorporate a contingency strategy to
address any potential complications that may arise during the remediation
process. Effective communication throughout the organization is crucial to
ensure that all stakeholders understand their roles and the importance of the
remediation activities. Ultimately, a well-structured remediation plan not
only addresses current security gaps but also lays a foundation for
continuous improvement and resilience against future cyber threats.

--- Page 287 ---
OWASP Guidelines
The OWASP Mobile Security Testing Guide (MSTG) and Mobile
Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS) are crucial in
developing remediation plans for mobile app vulnerabilities. They provide
structured approaches to tackle mobile-specific issues, offering vetted
strategies for immediate security enhancements. These guidelines are not
only practical for mitigating vulnerabilities but also serve as educational
resources, demonstrated through case studies showcasing their real-world
application.
In remediation planning, these OWASP resources guide the creation of
actionable, precise plans. MSTG offers in-depth solutions for issues like
insecure data storage and weak encryption, while MASVS sets a security
benchmark for mobile apps. Integrating these into the early stages of
planning helps in mapping specific OWASP recommendations to each
vulnerability, simplifying the remediation process. This approach provides
clear guidance for technical teams and a recognized standard for compliance
officers.
For example, addressing an insecure data storage issue in a mobile app could
involve referencing MSTG for secure storage techniques, ensuring that
developers have actionable steps. Including case studies in reports, like a
retail app mitigating session handling issues using OWASP guidelines,
demonstrates the frameworks' efficacy to all stakeholders.
In practice, a financial app with an insecure data storage vulnerability could
be guided by MASVS Level 2 controls for remediation, with a detailed plan
in the report and regular monitoring post-implementation. Utilizing OWASP
frameworks in this way ensures a standardized yet customizable approach to
vulnerability management, essential for both technical remediation and
organizational compliance.
Creating action plans
Prioritizing and remediating vulnerabilities in mobile applications is a
critical process, requiring detailed action plans for each identified issue.
These plans outline the technical steps for resolution, assign responsibilities,
and set timelines. For example, rectifying an insecure data storage issue in

--- Page 288 ---
an Android app might involve upgrading to a more secure encryption
method and utilizing Android's Keystore system, with clear roles and
deadlines for development, QA, and deployment teams.
The action plans should not only address immediate vulnerabilities but also
include strategies for future risk mitigation, such as integrating Secure
Development Lifecycle (SDLC) checks and continuous security testing in
the development pipeline. For instance, incorporating tools like Checkmarx
in the CI/CD process helps in early detection and prevention of
vulnerabilities.
In practice, a vulnerable healthcare app might have an action plan detailing
tasks like enhancing API security and employing real-time monitoring tools
for ongoing vigilance. The plan would specify who is responsible for each
task and the expected completion timeline, ensuring timely and effective
remediation. Adopting standards like OWASP's MSTG and MASVS can
guide sustainable security practices and continuous improvement.
Effective action plans are dynamic, evolving with new threats and findings.
They should be regularly reviewed and updated, with real-time monitoring
post-implementation to measure their effectiveness. This approach ensures
not only the immediate resolution of vulnerabilities but also the long-term
strengthening of mobile application security.
Prioritizing vulnerabilities
In the process of vulnerability management, prioritizing vulnerabilities is a
critical step that dictates the efficiency and effectiveness of the remediation
efforts. This task involves evaluating each vulnerability based on factors like
severity, exploitability, impact on the system, and the likelihood of
exploitation. The goal is to create a hierarchy where the most critical
vulnerabilities that pose the greatest risk to the organization are addressed
first. This prioritization ensures that resources are allocated in a manner that
mitigates the most significant threats swiftly, thereby reducing the window
of opportunity for potential attackers. It is a strategic approach that balances
the urgency of the threat with the practicality of the solution, ensuring that
efforts are not just reactionary but are also aligned with the overall security
posture and business objectives of the organization.

--- Page 289 ---
Risk assessment
Vulnerability prioritization in remediation plans is essential, particularly
after mobile penetration testing. Scoring frameworks like CVSS and
DREAD provide a systematic way to evaluate and communicate the severity
of vulnerabilities, considering factors like exploitation likelihood, impact,
and remediation ease. CVSS's detailed metrics are ideal for formal reporting
and compliance, while DREAD's simplicity aids in internal decision-
making. Both frameworks help identify high-priority vulnerabilities that
require immediate attention.
Integrating these scores into SIEM systems or vulnerability management
tools allows for effective monitoring and immediate response to high-risk
vulnerabilities. For instance, a high CVSS score can trigger an urgent alert in
the SIEM system, prioritizing rapid action from incident response teams. In
a practical scenario, such as a healthcare mobile app with a high CVSS score
for SQL Injection, immediate alerts and rapid remediation are essential. This
structured prioritization ensures that severe threats are mitigated first,
enhancing the overall security posture.
CVSS offers a detailed evaluation from 0 to 10, suitable for broad, formal
use, while DREAD provides a simpler, rapid assessment tool, ideal for agile
environments. Their use depends on the organization's needs, with some
opting to use both for comprehensive risk assessment. By incorporating
these scoring systems into monitoring and reporting platforms, organizations
can maintain a proactive, responsive security stance, ensuring both technical
and non-technical stakeholders are informed and aligned on the remediation
process:

--- Page 290 ---
Figure 9.2: The basic risk management cycle can be summarized as seen above
Calculating risk
To bring theory into practice, a detailed walkthrough of how to use CVSS
and DREAD for vulnerability evaluation is invaluable. Suppose an example
vulnerability from a recent mobile penetration test is an SQL Injection on an
Android application. Using CVSS, you might rate the Access Complexity as
low, Authentication is not required, and the impact on Confidentiality,
Integrity, and Availability as high. The composite score would indicate that
the vulnerability is of high severity and should be addressed immediately. In
parallel, applying DREAD might involve evaluating the damage potential as
significant, given the vulnerability could allow unauthorized access to
sensitive user data, among other things. In this case, both CVSS and
DREAD scores would indicate that the SQL Injection vulnerability should
be prioritized for immediate remediation.
When dealing with a specific vulnerability, such as SQL Injection,
discovered in an Android application, CVSS is often the first point of
reference for detailed risk assessment. Let us dissect how to apply CVSS to
this case:

--- Page 291 ---
1. Access Vector: The attack is network exploitable. The attacker can
execute SQL queries remotely.
2. Access Complexity: Classified as Low because SQL Injection often
just requires modified input in user fields.
3. Authentication: Set to None since the application does not necessitate
special authentication to exploit this vulnerability.
4. Confidentiality Impact: Scored as High due to the potential leak of
sensitive database information.
5. Integrity Impact: Also, High because the database contents can be
manipulated.
6. Availability Impact: Often considered High because a successful SQL
Injection attack can lock users out by corrupting data.
Once these parameters are scored individually, they contribute to a
composite score that usually falls in the range of high severity (CVSS scores
7.0-10.0).
While CVSS gives us a nuanced and comprehensive look, DREAD offers a
more straightforward, quicker assessment. For the same SQL Injection
vulnerability, the DREAD evaluation might look like this:
Damage Potential: Rated as High because unauthorized access to
sensitive data is possible.
Reproducibility: Scored as High since the vulnerability can be
consistently exploited.
Exploitability: Marked as High due to the ease with which SQL
Injection vulnerabilities can be exploited.
Affected Users: Classified as High, assuming most of the app’s user
base could be impacted.
Discoverability: Rated Medium to High, depending on how visible
the vulnerability is.
After averaging these scores, you would likely also arrive at a high DREAD
score, pointing toward the need for immediate remediation.

--- Page 292 ---
Utilizing CVSS and DREAD scores together provides a comprehensive view
of vulnerability risks. CVSS offers a detailed metric suitable for formal
reporting and compliance, while DREAD's simplicity aids in internal
discussions and swift decision-making. High scores from both systems, like
in an SQL Injection scenario, signal the need for immediate action.
Integrating these scores into SIEM systems or vulnerability management
tools is crucial for effective monitoring and response. For example, a high
CVSS score could trigger an automatic alert in the SIEM system, prompting
immediate action from the incident response team, while a high DREAD
score highlights the need for urgent discussion.
Consider a healthcare app with a high CVSS-scored SQL Injection
vulnerability found during a security audit. This triggers an alert in the SIEM
system for an emergency response, while the DREAD score underscores the
urgency internally. A Critical Vulnerability Report, including both scores, is
shared with technical and executive teams, contributing to a comprehensive
historical data record for future efficacy assessment.
The use of structured scoring frameworks like CVSS and DREAD enables
data-driven, quantitative analysis of vulnerabilities. This empirical approach
facilitates intelligent decision-making for prioritization and remediation,
focusing resources on higher-ranked vulnerabilities to minimize potential
damage. Such scoring systems ensure efficient allocation and scheduling of
remediation resources, enhancing overall cybersecurity management.
Methodologies in risk assessment:
CVSS methodology: When strict compliance with industry
regulations is required, CVSS serves as the go-to methodology. Its
granular scoring approach makes it apt for legal and compliance
reports.
DREAD methodology: For quick decision-making, especially in
internal organizational settings, DREAD offers a more straightforward
approach. It is best used in quick reviews or agile environments.
Hybrid approach: Often, a combination of both CVSS and DREAD
is employed to furnish a 360-degree view of risk assessment. This
approach is suitable for organizations with diverse risk landscapes that
need both detailed and rapid analyses.

--- Page 293 ---
Once vulnerabilities are scored and ranked, this information should be
integrated into security monitoring systems. For example, vulnerabilities
with CVSS scores above eight could be configured to generate real-time
alerts in your security monitoring systems, thereby shortening the response
time. Concurrently, regular reports containing aggregated DREAD scores
can be compiled and disseminated to department heads for internal reviews,
ensuring that everyone is on the same page regarding the risk landscape.
Imagine an e-commerce mobile application where multiple vulnerabilities
were found, including both IDOR and Cross-Site Scripting (XSS). After
using CVSS and DREAD scoring frameworks, the IDOR vulnerability was
ranked higher due to its potential for immediate financial damage.
A high-priority alert was triggered in the monitoring system, and the
cybersecurity response team was notified. A report was then generated that
included both the CVSS and DREAD scores and was sent to the technical
team for immediate remediation and to the senior management to make them
aware of the risk. In addition, the vulnerability data was logged in the
company’s security dashboard to track remediation efforts and validate the
efficacy of security controls over time.
By systematically employing scoring frameworks like CVSS and DREAD,
you can align your remediation efforts with empirical data, providing a solid
foundation for both immediate action and long-term security strategy. This
strategy ensures that you are not just reacting to vulnerabilities as they are
found but proactively managing them in a way that makes the most sense for
your organization's risk profile.
Tracking progress and verifying remediation
In the realm of cybersecurity, particularly in the aftermath of penetration
testing, it is crucial to have a structured approach for tracking progress and
verifying the remediation of identified vulnerabilities. This process involves
meticulously documenting each found vulnerability, the steps taken for its
rectification, and subsequently verifying that these fixes are both effective
and comprehensive. It is a cycle of continuous improvement, where the
remediation actions are not only implemented but also rigorously tested to
ensure that they stand up against potential future attacks. This phase is
pivotal in the overall security lifecycle, providing assurance that all efforts in

--- Page 294 ---
identifying and addressing vulnerabilities translate into a tangible
enhancement of the system's security posture. By maintaining a detailed log
of actions taken and regularly revisiting remediated vulnerabilities,
organizations can ensure a robust defense against evolving cyber threats,
thereby fortifying their digital infrastructure against potential breaches.
Progress tracking tools
In the realm of vulnerability remediation, it is imperative to employ
structured methods to track your progress. Tools such as Jira and Trello can
serve as invaluable assets in this regard. Jira is a widely used project
management tool designed for bug tracking, issue tracking, and project
management, making it a suitable choice for coordinating complex
remediation efforts. Trello, on the other hand, offers a more visual, board-
based approach that can be particularly useful for smaller teams or simpler
projects. Both platforms allow for the creation of tasks, assignment of roles,
and marking milestones, and they integrate well with various third-party
tools. To maximize their utility, you may start by defining the vulnerabilities
as issues or cards and updating their status as they move through the stages
of remediation, from identification to verification.
In a dynamic environment where vulnerabilities are continually discovered
and patched, the essence of effective remediation lies in a structured tracking
system. It is not just about identifying and fixing vulnerabilities but also
about verifying that the fixes are both effective and do not introduce new
security risks. Here, the choice of project management tools can make a
significant difference:
Jira methodology: Organizations with complex security postures or
larger teams usually find Jira to be an ideal tool. It is designed to
handle the intricacies of project management, including but not limited
to workflow customization, issue linking, and reporting. Jira’s robust
features, such as tracking story points and sprint planning, can be
adapted for tracking the lifecycle of each vulnerability from discovery
to remediation and verification. It is ideal for projects that require
granular control and detailed tracking, such as large-scale
vulnerability remediation in enterprise settings.

--- Page 295 ---
Trello methodology: For more straightforward use cases or smaller
teams, Trello offers a more accessible point of entry. Its board-based
visual layout allows team members to see the status of various
vulnerabilities at a glance. Cards can be moved from columns like
"Identified," "Under Review," "Being Fixed," and "Verified," offering
a quick but comprehensive view of remediation status. It is well-suited
for projects with fewer complexities, such as ad-hoc vulnerability
assessments or Small and Medium-sized Business (SMB)
environments.
Integration capabilities: Both Jira and Trello offer APIs and support
third-party integrations, making it easy to connect them with your
existing security tools. This integration ensures that new
vulnerabilities identified through scanners or pen tests can
automatically be converted into issues/cards in the project
management system.
Refer to the following figure:

--- Page 296 ---
Figure 9.3: Tracking methods such as Gantt charts are highly useful for tracking progress over time
Once vulnerabilities are identified and scored using systems like CVSS or
DREAD, they are added as individual issues or cards in project management
tools like Jira or Trello. These items are then prioritized and assigned to
relevant teams or individuals, categorized by the affected system or
component. Throughout the remediation process, updates and documentation
are added to each entry, creating an audit trail crucial for internal review and
external compliance.
These tools also support real-time monitoring and reporting. Custom
dashboards can display the status of high-priority vulnerabilities, and
automated reports are tailored for different audiences – detailed weekly
updates for technical teams and monthly summaries for executives focusing
on major resolved issues.
For instance, in a healthcare mobile app managing patient records,
vulnerabilities like API endpoint exposure and insecure storage are scored
and tracked in Jira, given the sensitive nature of healthcare data. The

--- Page 297 ---
progress of remediation efforts is monitored in real time, with status updates
as patches are deployed. Additionally, alerts in the SIEM system are set up to
detect future similar vulnerabilities. Regular reports from Jira keep technical
teams and management informed of ongoing progress and resolutions.
This structured approach in using tracking tools like Jira or Trello enhances
the efficiency and effectiveness of the vulnerability management process,
ensuring a robust and resilient security posture.\
Jira
In Jira, an Epic serve as a large container for your tasks, making it perfect
for major security projects. For example, if you are focusing on a substantial
initiative like Android App Security Enhancement, you can encapsulate the
entire project within an Epic by the same name.
Navigate to your Jira dashboard, click Create, and select Epic as the issue
type. Input necessary details such as the Epic name, description, and any
associated components or labels for categorization.
Within this Epic, you can create individual Issues for each vulnerability
discovered. These issues should contain all necessary data, including
vulnerability details, affected systems, and remediation steps.
Inside the Epic you created, click Create Issue in Epic. Populate it with
fields like summary, description, and assignee. Use custom fields for
vulnerability-specific data like CVSS score or DREAD assessment.
These Issues can be moved through a customized workflow that reflects
your organization's remediation process. Typically, statuses such as To Do,
In Progress, and Done can be extended to more descriptive states like
Identified, Under Review, Remediation in Progress, and Verified.
This Jira methodology is particularly well-suited for larger teams or complex
projects where various tasks are interdependent and require fine-grained
management.
Trello

--- Page 298 ---
In Trello, your mobile security project can be conceptualized as a dedicated
board. Create lists within the board to reflect stages of the vulnerability
management lifecycle, such as Identified Vulnerabilities, In Progress, and
Remediated.
After logging into Trello, click New Board and enter relevant details. Create
lists by clicking Add another list and name them appropriately.
Each identified vulnerability is represented as a card. Cards can be moved
from list to list as they progress through the remediation stages.
Inside a specific list, click Add another card and populate it with details
like vulnerability description, affected systems, and remediation steps.
Trello’s utility extends to its simplified task management features. Due dates
can be set for expected remediation times, checklists can outline the
necessary remediation steps, and labels can be used for quick categorization.
Trello is excellent for smaller teams or projects with fewer dependencies. Its
ease of use and visual workflow are particularly beneficial for quick
onboarding and simplified tracking.
Let us assume you are working on an e-commerce mobile app that has been
found to contain multiple security vulnerabilities. You choose Jira due to the
complexity of the application and the array of technologies it uses.
An Epic named E-Commerce Mobile App Security Overhaul is created,
summarizing the overarching goal of the project. Vulnerabilities like
Insecure Direct Object References and Broken Authentication are logged
as individual issues within the Epic. These issues are prioritized using CVSS
scores and are assigned to specific developers or security engineers for
remediation. Custom Jira dashboards provide real-time monitoring of issue
status. Automated reports are generated: a daily update for the security team
and a weekly executive summary highlighting the high and medium
vulnerabilities that have been remediated. Once a vulnerability is
remediated, it moves to a Verification status. Post-remediation testing
confirms the effectiveness of the fix, and the issue is marked as Done.
Lessons learned are documented within Jira for future projects, and
automated alerts are set up for similar vulnerabilities.

--- Page 299 ---
In this way, employing a structured approach to tracking vulnerability
remediation not only adds rigor to the process but also provides actionable
data and reporting capabilities for various stakeholders.
Communication management
Both Jira and Trello play crucial roles in managing communications during
the vulnerability remediation process. They offer features like comments,
attachments, and real-time notifications, ensuring all stakeholders stay
informed. For instance, in Jira, team members can add comments to issues,
tagging others for immediate feedback or updates, which is essential when
dealing with complex vulnerabilities. Attachments like patch notes or test
scenarios can be added for context. Jira's notification system alerts team
members about key updates, such as changes in issue status, enhancing the
efficiency of the verification process. Regular status reports and stand-up
meeting reminders can also be managed through Jira.
Trello's simplicity, with its comment system and card-based interface, is
effective for quick updates and discussions, especially in smaller teams. It
supports file attachments for additional context and integrates with third-
party tools for extended functionality. Notifications in Trello can be
customized for real-time updates on crucial changes.
In a practical scenario, managing a remediation project for a mobile
application with Jira ensures that all team members, from developers to QA
and project managers, are aligned and informed throughout the process.
Automated notifications and regular reports keep everyone updated on
progress and pending tasks. In contrast, Trello’s visual layout and simpler
interface cater well to non-technical stakeholders, providing an overview of
the project status and facilitating straightforward, real-time communication.
By using these tools effectively, teams can enhance coordination and
transparency in the remediation process, ensuring technical and
communication efforts are equally prioritized for successful vulnerability
management.

CHAPTER 10Detecting Vulnerabilities on Android Apps
Introduction
The transition of mobile applications from desktop complements to central players in digital interaction hasamplified their security importance, particularly in Android's expansive ecosystem. Android's open-source naturedrives innovation but also opens doors for malware and exploitation, evidenced by incidents like third-party appvulnerabilities and the Stagefright bug. This chapter dives into the intricacies of Android application security witha hands-on approach, moving from abstract concepts to practical applications. You will learn through realscenarios, like exploiting insecure data storage with Android Debug Bridge (ADB) and examining SQLitedatabases for sensitive data. The chapter assumes a basic understanding of Android, exploring the anatomy ofapps, including AndroidManifest.xml, APK structure, and coding in Java or Kotlin. It emphasizes that masteringAndroid security is a continuous journey, adapting to new updates from Google and evolving threats, equippingyou with skills and insights for proactive security management in the dynamic world of Android applications.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
Setting up your testing environment
The penetration test
From analysis to identification
Understanding vulnerable Android apps
Securing the future
Objectives
This chapter aims to deepen your understanding of Android application security through practical, hands-onexperiences. You'll learn to set up a testing environment, conduct penetration tests, and explore variousvulnerabilities in Android apps. Key topics include configuring Burp Proxy, understanding the anatomy of Androidapps, and working with tools like ADB. You will engage with real-world scenarios, such as exploiting insecuredata storage and navigating input validation issues. The chapter also covers more advanced topics like accesscontrol and hardcoded vulnerabilities. By the end, you will gain practical skills and knowledge to effectivelyidentify and address security flaws in Android applications, preparing you to stay ahead in the rapidly evolvingfield of mobile security.
Setting up your testing environment
Before diving into the world of penetration testing, the foundation must be solid. A suitable testing environment isthat cornerstone. Here, you will be walked through the steps to construct and refine your testing sandbox. Theconfiguration process involves essential tools crucial to your journey.

--- Page 303 ---
The preliminary step before embarking on any penetration testing journey is to establish a robust testingenvironment. It serves as the controlled ecosystem where you will conduct all your experiments, diagnoses, andvalidations. To accomplish this, the first tool you will need is Android Studio. This official IDE from Google is notjust for development; it comes with a suite of debugging and profiling tools. Specifically, Android Studio’s DeviceFile Explorer can be invaluable for inspecting the filesystem of an Android device or emulator. For example, youwill use this feature to check how an app stores data in SharedPreferences or to explore an app's SQLite database.Ensure that you have the latest version of Android Studio to take advantage of all its features.
Android Studio can be downloaded from the official website at the following link:https://developer.android.com/studio
Ensure that you also install the Platform Tools, found here:https://developer.android.com/studio/releases/platform-tools
This will allow you to use tools like ADB, which are essential to testing Android applications.
Next in line is Burp Suite, an indispensable tool for any penetration tester targeting web applications. It allows youto intercept, inspect, and modify the HTTP and HTTPS requests and responses between an Android app and itsbackend servers. For instance, during a test, you can use Burp Suite to intercept a login request from an app to seeif sensitive data like passwords are being sent in plaintext. To integrate Burp Suite effectively, you will need toinstall its CA certificate into your testing device or emulator, thereby enabling it to intercept SSL-encrypted trafficwithout raising flags. Once this is done, configure your Android device's or emulator's WiFi settings to use amanual proxy that points to Burp Suite's listener (usually on TCP port 8080).
Given the extensive variety of Android devices and emulators, it is unfeasible to provide universally applicableinstructions. However, pertinent external resources are provided to assist in the completion of your configuration.
Step 1: Configuring the Burp Proxy Listener
First, let us setup the Burp Proxy Listener:
1. Initialization: Launch Burp Suite Professional and navigate to the Settings dialog through the Settingsoption:
Figure 10.1: Settings dialog
2. Tool selection: Proceed to Tools | Proxy.
3. Listener addition: Within the Proxy Listeners section, select Add.

--- Page 304 ---
4. Port binding: Navigate to the Binding tab and assign Bind to port as 8080 or another unused port of yourchoosing:
Figure 10.2: Binding tab
5. Interface selection: Choose All interfaces and confirm by clicking OK.
6. Confirmation: A dialog prompt will appear. Click Yes to proceed.
Step 2: Device proxy configuration
Next, let us configure the proxy settings on our testing device:
1. Preliminary action: Prior to configuration, disconnect the Android device from the Wi-Fi network.
2. Access network settings: Go to Settings | Network & Internet on the Android device.
3. Wi-Fi network selection: Long-press on your Wi-Fi network and choose Modify.
4. Advanced configuration: From the Advanced options menu, navigate to Proxy | Manual.
5. Hostname configuration: Set the Proxy hostname to the IP address of the computer running Burp SuiteProfessional. For example, 192.168.1.3.
6. Port configuration: Set the Proxy port to the port value designated in Step 1 for the Burp Proxy listener. In atypical setup, this is port 8080.
7. Commit changes: Tap Save.
Step 3: Installation of CA Certificate
To intercept HTTPS traffic, a CA certificate from Burp Suite Professional must be installed on the Android device.This procedure can be intricate and differs among Android devices and versions. The following is based on ageneric Android installation, but please note that the steps will vary and should be adjusted according to yourspecific device:
1. Access device settings: Navigate to your device's Settings application.
2. Security settings: Select the Security & Privacy option, followed by More Security Settings and thenEncryption & Credentials.

--- Page 305 ---
3. Certificate installation: Choose the Install a Certificate option, and subsequently, select Wi-FiCertificate.
4. Menu access: Tap the Menu icon.
5. Certificate location: Navigate to the directory where the certificate file has been saved.
6. File selection: Tap on the certificate file to select it.
7. Key store password: If prompted, input the key store password and confirm by tapping OK.
8. Certificate naming: Assign an identifiable name to the certificate.
9. Completion: Confirm the settings by tapping OK.
10. Security setup requirement: If the device has not been previously secured with a PIN, pattern, or password,you will be prompted to establish one of these security measures before proceeding.
Step 4: Configuration verification
After setting everything up, it is essential to confirm the configuration is successful:
1. Launch software: Open Burp Suite.
2. Intercept activation: Navigate to Proxy | Intercept and toggle the Intercept is off switch to 'on'.
3. Functionality test: On the Android device's browser, navigate to an HTTPS web page.
4. Result verification: If the page loads without security warnings and corresponding requests appear withinBurp Suite Professional, the configuration is successful.
Special note for Android emulators
For certain Android emulators, proxy details must be added via the emulator's settings menu, as opposed to thenative Network/Wi-Fi settings on the emulated Android device.
Beginning with Android version 7 (Nougat), it is imperative to utilize a rooted device for the installation of aCertificate Authority (CA) certificate at the system level. The process of rooting an Android device commonlyvoids the manufacturer's warranty and carries the potential risk of rendering the device inoperative. Users arestrongly encouraged to fully comprehend the associated risks prior to undertaking such actions.
Setting up the testing device itself, either an actual Android device or an emulator is another key step. Opting foran emulator via Android Studio is often recommended for its ease of control and snapshot functionalities. Theemulator allows you to mimic various devices, OS versions, and hardware characteristics, like setting the CPUarchitecture to ARM or x86. The Extended controls in the emulator enable you to simulate different networkspeeds and latency, which can be crucial when testing how an app behaves under less-than-ideal networkconditions.
Configuring the emulated device for unrestricted access is essential for thorough testing. For example, you willneed to enable Developer Options and USB Debugging. To unlock Developer Options, you typically must go tothe About Phone section under the Android settings and tap on "Build Number" multiple times. Once this hiddenmenu is accessible, you can enable USB Debugging, which will allow tools like ADB to interact with the device.This is crucial for tasks like installing APKs outside of the Google Play Store, accessing the device shell, or pullinglogs for analysis.
Now, with your tools and emulated device in place, double-check that they are all functioning in concert. Run asimple, known, vulnerable app through your setup. Use Android Studio to monitor system logs, Burp Suite tointercept a sample request, and check that your emulated device permits the installation of third-party apps. Thisverification acts as a litmus test, ensuring that you are fully equipped for the deep dive into Android applicationsecurity that lies ahead.
The penetration test

--- Page 306 ---
In the realm of mobile application security, penetration testing forms the crux of identifying vulnerabilities andpotential exploits. These guided exercises are meticulously crafted to not just expose you to but also engage youwith various vulnerabilities in Android applications. One of the first things you will encounter is unprotectedAndroid components. These could range from unguarded Activities to exposed Broadcast Receivers. For instance,using tools like drozer, you can enumerate unprotected components and then directly interact with them. Anexample of exploiting an unprotected Activity would involve invoking it via ADB, thereby bypassing any intendedsecurity measures, such as authentication mechanisms.
The testing phase is also heavily focused on the art of intercepting and manipulating network traffic. For this, BurpSuite comes into play as your primary weapon. In a typical exercise, you will set up Burp Suite as a proxy for theAndroid emulator or physical device. Once the proxy is set up, you can monitor the HTTP/S traffic between theAndroid app and its backend server. For example, you could intercept a login request and manipulate variables likeusername and password to explore the implications of weak server-side validation. This could revealvulnerabilities such as authentication bypass or, in some cases, even SQL injection if the server-side code is notadequately securing database queries.
Next on the penetration testing agenda is client-side data storage and handling. This section will teach you how tolook for insecure data storage vulnerabilities commonly exploited by attackers to exfiltrate sensitive user data.Using tools like adb shell and jadx, you can inspect the app's data directories shared preferences or even reverseengineer the APK to check if sensitive data like authentication tokens are stored in an insecure manner. Forinstance, you can use the adb shell to navigate to the app’s data directory and use Linux commands like cat to readsensitive data stored in plaintext or XML files.
For encrypted traffic, we should consider the use of Frida. You will employ Frida scripts to hook into runningapplications to inspect or alter data within the app, which even allows for the decryption of encrypted data sentover the network. For instance, you can write a Frida script to hook into an app’s SSL certificate validationfunction. By changing the function's behavior, you can make it accept any SSL certificate, effectively enabling youto use Burp Suite to intercept even SSL-pinned traffic. This is a valuable skill, especially when dealing withapplications that have implemented strong SSL pinning mechanisms to prevent network traffic interception.
By the end of these exercises, you will not only have hands-on experience in conducting penetration tests but alsopossess a deep understanding of Android-specific vulnerabilities and attack vectors. Each exercise is designed tosimulate real-world vulnerabilities, thereby preparing you for challenges you may encounter in actual Androidapplications. You will understand that penetration testing is not just about finding vulnerabilities but also aboutthinking creatively to exploit them, and, most importantly, offering viable remediation strategies to mitigate thesevulnerabilities effectively.
From analysis to identification
A successful penetration test is only half the battle. The real skill lies in sifting through the data, making sense ofthe findings, and spotting vulnerabilities.
A penetration test's utility extends beyond the mere identification of vulnerabilities; it includes the crucial analysisphase where raw data is transformed into actionable intelligence. One of the quintessential tools for this is jadx, adecompiler that reverts Dalvik Executable (DEX) files back to human-readable Java source code. With jadx, youcan dissect an Android application to reveal its core logic, thereby identifying insecure coding practices or logicflaws that could lead to vulnerabilities. For instance, if an application uses a homegrown encryption algorithm,jadx can be used to examine the Java classes responsible for encryption and decryption, allowing you to evaluatethe algorithm's robustness.
Then comes apktool, another indispensable instrument in your arsenal. Apktool enables you to delve into the veryinnards of the APK file, including its manifest file and XML resources. The AndroidManifest.xml file, inparticular, offers a wealth of information, like the app's permission model, registered components (Activities,Services, Broadcast Receivers, etc.), and intent filters. Let us consider a scenario where an app requests excessivepermissions that it does not necessarily use. This can be discovered by examining the permissions in theAndroidManifest.xml file and correlating them with the actual functionalities accessed through the app. Suchexcessive permissions could lead to privilege escalation attacks if an attacker successfully exploits othervulnerabilities within the app.

--- Page 307 ---
Frida elevates your analytical capabilities to the next level. Unlike static analysis tools like jadx and apktool, Fridaspecializes in dynamic analysis by allowing real-time interactions with the running application. You can injectcustom JavaScript code or even your own libraries to modify the app's behavior on-the-fly. For example, you canutilize Frida to hook into an app's secure data storage functions. This enables you to modify the data before it isencrypted and stored, or to intercept it as it is read into the app, effectively allowing you to evaluate how secure thedata storage mechanisms are.
Data analysis for vulnerabilities is not merely a linear process; it is an iterative cycle of static and dynamicanalyses. The combination of jadx for static analysis, apktool for resource insights, and Frida for dynamicmanipulation provides a comprehensive suite of tools for this cycle. For instance, you may first use jadx to identifysuspicious code blocks, followed by Frida to test your hypotheses in real-time, and finally verify the app's actualbehavior against its declared permissions and configurations using apktool.
The analytical phase is pivotal in any penetration testing cycle. It not only pinpoints the vulnerabilities but alsohelps in understanding the why behind them. By mastering the utilization of tools like jadx, apktool, and Frida, youenable yourself to dig deep into the core constructs of Android applications. This heightened understanding isinvaluable for not just identifying vulnerabilities but also for crafting effective remediation strategies to securemobile applications against sophisticated threats.
Understanding vulnerable Android apps
To gain a nuanced understanding of Android application security, it is often recommended to start withapplications that are intentionally insecure. These vulnerable applications serve as training grounds where you canflex your penetration testing muscles without the legal implications associated with live, real-world applications.By working on these intentionally vulnerable apps, you will be exposed to a variety of security flaws thatcommonly plague real-world applications, hence providing you with invaluable hands-on experience.
Damn Insecure and Vulnerable App
Let us begin with Damn Insecure and Vulnerable App (DIVA). This application is laden with vulnerabilities,covering a wide spectrum from basic issues like insecure data storage to advanced problems like SQL injection.DIVA is particularly beneficial for those starting their journey in mobile application security. For instance, DIVAincludes challenges that demonstrate the potential perils of storing sensitive data in cleartext within the Androidfilesystem. You can explore these vulnerabilities with the guidance of ADB commands to read the insecurelystored files. DIVA acts as a multi-faceted platform that allows learners to understand both the breadth and depth ofmobile application vulnerabilities.
DIVA can be found here:
https://github.com/payatu/diva-android
Initial preparations
Before embarking on the installation process, it is crucial to confirm that your Android Virtual Machine (VM) isoperational. The VM serves as the environment where the DIVA application will be deployed.
Choice of installation methods
Two primary methods are available for installing the DIVA application on an Android VM:
Direct drag-and-drop of the APK file into the VM interface.
Utilizing the ADB for a command-line-based installation.
For this guide, the focus will be on the more controlled, command-line-based installation using ADB.
Navigating to the APK file location using command prompt
Locating the APK with the command prompt will allow us to proceed effectively:

--- Page 308 ---

2. Navigating to the Directory: Use the Change Directory (cd) command to move to the directory where theAPK file of the DIVA application is stored. For instance, if the APK file is located in a folder namedDownloads, you would type cd Downloads.
Verifying the status of connected Android devices
To ensure that your Android VM is correctly connected and recognized by your system, execute the followingcommand:
adb devices
This command lists all Android devices that are connected and operational. If your Android VM is running, itshould appear in this list, confirming that you can proceed with the installation.
Executing the installation of the DIVA application
To initiate the installation of the DIVA application, type the following command and press Enter:
adb install diva-beta.apk
Upon successful execution, a message such as Success should be displayed in the Command Prompt, indicatingthat the DIVA application has been successfully installed on your Android VM.

Hardcoding Issues – Part 1
Before attempting to solve this challenge, it is strongly advised to consult OWASP entry about hard-codedpasswords and credentials which can be found here:
https://owasp.org/www-community/vulnerabilities/Use_of_hard-coded_password
The material will offer crucial background on the security vulnerabilities associated with hardcoding sensitiveinformation within applications.
Initiating the hardcoding challenge
On your Android device or virtual machine, tap the button labeled Hardcoding Issues - Part 1. This action willlaunch a new activity or screen, signifying the beginning of this specific challenge.
Understanding the context
The essence of this challenge revolves around the vendor key, which is hardcoded into the application. To retrievethis key, reverse engineering techniques must be applied to the application's APK file.
File conversion and extraction
In order to allow for decompilation, we should convert the APK file to a RAR file, since the APK functionsinternally in the same manner as a RAR file:
1. APK to RAR conversion: The first step in the reverse engineering process involves converting the APK fileto a RAR file. This is achieved by merely changing the file extension from .apk to .rar.
2. RAR file extraction: After the conversion, extract the contents of the RAR file. Among the extracted files,you will find a DEX file, typically named classes.dex, along with other files and directories.
Dex to Jar conversion
Once we have the Dex file, we are able to convert it to the Jar format, which we can easily decompile and read:

--- Page 310 ---
1. Command Prompt navigation: Open the Command Prompt and navigate to the folder where the extractedclasses.dex file is located.
2. Dex2Jar tool execution: Utilize the Dex2Jar tool to convert the classes.dex file to a JAR (Java Archive)file. To do this, enter the following command:
d2j-dex2jar classes.dex
After successful execution, a JAR file bearing the same name as the original DEX file will be created in the samefolder.
Decompiling the JAR file
Open the newly created JAR file using JD-GUI, a graphical utility for Java decompilation. This tool will allow youto inspect the application's source code.
Retrieving the hardcoded vendor key
By retrieving the hardcoded vendor key, we are able to compromise the application and its secrets:
1. Vendor key identification: Within the decompiled source code, locate the hardcoded vendor's secret key.
2. Key usage: Input this vendor's secret key into the appropriate field in the application's interface to gainaccess.
If you successfully enter the vendor's secret key and gain access, you have effectively completed the HardcodingIssues - Part 1 challenge. This challenge serves as a practical illustration of the security risks associated withhardcoding sensitive information.

Insecure Data Storage – Part 2
This challenge is akin to the previous one (Insecure Data Storage - Part 1), but with a twist: the credentials arestored in a different location. Specifically, this time the application stores the credentials in a database named ids2,within a table called myuser.
Preliminary credential storage
Before proceeding with the challenge, input and save credentials within the application. This action establishes aset of data that you will later seek to locate and examine for security vulnerabilities.
Using SQLite3 for database access
In order to properly read the SQLite database, we need to use the SQLite3 command-line utitliy:
1. Introduction to SQLite3: SQLite3 is a command-line utility found in the Android platform tools folder. Thisutility allows for the direct interaction with SQLite databases, which is the type of database used in thischallenge.
2. Initiating the Linux (Android) shell: Open a Linux (Android) shell, which could either be an ADB shell oranother form of command-line interface that allows interaction with the Android device:
adb shell
3. Launching SQLite3 and Accessing the Database: Navigate to the directory where the SQLite3 tool resides,and initiate SQLite3 with the specific database you want to examine. For this challenge, the command wouldbe:

4. Listing Available Tables: Once inside the SQLite3 interface and connected to the ids2 database, list all tablesin the database to locate the myuser table where the credentials are stored:
sqlite>.tables
By running this command, you will get a list of tables available in that particular database. Look for themyuser table among these.
5. Extracting All Data from Table: Once you have identified the myuser table, extract all the data from thetable with a simple SQL command:
SELECT * FROM myuser;
6. Exiting SQLite3: After you've completed your examination, you can exit SQLite3 and return to yourprevious shell using the following command:

--- Page 312 ---
sqlite>.exit
Successfully accessing the database and identifying where the credentials are stored confirms the completion of theInsecure Data Storage - Part 2 challenge. This exercise demonstrates the risks associated with insecure datastorage in different types of databases.
Insecure Data Storage – Part 3
This challenge is an extension of the insecure data storage challenges series. The unique aspect of this particularexercise is that the application stores user credentials in a temporary file, as opposed to a database or SharedPreferences.
Initial data entry
The first step involves entering credentials into the application. Make sure to save these credentials, as they willserve as the data set you'll aim to locate during the challenge.
Identifying the temporary storage method
According to the challenge source code found in InsecureDataStorage3Activity.class, the credentials are stored in atemporary file. This form of storage is considered insecure, especially for sensitive information such as usercredentials.
The following line of code is used to create the temp file:
file = File.createTempFile(“uinfo”, “tmp”, file);
The official Android documentation for the createTempFile method(https://developer.android.com/reference/java/io/File#createTempFile(java.lang.String,%20java.lang.String,%20java.io.File) states that the method takes three arguments: a string prefix, a string suffix, and a directory.Therefore, we can assume that our log file is named something like uinfo234434temp.
Accessing the temporary file via shell
In order to access the temporary file, we need to open a shell on the device. This is usually ADB, for our purposes:
1. Opening a shell session: To begin, open a shell session that allows command-line interaction with theAndroid device or virtual machine. This could be an ADB shell or another Terminal that can communicatewith the Android operating system:
adb shell
2. Navigating to the temporary file location: The next step is to navigate to the directory where the temporaryfile is stored. The specific location may vary depending on the application and how it is configured. Use shellcommands like cd and ls to navigate through directories and locate the file.
An analysis of the source code for the class in this exercise reveals that the file is stored in the applicationdata directory. In our case, this would be:
/data/data/Jakhar.aseem.diva.
3. Examining the Temporary File: Once you have identified the temporary file, use commands like cat todisplay its contents. This will allow you to confirm whether the credentials have been stored in plaintext,highlighting the insecure storage method:
cat /data/data/Jakhar.aseem.diva/uinfo234234234234tmp
If you successfully locate the temporary file and find that it contains plaintext credentials, you have effectivelycompleted the Insecure Data Storage - Part 3 challenge. This scenario serves as a case study on the vulnerabilitiesassociated with storing sensitive data in temporary files.

Input Validation Issues – Part 1
This challenge is designed to expose the vulnerabilities associated with improper input validation, particularlyfocusing on SQL Injection. SQL Injection is a code injection technique that exploits a vulnerability in anapplication's software by manipulating SQL queries.
Initial input tests
In order to determine the likelihood of vulnerability, we should first test the input fields with single and doublequotes to identify injection points:
1. Single quote test: Initially, enter a single quote (') into the application's text field. Observe the result, whichwill likely manifest as a toast message or some other form of feedback. This step is crucial for identifyinghow the application handles special characters in SQL queries.
2. Double single quote test: Next, enter two single quotes ('') and observe the result. Comparing the outcomesof these two tests can provide insights into the application's input validation mechanisms.
Understanding output variations

--- Page 314 ---
Pay close attention to the toast messages or other feedback mechanisms. The discrepancies in outputs when usinga single quote versus double quotes can reveal underlying SQL Injection vulnerabilities.
Exploiting input validation flaws
Once you have established that your inputs are affecting the application in some way, it is time to manipulate thetext field further. Use a typical SQL Injection payload like ' OR '1' == '1 to test the system's vulnerability.
The payload ' OR '1' == '1 is designed to always evaluate as true, potentially allowing unauthorized access orrevealing sensitive information. Use this payload in the input field, and you should receive all the entries in thedatabase.
If you successfully manipulate the application's behavior using the SQL Injection payload, you have effectivelycompleted the Input Validation Issues - Part 1 challenge. This exercise serves as a practical example of the risksassociated with poor input validation and SQL Injection vulnerabilities.
