Access Control Issues – Part 1
The challenge focuses on access control vulnerabilities related to Android activities. Specifically, you are taskedwith determining whether it is possible to directly access the activity that displays API credentials, bypassing anyintended access control mechanisms.

--- Page 315 ---
Initial legitimate access
The application features a View API Credentials button that leads to the activity displaying the API credentials.Accessing this activity through the button is the "legal" or "intended" way to view these credentials. Risk arises ifit is discovered to be possible to access the activity without going through the intended endpoints, as thoseendpoints could be secured to only all authorized users to gain access.
Identifying the Target Activity
In order to identify the vulnerable endpoint, we need to determine the exact activity name which is associated withour target:
1. Utilizing Logcat: The Android Logcat utility can help you identify the name of the activity that you will betrying to access directly. Open an Android shell and run the Logcat command:
logcat
2. Activity name extraction: With Logcat running, click the View API Credentials button within theapplication. This action will generate a log entry, revealing the name of the activity that follows. Take note ofthis activity name for the next steps.
In our case, the activity is revealed to be:
Jakhar.aseem.diva/.APICredsActivity
Attempting unauthorized access
Now that you have the name of the activity, try to access it directly using the ADB activity manager command:
adb shell am start -n jakhar.aseem.diva/.APICredsActivity
Replace jakhar.aseem.diva/.APICredsActivity with the actual name of the activity you obtained from Logcat.
Evaluating the access control mechanism
If you are able to access the activity and view the API credentials without any authentication or other restrictions,it confirms a significant access control vulnerability. This also implies that other applications could potentiallyexploit this vulnerability to gain unauthorized access to the API credentials.
Successfully bypassing the intended access control mechanisms and directly accessing the API Credentials activitymarks the completion of the Access Control Issues - Part 1 challenge. This exercise illuminates the potential risksassociated with inadequate access control measures.
Access Control Issues – Part 2
The goal of this challenge is to investigate more complex access control issues, specifically through codedecompilation and parameter manipulation. The target is an activity that displays API credentials, much like theprevious challenge.
Decompiling the application
APKTool is a utility for reverse engineering Android APK files. Open the command line and enter the followingcommand to decompile the application:
apktool_2.4.1.jar d diva-beta.apk
This will create a folder with the application's name in the same directory as the APK file.
Analyzing the AndroidManifest.XML file
Navigate to the newly created folder and open the AndroidManifest.XML file. This file often contains valuableinformation about the application's activities and permissions.
Notice that our XML file contains valuable information about actions and permissions:

--- Page 316 ---
<activity            android:name=".APICreds2Activity"            android:label="@string/apic2_label" >            <intent-filter>                <action android:name="jakhar.aseem.diva.action.VIEW_CREDS2" />                <category android:name="android.intent.category.DEFAULT" />            </intent-filter>        </activity>
Examining the Java code files
Open the relevant Java code file(s) for the target activity. Look for any unique parameters or attributes related toaccess control.
The class file in question is APICreds2Activity.class. Specifically, the following block of code is suspicious:boolean bcheck = i.getBooleanExtra(getString(R.string.chk_pin), true);
Locating the chk_pin parameter
The Java code may refer to a specific parameter, such as chk_pin. Note this parameter, as it might be a controlcheck for accessing the API credentials.
Since we have decompiled the code, this value should be filled in with a parameter value. If we search for thatparameter in the file R.class, we can find the name of the parameter in question. However, it should be noted thatby analyzing the original source code, we can easily determine that the value is R.string.chk_pin.
Inspecting the strings.XML File
Navigate to the /res/values/strings.xml file within the decompiled application folder. Inspect this file todetermine the value or role of the chk_pin parameter. In our case, this value is check_pin.
Attempting unauthorized access
Use the ADB to attempt launching the activity directly, incorporating the details you have gleaned from thedecompiled code. The command may look something like:
adb shell am start -n jakhar.aseem.diva/.APICreds2Activity -a jakhar.aseem.diva.action.View_CREDS2 --ez check_pin false
Here, --ez check_pin false is used to disable the chk_pin check, based on the information found in thedecompiled code.
Verifying the security flaw
If the API credentials appear without requiring any authentication or checks, you have successfully bypassed theapplication's access control mechanisms.
Successfully exploiting the access control mechanisms to view the API credentials confirms the completion of theAccess Control Issues - Part 2 challenge. This exercise demonstrates the potential vulnerabilities stemming frominadequate access control and the importance of secure code practices.
Access Control Issues – Part 3
The central objective of this challenge is to explore access control vulnerabilities specifically related to contentproviders in Android applications. The case in point is an activity that involves notes, which are supposedlyprotected by a PIN.
Understanding the supposed security measure
According to the challenge description, accessing the notes within the application requires a PIN forauthentication. This security measure is what you will aim to bypass.

--- Page 317 ---
Bypassing access control with Content Provider
Android's Content Provider allows for the sharing of data between applications. In this case, the Content Provideris serving the notes that are supposed to be protected by a PIN. You can query this Content Provider directly fromthe ADB to see if the notes can be accessed without the PIN. Use the following command:
adb shell content query --uri content://jakhar.aseem.diva.provider.notesprovider/notes/
This command queries the specific Content Provider responsible for serving notes.
Evaluating the security implications
If the query returns the notes without requiring a PIN, then you have successfully bypassed the application’s accesscontrol mechanisms. This is a critical vulnerability, as it allows unauthorized access to supposedly secure data.
Successfully querying the Content Provider to access PIN-protected notes marks the completion of the AccessControl Issues – Part 3 challenge. This exposes the potential security risks associated with improper accesscontrol, particularly in the context of Content Providers.

Input Validation Issues – Part 3
The primary objective of this challenge is to assess the application's resilience to input validation flaws byattempting to crash it. Unlike other challenges where the goal is to bypass security measures or extractinformation, this challenge is all about testing the application's robustness (or lack thereof).
Preparing for input testing
Before proceeding, note that the goal here is not to gain unauthorized access or extract data but to crash theapplication. This is generally considered a Denial-of-Service (DoS) vulnerability.
Executing the test
Navigate to the relevant input field in the application. Enter an extremely long string of random characters. Theobjective is to provide input that the application is not equipped to handle, thereby causing it to crash.
Here, the term long is subjective and depends on the application's handling capacity. Start with hundreds ofcharacters and scale up as needed.
Observing the outcome
If the application crashes because of the input, you have successfully demonstrated a vulnerability in its inputvalidation mechanisms. Crashes can manifest in different ways, such as the application freezing, closing abruptly,or even triggering an error message.
Successfully causing the application to crash confirms that you have completed the Input Validation Issues - Part 3challenge. This exercise showcases the potential consequences of poor input validation, which can lead toapplication crashes and, in a broader context, denial-of-service attacks.
InsecureBankv2
Another rich resource is InsecureBankv2. It simulates a banking application and is designed to be replete withsecurity holes that commonly affect financial apps, such as issues with SSL pinning or vulnerable authenticationmechanisms. The app helps learners comprehend how hackers can exploit banking applications to commit fraud orsteal personal information. For example, InsecureBankv2 often has broken SSL configurations. As a tester, you canutilize tools like Burp Suite to intercept and alter SSL traffic, thereby demonstrating the importance of robust SSLpinning in financial apps.
GoatDroid
Then there is OWASP GoatDroid, sponsored by the globally recognized OWASP Foundation. This educational toolis designed to not only allow you to exploit vulnerabilities but also to deepen your understanding of them withsupplementary lessons and quizzes. For instance, GoatDroid includes a vulnerability related to insecurecommunication. The app contains lessons explaining how Man-in-the-Middle attacks work, along with quizzes totest your understanding. After exploiting the vulnerability yourself using Burp Suite, you can solidify yourknowledge through the associated lesson.
The diverse set of vulnerabilities provided by these applications equips you with the necessary practical knowledgeto deal with a variety of real-world scenarios. In addition, these platforms often come with well-documentedsolutions and community support. This offers a balanced approach to learning, combining theoreticalunderstanding with hands-on practice, thus allowing you to comprehend not just the how but also the why behindmobile application vulnerabilities. By practicing on these intentionally vulnerable Android applications, you areessentially preparing yourself for identifying and remedying security flaws in real-world applications, therebymaking you an asset in the increasingly critical field of mobile application security. The walkthrough of DIVA

--- Page 319 ---
should serve as a guide to your hands-on testing. Compromising InsecureBankv2 and GoatDroid are left as anexercise for the reader.
Securing the future
Identifying vulnerabilities is crucial, but the goal is to patch these gaps. The final segment will provide acomprehensive overview of remediation strategies. From understanding secure coding principles to leveragingAndroid's built-in security features, you will acquire the knowledge to not just find but also fix and fortify.
The lifecycle of mobile application security does not end with the identification of vulnerabilities; it extends toremediation and ongoing maintenance to thwart future threats. Secure coding principles form the bedrock of anyremediation strategy. Take, for example, the principle of Least Privilege, which dictates that an application shouldrequest only the permissions it absolutely needs. By adhering to this principle, you reduce the potential attacksurface. In Android development, this translates to specifying only necessary permissions in theAndroidManifest.xml file. You can further enforce this by utilizing runtime permission checks to requestpermissions only when a particular functionality requires that permission be used.
Another cornerstone of secure coding is input validation. Many vulnerabilities arise from an application blindlytrusting the incoming data, which could be in the form of user input, data received from server, or intents fromother applications. Always validate, sanitize, and, if necessary, escape the data before processing it. For instance, ifan application employs SQL databases for data storage, proper parameterized queries should be used to mitigateSQL Injection attacks. Android’s native SQLite API, when used correctly, provides parameterized query methodsthat help avoid this vulnerability.
Android also comes equipped with a variety of built-in security features that can be leveraged for strongerapplication security. Android Keystore, for instance, allows you to generate cryptographic keys that are stored in acontainer, making it more difficult to extract from the device. These keys can be used for data encryption anddecryption, secure authentication to servers, and more. Securely implementing cryptographic algorithms is non-trivial, and relying on proven Android security libraries and APIs can avoid a lot of pitfalls. For example, considerutilizing Android’s KeyChain API for secure certificate storage instead of a custom solution that may not fullyprotect against various attack vectors.
Beyond these measures, ongoing monitoring and maintenance are crucial. Security is not a one-off process but acontinuous cycle. This involves keeping abreast of updates in Android security best practices, promptly updatingyour application's libraries and SDKs, and regular re-testing of your application for new types of vulnerabilities.Automated tools like dependency scanners can alert you to outdated libraries that may have known vulnerabilities.Tools like Google’s Play Protect also play a vital role, continually scanning and verifying Android applicationsagainst known threats.
In summary, identifying vulnerabilities is a critical but not terminal stage in the security lifecycle of an Androidapplication. A multidimensional approach, incorporating secure coding practices and leveraging native Androidsecurity features, is essential for robust application security. Coupled with a sustained commitment to keeping up-to-date with the ever-evolving landscape of mobile security, you equip yourself not just to identify but also torectify and bolster the fortifications of your Android applications.
Conclusion
Mobile security, particularly on the Android platform, is an evolving battlefield. As you conclude this immersivechapter, you are no longer just a passive observer. Armed with practical experience, you are ready to take onchallenges, ensuring the Android ecosystem is safer and more resilient against threats.
In the next chapter, we will take a look at attack iOS applications.
Points to remember
Understanding the setup and configuration of a penetration testing environment for Android applications.
Recognizing common vulnerabilities in Android apps, including insecure data storage and input validationissues.

--- Page 320 ---
Familiarity with tools like ADB and Burp Proxy for examining and exploiting vulnerabilities.
Practical application of skills in real-world scenarios to uncover and address security flaws.
Adapting to the evolving landscape of Android security and staying informed about the latest threats andupdates.

CHAPTER 11Hands-on Practice: Vulnerable iOS Apps
Introduction
Apple's iOS, a significant player in the mobile market, is renowned for its robust security features like codesigning, sandboxing, and hardware-based encryption. Yet, vulnerabilities persist, exemplified by exploits like theMasque Attack, which exploited trust to replace legitimate apps with malicious ones. This chapter dives into iOSapplication security, debunking the myth of invincibility and providing a hands-on guide to uncovering anddefending against potential threats. It emphasizes the importance of familiarizing oneself with iOS developmenttools like Objective-C or Swift, Xcode, and the iOS SDK for both development and security analysis. Readers willexplore static analysis using tools like Clang Static Analyzer, and dynamic analysis through Frida and Cycript,which allow real-time examination and manipulation of running applications. This journey through iOS security isnot just theoretical; it is a practical hands-on approach to understanding and mitigating the vulnerabilities that canexist even within tightly controlled ecosystems, preparing readers to navigate and fortify the complex terrain ofiOS application security.
Structure
In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:
Crafting the testing ground
Peeling back the layers: Analysis and identification
Fortifying the Bastions
Vulnerable iOS apps: Learning from built-in flaws
iGoat
Damn Vulnerable iOS App
Objectives
In this chapter you will delve into the world of iOS application security with a focus on practical, hands-onexperience. This journey through the realm of Apple's iOS, a system known for its robust security features, aims touncover and understand the vulnerabilities that persist beneath its surface. You will learn to set up a testingenvironment using tools like Xcode, the iOS Simulator, Burp Suite, and even jailbroken devices. Throughexercises with vulnerable apps like iGoat and Damn Vulnerable iOS App (DVIAv2), you will explore varioustypes of security flaws from side-channel data leaks to insecure data storage methods like Plist, UserDefaults, andKeychain access. By engaging in reverse engineering and analyzing strings in iOS applications, you will gain adeeper understanding of how vulnerabilities can be exploited and, more importantly, how they can be mitigated.This chapter is designed to equip you with the skills and insights necessary to navigate the complex terrain of iOSapplication security, providing you with the knowledge to identify, understand, and fortify against the potentialthreats within this sophisticated mobile environment.
Crafting the testing ground

--- Page 322 ---
Every craftsman requires the right set of tools and an apt workspace. For an aspiring iOS penetration tester, thistranslates to a meticulously crafted testing environment. Here is a breakdown of what that involves.
Xcode
Apple's integrated development environment is an all-in-one suite for iOS application development and debugging.While developers use it to craft apps, penetration testers can use it to dissect them. It is a MacOS-only program, soyou will need access to a Mac computer.
Embarking on the journey of iOS penetration testing mandates not just theoretical knowledge but also a tailor-made, practical environment where you can unleash, test, and hone your skills. The first piece of the puzzle isXcode, Apple's official IDE for iOS development. Xcode offers a variety of features like debugging, profiling, andinterface builder, which are equally useful for a penetration tester. For instance, its LLDB debugger can be utilizedto inspect the program state at any given time, allowing you to understand how data is being handled in memoryand uncovering vulnerabilities like buffer overflows or race conditions. However, note that Xcode requires a Macenvironment and cannot be run on other operating systems.
iOS Simulator
Bundled with Xcode, this tool simulates various iOS devices and environments, providing a sandbox to test andobserve application behaviors without needing an array of physical devices.
Contrary to what the name suggests, it is not a traditional emulator but rather simulates the software environmentof the device, running your applications on macOS using the iOS runtime. The iOS Simulator allows you tosimulate a variety of hardware types (iPhones, iPads, and so on) and iOS versions. While it does not emulate thehardware, which means certain types of vulnerabilities might not manifest as they would on a real device, it isinvaluable for preliminary testing and functional checks. Moreover, it allows for easy debugging and introspectioninto running applications using the tools provided within Xcode.
Burp Suite
It intercepts network traffic, allowing you to inspect and modify requests and responses, granting insights intopotentially insecure communications.
When your focus shifts to network-level vulnerabilities, Burp Suite emerges as the go-to tool. Acting as a proxyserver between the mobile application and the backend services, Burp Suite captures all HTTP/HTTPS traffic. Itallows you to inspect requests and responses and even modify them on the fly, thereby enabling tests forvulnerabilities like Insecure Direct Object References (IDOR) or Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF). Oneadvanced feature to note is Burp's ability to perform automated scans for common web vulnerabilities, althoughthis usually requires the professional version of the tool:
Jailbroken iOS device
When it comes to testing iOS, there is often no substitute for the real thing. It is essential that anyone testing iOSapps have access to both a Mac computer and a physical, jailbroken iOS device.
While simulators are useful, sometimes, there is no alternative to testing on real hardware. A jailbroken iOS deviceoffers advantages like running unsigned code, which allows you to inspect and modify the application andoperating system in ways that are not possible otherwise. This is vital when looking for vulnerabilities that arespecific to the hardware or low-level operating system features. A jailbroken device can be enhanced with toolslike Cydia, which enables the installation of additional debugging and reverse engineering tools not permitted byApple, such as gdb or the GNU Debugger.
However, setting up a jailbroken device for testing involves risks, including bricking the device or running intostability issues. Moreover, because jailbreaking often exploits vulnerabilities in iOS, you are inherently weakeningthe security posture of the device. Therefore, it is advisable to use a dedicated iOS device for testing purposes,separate from your day-to-day phone.

--- Page 323 ---
Finally, proper configuration of your testing tools and environments is key. For instance, if you are using an iOSSimulator, make sure to simulate the exact iOS version and device type that your target application supports oraims to run on. Additionally, go into the settings and enable the necessary permissions and capabilities that yourtesting might require. If you are testing an application that makes use of the device’s camera or GPS, these willneed to be appropriately configured in the simulator or on the actual device. In the case of a real device, this couldmean jailbreaking, followed by installing specific utilities that allow you to manipulate these hardware features.
A well-configured testing environment is more than a luxury; it is a necessity. It empowers you to explore, debug,and challenge applications, laying bare their vulnerabilities. Each tool you set up is an extension of your testingcapabilities, and each configuration change you make fine-tunes your ability to expose security lapses withprecision. So, invest time in setting up your environment; the dividends in saved time and enhanced capabilitiesdown the line are well worth it.
Peeling back the layers: Analysis and identification
The second half of penetration testing often involves a nuanced analysis of data collected during the exploitationphase. Your tools are as critical here as in the reconnaissance and penetration steps. Each tool offers uniquecapabilities for diving deep into the application, from code-level insights to runtime manipulations.
Hopper is one such tool that enables the disassembly and decompilation of the iOS application binary. Its GraphView can be remarkably beneficial for understanding the control flow of complicated functions. For example, letus say you are looking into a login function of an app. Using Hopper's features, you can examine the conditionalbranches and pinpoint where the application checks for user credentials. You can also look for hard-codedcryptographic keys or unusual algorithms that could be a point of vulnerability. Moreover, Hopper allows you tomodify the compiled code directly, enabling you to change specific instructions to study their impact, perhapsbypassing a license check or disabling encryption algorithms for closer inspection.
Ghidra, an open-source tool developed by the National Security Agency (NSA), further enhances this analyticalcapability. Apart from its general reverse engineering functions like disassembly and decompilation, Ghidra offersrobust scripting capabilities. You could write a script to find all instances of a specific API call that you deeminsecure, thus streamlining the vulnerability identification process. Its ability to support various processorinstruction sets also makes it versatile for cross-platform analysis. One of Ghidra's standout features is functiongraphing, which provides a visual representation of the control flow, making it easier to spot potential areas whereimproper input validation or other common programming mistakes might occur.
Frida specializes in dynamic analysis, unlike the static analysis focus of Hopper and Ghidra. It serves as a SwissArmy knife for real-time manipulation of application behavior. For instance, if an iOS application uses SSLpinning to restrict its communication to a particular server, Frida can dynamically alter the SSL pinning validationlogic at runtime, allowing you to intercept the application's encrypted communications. The flexibility of Fridacomes from its scripting capabilities. You can write JavaScript code that Frida injects into the running process,giving you control to modify function arguments, log specific calls, or even change return values on the fly.
Furthermore, Frida can be used in conjunction with other tools to enhance their capabilities. For example, you canuse it alongside Burp Suite to modify HTTP headers dynamically or change POST parameters before they reachthe server. This real-time modification can be extremely useful for understanding how minor changes in the datapackets affect the application behavior and could potentially trigger vulnerabilities like SQL injection or commandinjection on the server-side component of the app.
It is essential to apply these tools in a concerted manner for a holistic analysis. Static analysis tools like Hopperand Ghidra give you a broad view of potential code-level vulnerabilities. In contrast, Frida allows you to interactwith the application in real-time, providing a dynamic perspective. Therefore, a balanced approach combiningstatic and dynamic analysis will likely yield the most comprehensive set of vulnerabilities. Once identified, thesevulnerabilities can then be classified based on their severity and impact, setting the stage for the subsequent phasesof reporting and remediation.
The analysis and identification phase is a crucial part of the penetration testing lifecycle. It is not just about findingvulnerabilities but understanding them in-depth. Tools like Hopper, Ghidra, and Frida are invaluable for thispurpose, each offering a unique set of features catering to different aspects of the analysis. Whether it isdisassembling a binary to understand its control flow or dynamically manipulating application behavior to confirm

--- Page 324 ---
a vulnerability, the choice and effective usage of these tools are key to a successful and comprehensive penetrationtest.
Fortifying the Bastions
Identifying vulnerabilities is an exercise in foresight; fixing them is an exercise in prudence. This section aims totransform your vulnerability findings into actionable remediation strategies, ensuring the application is not justresilient but also resistant to future attacks.
One of the most effective ways to patch vulnerabilities is to adopt secure coding practices right from thedevelopment stage. For instance, to mitigate SQL Injection attacks, you can implement parameterized queries.Apple's Core Data, when used correctly, offers robust support for SQL queries, including prepared statements thatare immune to SQL injection. This not only adds a layer of defense but also simplifies the codebase, making iteasier to maintain. The OWASP Secure Coding Practices Checklist can serve as a starting point to identify thecontrols you need to implement, such as input validation, secure authentication, and proper session management.
Another essential remediation strategy is the application of stringent security controls within the app. Role-basedaccess control (RBAC) is a classic example where you can define different levels of permissions for different userroles, thus limiting access to sensitive parts of the application to authorized personnel only. Utilizing features suchas Apple's Touch ID or Face ID for additional authentication can also enhance app security. Implementing thesecontrols significantly minimizes the attack surface, thereby reducing the risk of unauthorized data access orfunctional misuse.
Leveraging iOS's built-in security features can substantially simplify the remediation process. The iOS keychain,for example, is a secure storage mechanism for sensitive information like user credentials or cryptographic keys.By storing this information in the keychain rather than in plaintext within the app, you effectively mitigate therisks associated with data leakage. Likewise, iOS’s Data Protection APIs can encrypt files at rest using hardware-based encryption, offering another layer of security against potential attackers.
Moreover, security is not just about the codebase or the application layer. It also extends to the communicationchannels. Enforcing strong SSL pinning techniques, for example, can safeguard against Man-in-the-Middle(MitM) attacks. iOS natively supports SSL pinning, and implementing it involves just a few lines of code, but thereturn on investment in terms of security is substantial. This ensures that the app communicates only with thedesignated server, thereby eliminating the possibility of data interception.
Beyond these standard remediation strategies, post-compromise forensic readiness should not be overlooked.Implement logging mechanisms that can capture security-relevant events without recording sensitive userinformation. This data can be invaluable in understanding how, when, and where a security incident does occur,enabling faster and more effective incident response.
Fortifying an application against identified vulnerabilities is a multi-dimensional task that involves secure codingpractices, stringent internal controls, and optimal utilization of iOS's built-in security features. The goal is to createa fortified bastion that is not just patched up but is resilient to future attacks. Remember, security is not a one-timeexercise but a continual process. By integrating these remediation strategies into the development lifecycle, youwill be well-equipped to tackle not just the vulnerabilities you have identified but also to prepare for the evolvingthreat landscape.
Vulnerable iOS apps: Learning from built-in flaws
Deliberately vulnerable applications offer a safe yet realistic playground. Learning the ropes of iOS penetrationtesting is best done in a controlled environment that intentionally exposes you to vulnerabilities mimicking real-world scenarios.
Setting up your iOS device
Before diving into vulnerability assessments and penetration testing, it is crucial to establish a suitable testingenvironment on an iOS device. While simulators can provide a rudimentary layer of assessment, using a realdevice running iOS 16 or higher offers a more realistic and comprehensive testing landscape. There are numerous

--- Page 325 ---
tutorials available that detail the steps to set up a secure pen-testing environment on iOS, and these can serve asyour foundation for more specialized work.
The foundational step to initiate mobile security testing on an iPhone involves establishing a reliable connectionbetween the phone and a computer. Ensure that the computer, the iPhone, and the connecting USB cable are ingood working condition. To establish the connection, physically connect the phone to the computer via the USBcable. During the connection process, a Trust This Computer dialog box should appear on the iPhone, requiringuser affirmation.
In certain instances, the pop-up may not automatically appear, and manual intervention might be necessary. Forexample, on macOS systems, you may need to open the Finder application, navigate to the connected iPhone, andclick the Trust button to prompt the trust dialog box on the iPhone itself.
If you encounter connectivity issues or trust-related glitches, a factory reset may be necessary to resolve suchproblems. During the reconfiguration, it is advisable to set up the iPhone as a new device to eliminate the potentialcarryover of personal data or settings that could interfere with the testing process. You can associate the devicewith a new iCloud account or use an existing one. However, avoid restoring from backups to ensure that the deviceremains a pristine test environment devoid of personal or sensitive data.
After successfully triggering and accepting the Trust This Computer dialog on the iPhone, validate the connectionbetween the iPhone and the computer. It is imperative that this connection is stable and trustworthy, as many of thesubsequent actions, such as jailbreaking and other testing activities, rely on this interface. Once the trust andconnectivity are confirmed, proceed to the next step, which is achieving root privileges on the iPhone.
Root access is pivotal for an in-depth security assessment, offering unrestricted permissions to modify the filesystem, among other functionalities. Achieving this can be done through various jailbreaking techniques, thechoice of which often hinges on the iPhone's hardware and iOS version. For demonstration purposes, let usconsider the older iPhone SE running on iOS 13.5.1. A suitable jailbreaking tool for this hardware-softwarecombination is CheckRa1n.
CheckRa1n leverages a hardware-based exploit, making it compatible with a variety of older iPhone models.Initially, download the CheckRa1n application from its official website onto your computer. Although it is possibleto run CheckRa1n inside a Virtual Machine (VM), doing so may lead to unexpected issues; hence, a directinstallation on macOS or Linux is recommended.
After launching CheckRa1n, connect the iPhone to the computer. The application should recognize the device anddisplay its iOS version. Since the iOS version 13.5.1 was not officially supported at the time of this writing, it isnecessary to go to the options in CheckRa1n and enable the Allow untested iOS versions setting.
With the untested iOS version option enabled, proceed by clicking Start in CheckRa1n. Follow the on-screeninstructions carefully to initiate the jailbreaking process. Typically, the process should be completed within acouple of minutes, granting root access to the device. However, it is worth noting that CheckRa1n offers a semi-tethered jailbreak. This implies that the jailbreak will only be active until the device is rebooted. Post-reboot, youwill need to repeat the jailbreaking process to regain root access.
By following these steps, you will have prepared an iPhone for comprehensive security testing, with the elevatedprivileges that are crucial for probing deeper into the iOS ecosystem for vulnerabilities and weaknesses.
Once your iOS device is successfully jailbroken, one of the first applications you will notice is Cydia. Cydia servesas a specialized package manager, granting access to a range of software packages and tools not available on non-rooted devices. On opening Cydia for the first time, you may encounter notifications regarding missing packages.If this happens, select the option to Upgrade All to ensure that all package dependencies are satisfied.
The first tool to focus on installing is OpenSSH, which provides secure shell access to your iOS device. Toaccomplish this, navigate to the Search tab at the bottom of the Cydia interface and type OpenSSH. Install thepackage. To confirm the successful installation of SSH, open the iOS Settings app, then go to General | WiFi, andtap on the network to which you are connected to note down the IP address. Subsequently, use the terminal on yourcomputer to run the command ssh root@<iPhone's IP Address> with the default password alpine. It is stronglyadvised to change this password immediately upon successful login by executing the passwd command.

--- Page 326 ---
AppSync Unified is another essential package that allows for the installation of custom or unsigned iOSapplications. To install this package, tap on the Sources tab in Cydia, followed by Edit at the top-right corner andthen "Add" at the top-left corner. Insert the URL https://cydia.angelxwind.net/ as the source. After adding it,return to Cydia's main screen, go to Search, and find AppSync Unified to install it.
Frida is an invaluable tool for dynamic analysis and instrumentation of applications. To install Frida, again go toSources in Cydia, click Edit and then Add, and input the source URL https://build.frida.re. After this, navigate toSearch, type Frida, and proceed with the installation. In this example, the option chosen was pre-A12 iOS, which isappropriate for older iPhone models.
Installing the iOS Reverse Engineering Toolkit (iRET) can be more involved. Currently, iRET may not workright out-of-the-box; further configuration or dependencies might be required. To proceed with its installation, SSHinto the iOS device and execute the following series of commands:
wget --no-check-certificate https://www.veracode.com/sites/default/files/Resources/Tools/iRETTool.zip
apt-get install unzip
unzip iRETTool.zip
cd iRET-Tool/
dpkg -i iRET.debkillall SpringBoard
If disconnected, SSH back into the device.
uicache
After executing these commands, you should see the iRET application icon on your iOS device. If it does notopen, it is likely that some dependencies are missing, which will be addressed in subsequent updates.
It is vital to regularly update the toolset as security testing on iOS evolves. Each tool serves a distinct purpose andmay require specific configurations or updates to function optimally. For example, Frida and AppSync Unified areoften updated to provide compatibility with the latest iOS versions or to include new features, which necessitatesregular checks for updates in their respective Cydia repositories.
Installing essential development and reverse engineering tools on macOS
In the domain of mobile security assessment and reverse engineering, the macOS platform holds significantrelevance, especially for iOS-focused endeavors. While Kali NetHunter equips you with a robust arsenal forAndroid assessment, macOS becomes indispensable for its iOS counterpart. A well-configured macOSenvironment allows you to delve into the iOS architecture more effectively. Let us discuss the essential tools forsetting up your macOS for iOS assessments.
Xcode is the primary development environment for macOS, providing everything you need for developing appsfor Apple's ecosystem. It includes an advanced interface builder, integrated version control, and extensive libraries,among other features. Xcode can be downloaded directly from the Apple Store. Notably, it also provides an iOSsimulator, a helpful utility for loading and running applications on virtual iOS devices. Due to its large size, thedownload may take a considerable amount of time. Once installed, make sure you also install its command-lineutilities by running xcode-select --install in the terminal.
Frida is a toolkit for dynamic instrumentation of code. For macOS, Python3 should be already installed by default.If not, you can download it from Python's official website. Next, make sure you have pip3 installed; if it ismissing, it can be installed via the Python3 package manager. Installation of Frida is as straightforward as runningpip3 install frida-tools in the terminal. Verify its installation by connecting your iOS device via USB and runningfrida-ps -U. This command should list the running processes on the connected iOS device.
iFunBox is a veteran tool used for browsing the iOS file system. It is old but reliable. Navigate to iFunBox'sofficial website and download the macOS version. Installation is straightforward; drag and drop the downloaded

--- Page 327 ---
file into the Applications folder. Once installed, connect your iOS device via USB and use iFunBox to browse itsfile system. It's a useful tool for transferring files and backups.
Ghidra is a powerful open-source software reverse engineering tool developed by the National Security Agency.Download Ghidra from its official website; it comes as a ZIP file containing a self-contained Java application.Before running Ghidra, ensure that the latest version of Java Development Kit (JDK) is installed. Navigate to theGhidra directory via terminal and execute ./ghidraRun to start the tool. To reverse engineer an iOS application,locate its .ipa file, rename it to .zip, and unzip it. Inside, you will find a .app file that can be loaded into Ghidraafter creating a new project.
Refer to the following figure:
Figure 11.1: Ghidra logo
Objection is a runtime mobile exploration toolkit, powered by Frida, useful for injecting into running applications.Its installation is similar to Frida and can be achieved by running pip3 install objection in the terminal. To verify,typing objection in the terminal should display the help menu, indicating a successful installation.
Keychain Dumper is specifically designed for extracting Keychain entries on iOS. However, Passionfruit canaccomplish the same along with other features. To install Passionfruit, first install Node.js by running brew installnode. Subsequently, run npm install -g passionfruit to install the tool globally. The tool exposes a web interfaceaccessible by navigating to localhost:31337 in a web browser. Keep in mind that Passionfruit is dependent onFrida and can sometimes crash; however, it is generally easy to reboot.
Grapefruit, a developmental version of Passionfruit, is purported to offer more stability, although it may lack somefeatures. Installation instructions can be found in the official GitHub repository.
SQLite Browser allows you to inspect SQLite databases. Simply Google for SQLite Browser and follow standardmacOS application installation procedures to get it up and running.
Finally, Realm Browser is another database inspection tool you can download from the Apple Store. It isparticularly useful for viewing and editing Realm database files on macOS.
Your first option for installing a vulnerable iOS app for testing purposes is via Xcode, Apple's IDE. Begin bydownloading or cloning the source code of the chosen vulnerable application. You must have Xcode installed onyour macOS system. After installing Xcode, navigate to the project folder and look for the .xcworkspace file toopen, as many modern iOS projects use dependency managers like CocoaPods, which necessitate this file.
To test the application on an iOS simulator, select the appropriate simulator version in Xcode that aligns with iOS16 or higher. Execute the Run command (Cmd + R), and the application will be compiled and installed on thechosen simulator. For testing purposes, this offers a quick and straightforward method but lacks the real-worldvariables present in physical devices.
To install the vulnerable app on a physical device running iOS 16 or higher, a valid provisioning profile isrequired. This necessitates enrollment in the Apple Developer Program at a cost of $99/year. In your Xcodeenvironment, go to Project Settings | Select your Target | Code Signing, and ensure the correct Code Signing

--- Page 328 ---
identity and Provisioning profile are selected. After setting these, execute the Run command to compile and installthe app on your connected iOS device.
For a more hands-on installation process, you can use the .app file method. Download the .ipa file and rename itto .zip. After unzipping, you will find a folder named Payload, inside of which is the .app file. You can transferthis .app file to your device's /Applications directory using scp or sftp tools.
Once the .app file is on the device, SSH into it. Gain root access by executing the su command and give theapplication's binary executable permissions. To finish the installation, run the uicache command as the mobile user.If the application does not appear, a device reboot may be necessary.
If you are using a jailbroken device, alternative methods like IPA Installer or AppSync can be employed. For IPAInstaller, upload the .ipa file to your device via sftp. Then use the IPA Installer Console with the commandipainstaller <YourVulnerableApp.ipa> to install the application.
For AppSync, it's essential first to install the utility from a Cydia repository. Once installed, double-click the .ipafile on your computer to add it to iTunes. Sync it to your device, which will effectively install the app. However,note that using AppSync is generally not recommended for non-jailbroken devices due to security concerns.
iGoat
iGoat serves as an invaluable resource for practitioners keen on mastering the nuances of iOS application security.Designed with pedagogical objectives, the platform offers a myriad of vulnerabilities, each reflecting real-worldattack scenarios, thus making it an ideal training ground. Moreover, each vulnerability is paired with a lesson and aquiz, reinforcing the theoretical principles that underlie these security flaws.
One of the fundamental areas that iGoat covers is Key Management. This includes challenges and lessons aroundHardcoded Encryption Keys, Key Storage on the Server Side, and Random Key Generation. For instance, theHardcoded Encryption Keys challenge allows you to identify and exploit an encryption key that's embedded withinthe app's code. This is particularly instructive because it mirrors a common but perilous practice in real-worldapplications. With hands-on experience, you will gain insight into why such practices are detrimental and how tofind them during an actual penetration test.
iGoat also explores various aspects of Social Engineering and URL Scheme Attacks. Here, the focus shifts fromthe code's security posture to the human element. Phishing attacks and manipulating custom URL schemes in iOScan lead to unauthorized access and data loss. These lessons illustrate how these attacks can be carried out andwhat preventive measures can be instituted. In URL Scheme Attacks, you can tamper with the app's custom URLschemes to either access restricted information or perform unauthorized actions, thereby learning how to secureyour own applications against such vulnerabilities.
Reverse Engineering is another significant area covered by iGoat. Within this category, there are challenges relatedto String Analysis, enabling you to examine how sensitive information might be exposed through strings in thecompiled code. Learning how to identify these strings and their locations can give you an edge in determiningpotential data leakages in real-world applications.
iGoat's robust curriculum extends into Data Protection, both at rest and in transit. Topics include Local DataStorage techniques such as SQLite and Plist Storage, and more secure methods like Keychain Usage andNSUserDefaults Storage. Through practical exercises, you can scrutinize how data is stored locally on the deviceand identify potential security risks associated with each method. In the realm of Data Protection in Transit,challenges related to Server Communication and Public Key Pinning provide an understanding of how data can becompromised during transmission and how to enforce strong SSL pinning mechanisms to prevent MitM attacks.
Lastly, but certainly not least, iGoat delves into various forms of injection flaws, such as SQL Injection and Cross-Site Scripting (XSS), and issues with Broken Cryptography. In the SQL Injection challenge, you can manipulateSQL queries to access or alter data, providing an in-depth understanding of how these attacks manifest and how todefend against them. Broken Cryptography challenges, on the other hand, showcase common cryptographicmistakes made during app development, such as using weak algorithms or improper key management, allowingyou to both recognize and remedy these issues.

--- Page 329 ---
The iGoat platform is accessible on GitHub, providing an open-source avenue for security education. As of version3.0, the platform encompasses a broad range of vulnerabilities, making it an essential tool for anyone seeking togain or refine their skills in iOS application security. By actively engaging with these exercises, you do not justlearn to identify vulnerabilities; you understand their root causes and, most importantly, how to prevent them inyour applications.
iGoat exercise side channel data leaks: Backgrounding
In iOS, when an application is moved to the background or is terminated, a snapshot of the application's currentview is saved in the form of a JPG file. This snapshot serves as a visual placeholder used by the system to providea fast user experience when switching between apps. However, the concern arises because this snapshot is savedon the device's local storage without any form of encryption or access control mechanisms.
To exploit this vulnerability, an attacker can locate the JPG snapshot by searching for it within the device's filesystem. In a practical example with the iGoat application, the find * | grep "Snapshot" command will search forfiles containing the word Snapshot. A result like the following might be encountered:
Found snapshot: Containers/Data/Application/E9EE64E5-62D4-4010-9A7B-63AAC086F971/Library/SplashBoard/Sna
To view the located snapshot, the open command can be used to open the file directly. The full command might be:
open Containers/Data/Application/E9EE64E5-62D4-4010-9A7B-63AAC086F971/Library/SplashBoard/Snapshots/scen
Cut and paste functionality: A double-edged sword
In iOS, the UIPasteboard is an enormously useful feature that allows users to copy and paste data within anapplication or between different applications. The UIPasteboard comes in two flavors:
System-wide General Pasteboard: This is accessible to all apps and is persistent across device restarts andapp uninstalls starting from iOS 10.
Custom or Named Pasteboards: These are intended for sharing data within the app itself or with anotherapp that has the same team ID. They are non-persistent by default and are deleted when the creating appquits.
An attacker can exploit the cut and paste buffer by accessing the UIPasteboard to retrieve sensitive data. Forinstance, if an app allows copying sensitive information like passwords, an attacker could read that data from theclipboard and use it maliciously.
To locate the sensitive data within the UIPasteboard, first copy some data from the target application, say iGoat.Next, explore the pasteboard directory within the iOS sandbox where clipboard data is stored. With appropriatepermissions, the data can be accessed and manipulated.
iGoat exercise reverse engineering: String analysis in iOS applications
String analysis is a common technique in reverse engineering, particularly for mobile applications, aimed atuncovering hidden secrets or sensitive data embedded within an application’s binary. In this realm, strings areoften one of the most immediate and informative elements to examine as they can contain passwords, API keys, orhints about the application's internal logic. For demonstration purposes, let us consider an exercise where we aretasked with extracting a secret piece of information embedded in a string located within an iOS app's .ipa file.
The .ipa file is essentially the application package file used for distribution on Apple's iOS operating system. Thefirst step towards reverse engineering involves decompiling this .ipa file. One of the popular tools for this isHopper, which allows for both static and dynamic analysis of application binaries. Using Hopper, you can open the.ipa file and navigate through its assembly language code, all while being able to search for specific strings ormethods.
Command example for Hopper:
$ Hopper -f /path/to/app.ipa

--- Page 330 ---
For this specific exercise, the application may contain a text field for user input. Submitting an empty text fieldwill trigger a certain function that we can inspect. This could be the method that contains the string with theembedded secret. With Hopper or another debugging tool, set breakpoints or logs at this function to track theexecution flow when the text field is submitted empty.
Once you have located the function triggered by the empty text field submission, search for the string Emptyentry!! or its equivalent within the function's assembly code. This string serves as a clue, potentially leading us tothe location where the secret information is stored or processed.
Command to search in Hopper:
Edit -> Find -> Find Text -> "Empty entry!!"
Unveiling the Embedded Secret pinpointed the location of the Empty entry!! string, the next logical step is toscrutinize the surrounding assembly or source code. Chances are that the secret string is embedded in closeproximity. Analyze variables, method calls, or any data manipulations adjacent to this string to uncover the secret.
Example in Hopper, if you find a line like this:
0x1000abcd:  leaq    0x1bf9(%rip), %rdi      ; "Empty entry!!"
The following or preceding lines could very well contain manipulations of the secret data.
Damn Vulnerable iOS App
Damn Vulnerable iOS App (DVIA) is an advanced training platform for iOS security, offering an assortment ofprogressively challenging vulnerabilities. Designed to equip security professionals with hands-on skills, DVIAgoes beyond basic issues like data leakage and session handling, delving into intricate topics like runtimemanipulations, method swizzling, and binary patching. The application is especially useful for those who arealready familiar with the basics and are looking to enhance their skills in detecting and mitigating advancedvulnerabilities.
One of the striking features of DVIA is its focus on Local Data Storage vulnerabilities. In a typical challenge, youmay encounter data that is inappropriately stored in plaintext within the application's sandbox. You can uncoverthis flaw by using file management tools like iFunBox or by SSH-ing into a jailbroken device to directly access theapplication's data container. Here, the vulnerable storage locations can be identified, and the sensitive data read,thereby exposing the flawed mechanism. Such hands-on exercises are crucial for understanding how data can beimproperly stored and how to write code that securely handles sensitive information.
Another advanced topic covered in DVIA is Runtime Manipulation. By utilizing dynamic binary instrumentationtools like Frida or Cycript, you can hook into running processes of the target application. This enables you tomanipulate or inspect method calls in real-time, altering the behavior of the application on-the-fly. For instance,challenges may involve bypassing license checks or changing in-app purchase statuses. This helps you understandthe level of intrusion that can be attained through runtime manipulation, providing you with insights on bothdetecting and preventing such vulnerabilities.
DVIA also delves into Anti Anti-Hooking/Debugging, a category that allows you to bypass mechanisms designedto prevent hooking or debugging. Essentially, some applications deploy techniques to detect whether they arebeing debugged or hooked by security tools, aiming to impede your testing process. DVIA teaches you how tocircumvent these mechanisms using techniques like altering flag values within the binary that indicate a debuggingenvironment, thereby enabling unrestricted testing.
Binary Protection is yet another crucial domain where DVIA excels. This section focuses on identifying andbypassing protections like code obfuscation, data encryption, and anti-tampering checks. By leveraging tools suchas IDA Pro or Ghidra for static analysis, you can uncover how these protection mechanisms are implemented andformulate strategies to bypass them. For example, you might find a function that verifies the integrity of theapplication's binary at runtime. Once located, you can modify this function to always return true, effectivelybypassing the anti-tampering checks.
Moreover, DVIA brings into focus issues related to Touch/Face ID Bypass and Phishing. The application offerschallenges where biometric authentication processes can be bypassed by manipulating function returns through

--- Page 331 ---
tools like Frida. Similarly, the Phishing exercises help you understand how an attacker can deceive users intorevealing sensitive information like usernames and passwords. These exercises expose the inherent risks associatedwith these technologies and guide you in implementing more robust security controls.
The application is readily available on GitHub, allowing easy access to its rich content. Written in Swift, DVIA'slatest version provides a broad landscape to explore, including Side Channel Data Leakage, IPC Issues, BrokenCryptography, Webview Issues, Network Layer Security, Application Patching, and Sensitive Information inMemory. By working through DVIA's gamut of challenges, you not only gain a thorough understanding of iOSvulnerabilities but also learn the methodologies to identify and rectify these security gaps, thereby contributing tothe creation of more secure iOS applications.
DVIA allows for auditing both through a simulator and an actual iOS device. For a realistic testing environmentand nuanced vulnerability assessment, it is strongly advised to use an actual device. To get your hands dirty,navigate to the 'Client-Side Injection' challenge from the navigational menu.
The initial challenge under the 'Client-Side Injection' heading involves invoking an alert box through a successfulinjection. It is noteworthy that DVIA uses a UIWebView for this particular functionality. To test the inputvalidation, one could insert a benign string like "Apple" and observe how DVIA processes it.
If DVIA does not properly sanitize this input, it becomes feasible to inject JavaScript code that will execute withinthe context of the UIWebView. To test this, you could input a string like <script>alert('Injected')</script>.Successful execution of this script would manifest as an alert box on the UIWebView, confirming the application'svulnerability to client-side injection. For more advanced exploits, JavaScript can be used to call native iOSfunctionalities, which often involves understanding URL schemes and their implementation.
Switching to another critical challenge, 'Jailbreak Detection' aims to assess how well the application can detect ajailbroken device. The crux of this challenge lies in manipulating DVIA into falsely reporting that a jailbrokendevice is, in fact, not jailbroken. To achieve this, SSH into the device and navigate to the folder where DVIA'sbinary is located. Run class-dump on DVIA to reveal its classes and methods.
The 'Jailbreak Detection' feature likely utilizes a method, possibly isJailbroken, in a class such asJailbreakDetectionVC, returning a BOOL (Boolean) value. Cycript can be used to overwrite this method's behaviordynamically. Attach to DVIA's running process using Cycript with the command cycript -p [PID], where [PID]is the process ID of DVIA. Once attached, overwrite the method with:
JailbreakDetectionVC.messages['isJailbroken'] = function() { return NO; };
After this change, navigate back to the DVIA interface and tap the relevant button to initiate the jailbreak test. Ifdone correctly, the alert will indicate that the device is not jailbroken, despite it being so. This proves thesuccessful manipulation of DVIA's jailbreak detection mechanism.
By working through these challenges in DVIA, you can develop a nuanced understanding of commonvulnerabilities in iOS applications. This serves as both a learning experience and a practical skill set invaluable forreal-world iOS application security assessments.
While these applications offer a broad range of challenges, it is important to consider the iOS version and devicemodel compatibility. For instance, some older vulnerabilities might not be applicable in the latest iOS versions dueto enhanced security measures implemented by Apple. On the flip side, newer versions of iOS may introducevulnerabilities or behaviors that have not been covered by these training applications yet. Therefore, it isrecommended to maintain an environment where you can switch between different iOS versions and hardwaresetups, either via simulators or through multiple physical devices.
However, be cautious when you extrapolate your findings from these deliberately vulnerable applications to real-world scenarios. While they are excellent for understanding the mechanics of vulnerabilities and sharpening yourexploitation skills, they are engineered to be flawed. Real-world applications may have multiple layers of defense,such as advanced intrusion detection systems, or may integrate third-party security libraries that make exploitationsignificantly more challenging. For instance, a real-world app might use third-party solutions like TrustKit for SSLpinning or employ advanced obfuscation techniques to hinder reverse engineering. Thus, your real-world approachwould require you to adapt and evolve your exploitation techniques to overcome these added complexities.

--- Page 332 ---
Inspecting insecure data storage with DVIAv2
Mobile applications often contain large amounts of user data and metadata to function smoothly. The mobilesecurity model aims to protect this data from unauthorized access, but vulnerabilities may exist, especially when adevice is rooted or jailbroken. For our experiments, we will use DVIAv2, an open-source iOS applicationdeveloped to study and understand various iOS security issues.
Plist Storage
DVIAv2 has a section called Local Data Storage where you can input data into different types of storagemechanisms. After injecting a string like "helloplist" into Plist storage, you can examine where it resides. To dothis, fire up Grapefruit, a mobile application security auditing tool, and navigate to your connected iOS device.Clicking into the DVIA-v2 application, navigate to the file explorer by clicking the finder icon. Inside, go toDocuments and open the userInfo.plist file. Here you should be able to locate the string helloplist, indicatinghow Plist is not a secure method for data storage.
UserDefaults
Another area within Grapefruit is the UserDefaults panel. After injecting data with the tag hellouserdefaults, youcan view it under the NsUserDefaults panel. This further emphasizes the insecure nature of UserDefaults when itcomes to sensitive data storage.
Keychain access
For keychain access, Grapefruit might have issues displaying values. As an alternative, launch Passionfruit andnavigate to Storage | Keychain. You should be able to see your injected values here. Keep in mind that Passionfruitcan be unstable and may require frequent restarts.
Core data
Within Passionfruit, navigate to Data | Library | Application Support and click the file named Model.sqlite. Thisshould open an SQL viewer. Locate the table ZUSER to find the string hellocoredata that you previously entered.This presents another insecure storage mechanism demonstrated by DVIAv2.
Webkit Caching
Inside Passionfruit, navigate to Data | Library | Caches. This directory contains folders that are integral to howWebKit caches data. Pay close attention to the com.highaltitudehacks.DVIAswiftv2 folder and the Cache.db fileinside. The tables prefixed with cf_url are of particular interest and show how insecure WebKit caching can be.
Realm storage
Moving on to Realm storage, within Passionfruit navigate to Documents to find the default.realm file. To inspectthis file, use Realm Browser, available from the Apple Store. The database may appear empty, but as you insertmore data into the app, the entry count increases, suggesting an insecure method of data storage.
Couchbase Lite
In Passionfruit, navigate to Data | Library | Application Support | CouchbaseLite | dvcouchbasedb.cblite2.Download the db.sqlite3 file and open it in a SQLite browser. Browse the revs database to locate your injectedstring hellocouchbase, along with other sensitive data, revealing its insecure nature.
YapDatabase
Finally, explore YapDatabase by navigating to Data | Library | Application Support within Passionfruit.Download the file named YapDatabase.sqlite and inspect it using a SQL browser on your machine. Although thedatabase does not readily display the values, you will find blobs in the database. Clicking on them reveals the texthelloyap, proving that YapDatabase is another insecure storage option.
Using DVIAv2 in conjunction with tools like Grapefruit and Passionfruit allows for in-depth exploration of variousinsecure data storage mechanisms in iOS applications. This hands-on investigation is integral for penetration

--- Page 333 ---
testers and red teamers to understand how to exploit and subsequently secure these vulnerabilities effectively.Always remember to approach real-world testing with the same rigor and attention to detail to ensure robustmobile application security. The rest of the vulnerabilities in DVIA are left as an exercise for the reader.
Using intentionally vulnerable iOS applications like iGoat and DVIA for penetration testing training offers an idealblend of theory and practical exposure. They are platforms where you can safely make mistakes, learn, andunderstand the underpinnings of mobile security vulnerabilities, all without the legal ramifications of hacking intounauthorized systems. However, they should be used as starting points in your journey, supplemented by testingagainst more complex, real-world applications and systems to truly refine and validate your skill set.
Conclusion
Through the journey of this chapter, you have armed yourself with the essential techniques and tools to decipher,challenge, and augment the security protocols of iOS applications. No longer are you just a passive participant inthe realm of mobile application security; you are now an active contributor, equipped with the requisite hands-onexperience to identify vulnerabilities and propose solutions. The knowledge you have gained is not merelytheoretical; it has been supplemented with practical exercises that simulate real-world security threats andchallenges. Whether it is exploiting side-channel data leaks, manipulating the cut-and-paste feature, or reverseengineering to reveal hidden strings, these exercises have granted you a deeper understanding and an advancedskill set.
As we wrap up, it is imperative to realize that the field of mobile application security is ever-evolving. Thetechniques and vulnerabilities discussed are subject to change as new iterations of iOS are released and as thecybersecurity landscape shifts. Therefore, continuous learning and adaptation are crucial. Nevertheless, with thefoundational skills and understanding you have acquired, you are well-prepared to contribute to making the iOSenvironment more secure and robust. Now that we have learned the inner workings of Android and iOS, we willtake a look at how to use your skills to advance your career.
The next chapter will guide you through how to start your career in mobile security or help you advance yourexisting career.
Points to remember
iOS Security architecture: Recognize the robust security features of iOS, including code signing,sandboxing, and hardware-based encryption, but also understand its vulnerabilities.
Hands-on tools: Be familiar with the tools necessary for iOS security testing, such as Xcode, iOS Simulator,Burp Suite, and the use of jailbroken devices.
Vulnerability exploration: Acknowledge the various types of vulnerabilities that can exist in iOS apps,including side-channel data leaks, insecure data storage, and issues with functionalities like cut and paste.
Practical exercises: Remember the importance of practical, hands-on exercises with vulnerable iOS appslike iGoat and Damn Vulnerable iOS App (DVIAv2) to understand real-world application security flaws.
Reverse engineering and analysis: Understand the value of reverse engineering and string analysis inidentifying and understanding the nature of vulnerabilities within iOS applications.
Mitigation strategies: Be aware of the strategies and best practices for mitigating identified vulnerabilitiesto strengthen the security posture of iOS apps.
Questions
1. What are the key security features of iOS, and how can they be compromised?
2. How does the use of tools like Xcode and Burp Suite contribute to the security testing of iOS applications?
3. What types of vulnerabilities are most common in iOS apps, and how can they impact the user and theorganization?

--- Page 334 ---
4. How do exercises with apps like iGoat and DVIAv2 help in understanding and mitigating iOS vulnerabilities?
5. In what ways can reverse engineering and string analysis aid in the detection and understanding of securityflaws in iOS applications?
6. What are some effective strategies for mitigating the vulnerabilities identified during the penetration testing ofiOS apps?

Navigating the complexities of mobile security

--- Page 337 ---
Mobile security has become a critical aspect of cybersecurity in our era of
ubiquitous mobile devices. It is a dynamic field, constantly evolving with
technological advances and new threats. It is more than just protecting
against malware or unauthorized access; it involves app security, network
security, and the physical security of the devices themselves. For example,
as mobile banking becomes increasingly popular, ensuring the security of
these apps is vital to protect users' financial data and maintain trust in
digital platforms.
As technology progresses, so do the tactics of cyber adversaries, presenting
multifaceted challenges like sophisticated phishing attacks and advanced
threats using mobile devices as gateways to broader networks. This
constantly changing threat landscape highlights the necessity for
professionals to have a solid foundation and the ability to adapt quickly.
Consider the journey of Sarah, a computer science graduate passionate
about cybersecurity. She starts by mastering programming languages and
understanding mobile platform architectures, coupled with practical tools
like Wireshark and Burp Suite. By setting up a mobile device lab,
participating in community forums, and attending industry conferences, she
gains real-world experience and eventually becomes a lead mobile security
expert.
Aspiring mobile security professionals face a challenging but rewarding
path. It offers a mix of technical challenges, continuous learning, and the
satisfaction of making a real impact. This chapter provides a roadmap for
those ready to embark on a career in this vital and exciting field.
The journey toward expertise
Venturing into mobile security is a journey of continuous learning and
adaptation, demanding a proactive mindset and a deep understanding of
evolving technologies to counter emerging threats. This field, both
rewarding and impactful, requires an in-depth exploration of the unique
complexities of various mobile platforms and their ecosystems. Android's
open-source nature offers customization but brings vulnerability risks,
while iOS's closed system is generally more secure but not impervious to

--- Page 338 ---
attacks. Each application, with its distinct codebase and functionality, adds
another layer to this intricate security landscape.
Grasping the subtleties of each platform and the myriad of applications is
crucial for anyone keen on making a significant impact in this dynamic
field. Aspiring professionals must delve into the architectures of Android,
iOS, and other systems, understanding their strengths and vulnerabilities.
They must also navigate the vast universe of apps, each a potential
goldmine for cybercriminals if not developed with stringent security
measures. This expansive and varied landscape offers endless opportunities
for growth, innovation, and contribution, making mobile security a
challenging yet fulfilling career path for those ready to continuously learn
and adapt.
Specialization
The road to mastering mobile security is paved with endless opportunities
to specialize. Whether one chooses to be a platform-specific expert, an app

The path to mastery in mobile security is not merely about recognizing

Laying the groundwork: a strong foundational base

--- Page 339 ---

Beyond the technical, a mobile security professional's arsenal must include

but preempted potential future ones.
One characteristic that invariably stands out in the mobile security realm is

While technical acumen is paramount, aspiring professionals should not

Ultimately, the path to mobile security expertise is not a destination but an

--- Page 340 ---
skills, mindset, and passion, it promises a rewarding and impactful career,

Key skillsets for a career in mobile security
Mobile security is not a monolithic entity; it is a mosaic of interconnected
domains, each demanding its unique set of skills. At its heart, however, are
certain foundational elements that every professional in the field should
master. These elements form the bedrock upon which specialized expertise
can be built, ensuring a comprehensive understanding of the challenges and
